Invariant graphs in Julia sets and decompositions of rational maps
Guizhen Cui, Yan Gao, Jinsong Zeng

Abstract
. In this paper, we prove that for any post-critically finite rational map f f on the Riemann sphere ℂ ¯ \overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}} , and for each sufficiently large integer n n , there exists a finite and connected graph G G in the Julia set of f f such that f n ​ ( G ) ⊂ G f^{n}(G)\subset G . This graph contains all post-critical points in the Julia set, while every component of ℂ ¯ ∖ G \overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus G contains at most one post-critical point in the Fatou set. The proof relies on the cluster-Sierpiński decomposition of post-critically finite rational maps.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 37F20; Secondary 37F10

1.
Introduction

Let
f
be a rational map on the Riemann sphere
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
with
\deg f\geq 2
. The Fatou set and Julia set of
f
are denoted by
F_{f}
and
J_{f}
, respectively. Their definitions and basic properties can be found in
[
37
]
. The set of post-critical points of
f
is defined by

P_{f}=\bigcup_{n>0}\{f^{n}(c):f^{\prime}(c)=0\}.

In particular, the map
f
is called
post-critically finite
, or simply
PCF
, if
\#P_{f}<\infty
. Generally, a
marked rational map

(f,P)
is a PCF rational map
f
with a finite set
P\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
P_{f}\subset P
and
f(P)\subset P
.

In complex dynamics, a fundamental problem is understanding the structure of Julia sets for rational maps. Significant progress has been made in this area for polynomials, largely since the Julia set of a polynomial is the boundary of its basin of infinity. However, for a general rational map, it is not possible to observe the entire Julia set from only a single Fatou domain. Therefore, we need to consider not only the boundary of each Fatou domain, but also the arrangement of distinct Fatou domains.

An effective approach to this problem is to construct a suitable invariant graph. In this paper, the term
graph
refers to a finite and connected graph in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. For PCF polynomials, the well-known Hubbard trees are invariant and completely characterize the dynamics of the polynomials
[
14
,
40
]
. Invariant graphs for Newton maps and critically fixed rational maps have been studied by several groups
[
15
,
16
,
30
,
31
,
41
,
46
,
6
,
23
]
.

The first breakthrough in the general situation was made by Cannon, Floyd, and Parry
[
5
]
and Bonk and Meyer
[
3
]
independently. They proved that

Theorem A

(
[
3
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Any marked rational map
(f,P)
with
J_{f}=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
admits an
f^{n}
-invariant Jordan curve passing through all points of
P
for each sufficiently large integer
n
.

The same conclusion was obtained for marked
Sierpiński rational maps
, i.e., rational maps with Sierpiński carpet Julia sets, by Meyer, Haïssinsky and the last two authors of this paper
[
20
]
. The following theorem is an enhanced version of
[
20
, Theorem 1.2]
.

Theorem B

(
[
20
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked Sierpiński rational map such that no points of
P
lie on the boundaries of Fatou domains. Then for each sufficiently large integer
n
, there exists an
f^{n}
-invariant Jordan curve passing through all points of
P
, such that its intersection with the closure of any Fatou domain is either empty or the union of two closed internal rays.

Recently, by extending the Bonk-Meyer method in
[
3
]
, the authors of this paper demonstrated that every PCF rational map
f
admits an
f^{n}
-invariant graph containing
P_{f}
for each sufficiently large integer
n
; see
[
7
, Theorem 1.1]
.

However, not all invariant graphs are sufficient to capture the full complexity of the Julia set. For example, for a PCF polynomial without bounded Fatou domains, the union of external rays landing at the post-critical points forms an invariant graph. Unlike the Hubbard tree, this graph provides limited information about the Julia set. Therefore, to better address these limitations, we aim to confine the graphs within the Julia set.

The main result of this paper is as follows.

Theorem 1.1

(Invariant graph in the Julia set)
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map. Then, for each sufficiently large integer
n
, there exists a graph
G\subset J_{f}
such that
f^{n}(G)\subset G
,
P\cap J_{f}\subset G
, and each component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus G
contains at most one point of
P
.

Remark 1.1
.

(1) Based on this theorem, we obtain an increasing sequence of invariant graphs
\{f^{-kn}(G)\}_{k\geq 1}
that approximate the Julia set from within.

(2) Theorem
1.1
is essentially known for PCF polynomials. Specifically, let
X
be the union of
P_{f}
and the branch points of the Hubbard tree
T
.
If
f
has no bounded Fatou domains, then
T
itself serves as the desired graph. Otherwise, for each bounded Fatou domain
U
that intersects
T
, if
\overline{U}\cap X\neq\emptyset
, we substitute
U\cap T
with the Jordan curve
\partial U
; if
\overline{U}\cap X=\emptyset
, we replace the segment
U\cap T
with a suitable choice of one of the two open arcs as the components of
\partial U\setminus T
. The resulting graph satisfies the conditions of Theorem
1.1
.

(3) The proof of Theorem
1.1
is entirely independent of our earlier work
[
7
, Theorem 1.1]
presented after Theorem B. Instead,
[
7
, Theorem 1.1]
can be directly derived from Theorem
1.1
.

Indeed, we may mark one point on the boundary of each Fatou domain intersecting
P_{f}
such that the union of these marked points, together with
P_{f}
, forms an
f
-invariant set, denoted by
P
. By applying Theorem
1.1
to
(f,P)
, we obtain an
f^{n}
-invariant graph
G^{\prime}\subset J_{f}
such that
P\cap J_{f}\subset G^{\prime}
, for each sufficiently large integer
n
. Thus, the union
G
of
G^{\prime}
and all internal rays landing at points of
P
is an
f^{n}
-invariant graph containing
P_{f}
.

There exist several key ingredients in proving Theorem
1.1
, as outlined in the schematic diagram in Figure
1
and summarized below.

Decompose rational

(Sections 4 and 5)

graphs of sub-systems
(Section 8)

f

Figure 1.
An outline of the procedure for proving Theorem
1.1
.

The first key ingredient refers to the invariant graphs on the boundaries of Fatou domains, serving as a semi-local counterpart to Theorem
1.1
.
Let
f
be a PCF rational map, and let
U
be a Fatou domain of
f
with
f(U)=U
.
If
f
is a polynomial, then

\partial U
admits an invariant graph by Remark
1.1
(2). It is natural to inquire whether this conclusion holds in general.

The answer to this question is negative, as illustrated by a counterexample in Theorem
2.3
. On a positive note, we can construct an invariant graph associated with
\partial U
within a larger invariant set, namely the
Fatou chain generated by
\boldsymbol{U}
, which is defined as

\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}},

where
E_{k}
is the component of
f^{-k}(\overline{U})
containing
U
.

Theorem 1.2

(Invariant graph associated with a Fatou domain)
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map, and let
U
be a fixed Fatou domain of
f
. Then there exists a graph
G\subset J_{f}
in the Fatou chain generated by
U
, such that
f(G)\subset G
and
G
is isotopic rel
P
to a graph
G_{0}\subset\partial U
, which satisfies that
G_{0}\cap P=\partial U\cap P
, and that two points of
P
lie in distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus G_{0}
provided that they belong to distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\partial U
.

Theorem
1.2
is proved in Section
2
, based on an explicit study of the dynamics on
\partial U
.

We aim to extend the invariant graph in Theorem
1.2
to a broader setting. Inspired by the Fatou chain generated by a single Fatou domain, we introduce the concept of general Fatou chains. The second key ingredient involves constructing invariant graphs within Fatou chains.

A
continuum
is a connected and compact subset of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
containing more than one point.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
f
be a rational map with
J_{f}\neq\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. A
level-
\boldsymbol{0}
Fatou chain
of
f
is defined as the closure of a Fatou domain of
f
. A
continuum

K\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is a
level-
\boldsymbol{1}
Fatou chain
of
f
if there exists a sequence of continua
\{E_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
, each of which is the union of finitely many level-
0
Fatou chains, such that

E_{k}\subset E_{k+1}\quad\text{and}\quad K=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}}.

Inductively, a continuum
K\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is a
level-
\boldsymbol{(n+1)}
Fatou chain
if there exists a sequence of continua
\{E_{k}\}
, each of which is the union of finitely many level-
n
Fatou chains, such that
E_{k}\subset E_{k+1}
and
K=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}}
.

A Fatou chain
K
is
maximal
if any Fatou chain intersecting
K
is contained in
K
.

By definition, a level-
n
Fatou chain is also a level-
m
Fatou chain if
n<m
, and the Fatou chain generated by a fixed Fatou domain is a level-
1
Fatou chain. Moreover, for Sierpiński rational maps, any maximal Fatou chain is simply the closure of a Fatou domain, while for polynomials or Newton maps, the entire sphere is a maximal Fatou chain.

Theorem 1.3

(Maximal Fatou chain)
.

Let
f
be a rational map with
J_{f}\neq\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Then each Fatou domain of
f
is contained within a maximal Fatou chain. Moreover, the image and components of the pre-image of a maximal Fatou chain under
f
are also maximal Fatou chains.

The proof of Theorem
1.3
is presented in Section
3
. In Section
6
, we revisit maximal Fatou chains, exploring their combinatorial and topological properties. With these foundations, the following result will be proved in Section
7
.

Theorem 1.4

(Invariant graphs on maximal Fatou chains)
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map with
J_{f}\neq\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, and let
K
be the intersection of
J_{f}
with an
f
-invariant maximal Fatou chain. Then there exists a graph
G\subset K
such that
f(G)\subset G
,
G\cap P=K\cap P
, and two points of
P
lie in distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus G
provided that they belong to distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K
.

Remark 1.2
.

If a PCF rational map has a maximal Fatou chain equal to
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, then Theorem
1.1
follows directly from Theorem
1.4
since every Fatou domain contains at most one marked point. From the perspective of Julia set configurations, such a map can be viewed as a generalization of polynomials and Newton maps, and is referred to as a
cluster rational map
.

The third key ingredient concerns the decomposition of a marked rational map. According to Theorem
1.4
, in order
to construct a global invariant graph, it is necessary to investigate the dynamics outside the union of marked maximal Fatou chains. This approach leads to a decomposition of marked rational maps by maximal Fatou chains, which we present in a generalized form.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
f
be a rational map, and let
{\mathcal{K}}
be a union of finitely many pairwise disjoint continua. We call
{\mathcal{K}}
a
stable set
of
f
if
f({\mathcal{K}})\subset{\mathcal{K}}
and each component of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}})
is either a component of
{\mathcal{K}}
or disjoint from
{\mathcal{K}}
.

According to Theorem
1.3
, the union of all periodic maximal Fatou chains is a specific example of a stable set. By definition, each component of a stable set is eventually periodic. Thus, the following result describes the dynamics on a stable set.

Theorem 1.5

(Renormalization)
.

Let
f
be a PCF rational map, and let
K\not=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a connected stable set of
f
. Then
f
is
renormalizable
on
K
, i.e., there exist a rational map
g
and a quasiconformal map
\phi
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
J_{g}=\phi(\partial K)
and
\phi\circ f=g\circ\phi
on
K
.
Moreover, the rational map
g
can be taken to be PCF and is unique up to conformal conjugacy. We call
g
the
renormalization
of
f
on
K
.

Next, we consider the dynamics outside a stable set.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map, and let
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\subset{\mathcal{V}}
be open sets with
\partial{\mathcal{V}}\subset J_{f}
such that each component of
\partial{\mathcal{V}}
contains more than one point. We say
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
is an
exact sub-system
of
(f,P)
if

(1)

{\mathcal{V}}
has finitely many components, each of which is finitely connected;

(2)

{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
is the union of some components of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{V}})
;

(3)

each component of
{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
is a continuum disjoint from
P
.

By definition, each component of
{\mathcal{V}}
contains a unique component of
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
.
Consequently, there exists a self-map
f_{\#}
on the collection of components of
{\mathcal{V}}
defined by
f_{\#}(V):=f(V_{1})
, where
V_{1}
is the unique component of
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
contained in
V
. Since
{\mathcal{V}}
has finitely many components, every component of
{\mathcal{V}}
is eventually
f_{\#}
-periodic.
Therefore, the dynamics of an exact sub-system is characterized by the following theorem.

Theorem 1.6

(Blow-up)
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map. Suppose that
f:V_{1}\to V
is an exact sub-system of
(f,P)
such that
V
is connected. Denote

V_{n}=(f|_{V_{1}})^{-n}(V)\quad\text{ and }\quad E=\bigcap_{n>0}\overline{V_{n}}.

Then there exist a marked rational map
(g,Q_{g})
, a continuum
K_{g}\supset J_{g}
with
g^{-1}(K_{g})=K_{g}
, and a continuous onto map
\pi:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that

(1)

components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K_{g}
are all Jordan domains with pairwise disjoint closures;

(2)

E=\pi(K_{g})
and
f\circ\pi=\pi\circ g
on
K_{g}
;

(3)

for any point
z\in\bigcap_{n>0}V_{n}
, the fiber
\pi^{-1}(z)
is a singleton;

(4)

for any component
B_{n}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus V_{n}
, the set
\pi^{-1}(B_{n})
is the closure of a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K_{g}
;

(5)

a point
x\in Q_{g}
if and only if either
\pi(x)\in P\cap V
, or
x
is the center in the Böttcher coordinate of a component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K_{g}
such that
\pi(\overline{D})\cap P\not=\emptyset
.

Moreover, the marked rational map
(g,Q_{g})
is unique up to conformal conjugacy.

The marked rational map
(g,Q_{g})
is called the
blow-up
of the exact sub-system
f:V_{1}\to V
of
(f,P)
. Generally, if
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
is an exact sub-system of
(f,P)
, and
V
is an
f_{\#}
-periodic component of
{\mathcal{V}}
with period
p
, then the blow-up of the exact sub-system
f^{p}:V_{p}\to V
of
(f^{p},P)
is regarded as a
blow-up
of
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
(associated with
V
). Here,
V_{p}
denotes the component of
(f|_{{\mathcal{V}}_{1}})^{-p}(V)
contained in
V
.

The primary result of the third key ingredient is the decomposition theorem below.

A connected open or closed set
E
is called
simple-type
(rel
P
) if there is a simply connected domain
D\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
E\subset D
and
\#(D\cap P)\leq 1
; or
annular-type
if
E
is not simple-type and there is an annulus
A\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P
such that
E\subset A
; or
complex-type
otherwise.

Theorem 1.7

(Cluster-Sierpiński decomposition)
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map with
J_{f}\neq\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Then there exists a stable set
{\mathcal{K}}\subset J_{f}
such that

(1)

for any periodic component
K
of
{\mathcal{K}}
with period
p
, the renormalization of
f^{p}
on
K
is a cluster rational map;

(2)

either
{\mathcal{V}}=\emptyset
or
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
is an exact sub-system of
(f,P)
, where
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
are the unions of complex-type components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}
and
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}})
, respectively.

Moreover, each blow-up of
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
is a marked Sierpiński rational map.

Remark 1.3
.

By Theorem
1.7
, the dynamics of
(f,P)
is essentially inherited by the sub-systems
f:{\mathcal{K}}\to{\mathcal{K}}
and
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
. In fact, the complement of
{\mathcal{K}}\sqcup{\mathcal{V}}
can be expressed as
{\mathcal{A}}\sqcup{\mathcal{S}}
, where
{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathcal{S}
denote the unions of all annular-type and simple-type components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}
, respectively.

The set
{\mathcal{A}}
has finitely many components, each of which is an annulus (see Theorem
4.2
). Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
be the union of all annular-type components of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{A}})
. It follows that
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
and
f:{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{A}}
forms an annular sub-system. The dynamics of an annular sub-system is straightforward and has been extensively studied in
[
9
]
.

Additionally, the dynamics of
f
associated with
\mathcal{S}
is trivial by the shrinking lemma (see Lemma
A.1
) since each component of
{\mathcal{S}}
contains at most one point of
P_{f}
.

Theorem
1.7
(1) and (2) and Theorem
1.5
are established in Section
4
. Theorem
1.6
is proved in Section
5
, which immediately implies the remaining part of Theorem
1.7
.

Now, according to Theorem
1.7
, any marked rational map with a non-empty Fatou set can be decomposed into several marked cluster or Sierpiński rational maps. The invariant graphs for marked cluster rational maps are established in Theorem
1.4
, while those for marked Sierpiński rational maps appear in Theorem B.

In the fourth and final key ingredient, we
will connect the invariant graphs associated with these sub-systems to derive a global invariant graph. This can be accomplished by identifying invariant arcs within the annular sub-system described in Remark
1.3
. The process is encapsulated in the following proposition, which is proved in Section
8
.

A graph is called
regulated
for a PCF rational map if its intersection with the closure of any Fatou domain of the map is either empty or the union of finitely many closed internal rays.

Proposition 1.1
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map with
J_{f}\not=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, and let
{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
represent the sets specified in Theorem
1.7
. Suppose each blow-up
(g,Q_{g})
of the exact sub-system
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
admits a
g
-invariant regulated graph containing
Q_{g}
. Then there exists an
f
-invariant graph
G\subset J_{f}
such that
P\cap J_{f}\subset G
and each component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus G
contains at most one point of
P
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

If
J_{f}=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, then Theorem
1.1
follows immediately from Theorem A.

Suppose that
J_{f}\neq\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Let
{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
represent the sets specified in Theorem
1.7
.
For every
n\geq 1
, the stable set
{\mathcal{K}}
induces a cluster-Sierpiński decomposition of
(f^{n},P)
. In particular,
f^{n}:{\mathcal{V}}_{n}\to{\mathcal{V}}
is an exact sub-system of
(f^{n},P)
, where
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
denotes the union of all complex-type components of
f^{-n}({\mathcal{V}})
.
We will compare the blow-ups of
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
and those of
f^{n}:{\mathcal{V}}_{n}\to{\mathcal{V}}
.

Let
V
be any
f_{\#}
-periodic component of
{\mathcal{V}}
with period
p
. Denote
(g,Q_{g})
as the blow-up of the exact sub-system
f^{p}:V_{p}\to V
, where
V_{p}
refers to the unique component of
{\mathcal{V}}_{p}
contained in
V
. Fix any integer
n\geq 1
. Let
m=m(n,V)
be the least common multiple of
n
and
p
. Then the period of
V
under
(f^{n})_{\#}
is
m/{n}
. Moreover, the blow-up of
f^{n}:{\mathcal{V}}_{n}\to{\mathcal{V}}
associated with
V
is the blow-up of the exact sub-system
f^{m}:V_{m}\to V
of
(f^{m},P)
, which is exactly
(g^{m/p},Q_{g})
.

Since
m(n,V)\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, it follows from Theorem B that each blow-up
(g^{m/p},Q_{g})
of
f^{n}:{\mathcal{V}}_{n}\to{\mathcal{V}}
admits a
g^{m/p}
-invariant and regulated graph passing through
Q_{g}
for each sufficiently large integer
n
. Therefore, by applying Proposition
1.1
to
(f^{n},P)
and
{\mathcal{K}}
, we obtain an
f^{n}
-invariant graph
G
with all the properties stated in Theorem
1.1
.
∎

The standard spherical metric is denoted by
\sigma(z)|dz|
with
\sigma(z)=1/(1+|z|^{2})
. Without emphasis, the distance, diameter, convergence, etc., are all considered under the spherical metric. Thus, we use simplified notations such as
{\rm dist}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
{\rm diam}(\cdot)
instead of
{\rm dist}_{\sigma}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
{\rm diam}_{\sigma}(\cdot)
.

Another metric used in this paper is the
orbifold metric

\omega
with respect to a PCF rational map. Its definition and properties are given in Appendix
A.1
.
Under this metric, we typically use the
homotopic length

L_{\omega}[\cdot]
and the
homotopic diameter

\text{H-diam}_{\omega}(\cdot)
instead of the usual length and diameter for a smooth curve and a connected set in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{f}
, respectively; see Appendix
A.1
for their definitions and detailed discussions.

In Appendix
A.2
, we introduce an isotopy lifting lemma under rational maps and a well-known convergence result for a sequence of isotopies obtained by lifting. Appendix
A.3
includes three topological results related to local connectivity.

1.1.
Related work

Theorem
1.7
is closely related to Theorem C in a recent work
[
17
]
by
Dudko, Hlushchanka and Schleicher. We first became aware of their work in 2022 from a slide
by Hlushchanka, by which time the main results of our paper had already been completed.

In our opinion, these two decomposition theorems are essentially the same, but with quite different formulations and approaches.
In
[
17
]
, the decomposition is by means of stable multicurves,
as done by Pilgrim in
[
39
]
; while our decomposition directly utilizes stable sets. Netherless, both
of the starting points are the maximal Fatou chains (called
maximal clusters
in
[
17
]
). Another
relevant work can be found in
[
11
]
.

Recently, several interesting results about PCF cluster maps were announced. For example, this type of map has a zero-entropy invariant graph containing
P_{f}
(see
[
17
, Theorem B]
), and the Ahlfors-regular conformal
dimension of its Julia set is equal to one (see
[
38
, Theorem A]
).

D. Thurston posed a question (see
[
42
, Question 1.19]
) regarding the identification of a preferred “best” spine of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{f}
for a hyperbolic PCF cluster rational map. In this case, the invariant graph obtained in Theorem
1.4
appears to
be a good candidate.

The existence of invariant graphs has also been studied beyond the rational case. A
Thurston map
is a PCF branched covering on the
2
-sphere. Bonk and Meyer
[
3
]
proved that any
expanding
Thurston map
f
admits an
f^{n}
-invariant Jordan curve passing through all post-critical points for each sufficiently large integer
n
. More broadly, a Thurston map is
Böttcher expanding
if it has a certain “expansion property” near its Julia set (see
[
2
]
). The dynamics of such maps is investigated in a series of works, including
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
18
,
19
]
. In particular, Floyd, Parry, and Pilgrim
[
19
]
showed that a suitable iterate of a Böttcher expanding Thurston map admits an isotopy-invariant graph containing all post-critical points.

Invariant graphs are extensively used in the study of the dynamics of PCF rational maps and Thurston maps. For example, Meyer
[
36
]
investigated the unmating of PCF rational maps with empty Fatou sets using invariant Peano curves. Hlushchanka and Meyer employ the invariant Jordan curves from Theorems A and B to calculate the growth of iterated monodromy groups for certain PCF rational maps. Additionally, based on Theorem A, Li established the thermodynamic formalism
[
25
,
26
]
and, in collaboration with Zheng, the prime orbit theorems
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
for expanding Thurston maps.

1.2.
Future directions

First, a natural question arises regarding whether the iterate is strictly necessary in Theorem
1.1
. To address this question, we propose the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.1
.

For any marked rational map
(f,P)
, Theorem
1.1
holds with
n=1
. In other words, there exists an
f
-invariant graph
G\subset J_{f}
such that
P\cap J_{f}\subset G
and each component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus G
contains at most one point of
P
.

According to Proposition
1.1
, this conjecture is true if we can confirm that any marked rational map
(g,Q)
with its Julia set equal to either the sphere or the Sierpiński carpet admits a
g
-invariant and regulated graph containing
Q
.

Every PCF rational map with the Julia set equal to
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is an expanding Thurston map. In addition, each PCF Sierpiński rational map
f
can descend to an expanding Thurston map
F
by collapsing the closure of each Fatou domain to a point, and any graph in the
F
-plane can be lifted to a regulated graph for
f
; see
[
20
, Sections 5 and 6]
.
Therefore, Conjecture
1.1
is implicated by the following conjecture, which appeared in
[
3
, Problem 2]
.

Conjecture 1.2
.

For any marked expanding Thurston map
(F,Q)
, there exists an
F
-invariant graph containing
Q
.

Another direction concerns the renormalizability of a rational map on stable sets.
A classical result by McMullen asserts that any rational map is renormalizable on each of its fixed Julia components
[
33
, Theorem 3.4]
.
It is worth noting that every fixed Julia component is a specific connected stable set. On the other hand, Theorem
1.5
shows that if the rational map is PCF, then it is renormalizable on any connected stable set, due to the expansion property near the Julia set.

Question 1.1
.

Is every rational map renormalizable on any connected stable set or on any fixed maximal Fatou chain of the map?

The next direction examines the invariant graphs derived from Theorem
1.1
from the perspective of entropy.
According to W. Thurston, the
core entropy
of a polynomial is the topological entropy on its Hubbard tree, which is a very useful tool for studying the bifurcation locus of polynomials
[
21
,
43
,
44
,
45
]
. However, there exists currently no definition for the core entropy of a rational map.

Consider a marked rational map
(f,P_{f})
, and let
\mathscr{G}
denote the collection of all graphs obtained in Theorem
1.1
. For polynomials, the topological entropy of
f
on the graphs in
\mathscr{G}
remains constant, which equals the maximum of the core entropy of
f
and
\log d_{U}/p_{U}
for all periodic Fatou domains
U
, where
p_{U}
denotes the period of
U
and
d_{U}
denotes the degree of
f^{p_{U}}:U\to U
.
Based on this observation, a potential candidate for the core entropy of
f
is given by

h(f)=\inf_{G\in\mathscr{G}}\{h_{top}(f^{n}|_{G})/n:f^{n}(G)\subset G,n\geq 1\},

where
h_{top}(f^{n}|_{G})
denotes the topological entropy of
f^{n}:G\to G
. Indeed, a motivation for us to construct invariant graphs within the Julia set is to define the core entropy of a rational map.

Additionally, when
f
is a polynomial, the graphs in
\mathscr{G}
are isotopic rel
P_{f}
under some natural restrictions. However, in the general case, the elements of
\mathscr{G}
are far from unique up to isotopy. Therefore, it is important to seek invariant graphs with canonical conditions. From the perspective of entropy, we may ask

Question 1.2
.

Is there a
(
unique
)

f^{n}
-invariant graph
G\in\mathscr{G}
such that
h(f)=h_{top}(f^{n}|_{G})/n
?

The final direction is to generalize Theorem
1.1
to the non-rational case, specifically to Böttcher expanding Thurston maps as mentioned in Section
1.1
.
These maps also have Julia and Fatou sets and share several similarities with PCF rational maps. Hence, it is plausible to expect that Theorem
1.1
applies to Böttcher expanding Thurston maps as well.

Question 1.3
.

Do
(
any of
)
the theorems listed in the Introduction still hold for Böttcher expanding Thurston maps after appropriate revisions?

The authors are grateful for insightful discussions with Zhiqiang Li, Xiaoguang Wang, Yunping Jiang, Dylan Thurston, and Luxian Yang. The first author is supported by the National Key R&D Program of China (Grant no. 2021YFA1003203)
and the NSFC (Grant nos. 12131016 and 12071303).
The second author is supported by the NSFC (Grant no. 12322104) and the NSFGD (Grant no. 2023A1515010058).
The third author is supported by the NSFC (Grant no. 12271115).

2.
Invariant graphs associated with fixed Fatou domains

In this section, we study the dynamics of a rational map
f
on the boundary of a fixed Fatou domain
U
of
f
. We begin by examining the mapping behavior of
f
on
\partial U
. Next, we construct an invariant continuum on
\partial U
with nice topological properties, called the
circle-tree
. Finally, we present the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

2.1.
Circle-trees

Let
U\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a simply connected domain such that
T_{0}:=\partial U
is a locally connected continuum. The following lemma is classical (see
[
14
, Chapter 2]
). In this paper, a
circle
means a Jordan curve, and a
disk
means a Jordan domain in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. An
arc
is a continuous injective map from
[0,1]
into
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, and its restriction to
(0,1)
is called an
open arc
.

Lemma 2.1
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

Both
T_{0}
and
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus U
are arcwise connected;

(2)

All components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U}
are disks, whose diameters converge to zero;

(3)

Each circle
C\subset T_{0}
is the boundary of a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
C\subset T_{0}
be a circle. If
E\subset T_{0}
is a continuum, then
C\cap E
is connected. If
C^{\prime}\neq C
is also a circle in
T_{0}
, then
\#(C\cap C^{\prime})\leq 1
.

Proof.

Suppose, to the contrary, that
C\cap E
is disconnected. Then
C\setminus E
has at least two components. Let
x
and
y
be two points contained in two distinct components of
C\setminus E
, respectively. Let
D
be the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus C
disjoint from
U
. Then there exist open arcs
\alpha\subset U
and
\beta\subset D
, both joining the points
x
and
y
. Now,
\alpha\cup\beta\cup\{x,y\}
is a Jordan curve disjoint from
E
, and both of its complementary components intersect
E
. This contradicts the connectivity of
E
.

Suppose
C^{\prime}\neq C
is also a circle in
T_{0}
. Then
I=C\cap C^{\prime}
is connected by the above discussion. If
I
contains at least two points, then it contains an open arc
\gamma
. This implies that each point in
\gamma
is an exterior point of
U
, which contradicts the fact that
\gamma\subset C\subset\partial U
.
∎

Motivated by the above results, we consider circles in
T_{0}
as entire entities when discussing subsets of
T_{0}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A continuum
T\subset T_{0}
is called a
circle-tree
of
T_{0}
if, for any circle
C\subset T_{0}
, either
C\subset T
or
\#(C\cap T)\leq 1
.

Let
T
be a circle-tree of
T_{0}
. A point
x\in T
is a
cut point
of
T
if
T\setminus\{x\}
is disconnected. A circle
C\subset T
is an
end circle
of
T
if
C
contains at most one cut point of
T
. A point
x\in T
is an
endpoint
of
T
if it is neither contained in a circle in
T
nor a cut point. By an
end
, we mean an endpoint or an end circle. We call
T
a
finite circle-tree
if
T
has finitely many ends.

In order to study circle-trees and their topology, one useful tool is the geodesic lamination introduced by W. Thurston. Let
\mathbb{D}
denote the unit disk. Then there exists a conformal map
\phi:\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}\setminus\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}}\to U
, which can be extended continuously to the boundary. For each point
x\in T_{0}
, denote by
H_{x}
the convex hull within
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}}
of
\phi^{-1}(x)
under the Poincarè metric on
\mathbb{D}
. The basic observation of lamination theory is

H_{x}\cap H_{y}=\emptyset\quad\text{ if }x\neq y.

Note that
\partial H_{x}\cap\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
consists of geodesics if it is non-empty. The
lamination

{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
induced by
U
is defined as the union of all such geodesics, which are called
leaves
. Then
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
is closed in
\mathbb{D}
, and the closure of a component of
\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}\setminus{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
is a
gap
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Assume that
U
is not a disk. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

For each gap
A
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
,
\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
is either a point or a circle. Conversely, for any circle
C\subset T_{0}
, there exists a unique gap
A
such that
\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})=C
. Moreover,
C
is an end circle of
T_{0}
if and only if
A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
is connected.

(2)

A point
x\in T_{0}
is an endpoint if and only if
\#\phi^{-1}(x)=1
, and there exists a sequence of leaves
\{L_{n}\}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
converging to
\phi^{-1}(x)
, such that
L_{n}
separates
L_{n-1}
from
L_{n+1}
.

(3)

Let
x\in T_{0}
be a point, and let
I_{0}
be a component of
\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}\setminus\phi^{-1}(x)
. Then either
\phi(\overline{I_{0}})
is an end circle, or
\phi(I_{0})
contains an end.

(4)

Let
C\subset T_{0}
be a circle, and let
I_{0}
be a component of
\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}\setminus\phi^{-1}(C)
. Then either
\phi(\overline{I_{0}})
is an end circle, or
\phi(I_{0})
contains an end.

t_{0}
A

Figure 2.
\phi(\partial\mathbb{D}\cap A)
is an end circle and
\phi(t_{0})
is an endpoint.

Proof.

(1) Note that
\partial A
is a Jordan curve. Define a map
\phi_{A}:\partial A\to T_{0}
by
\phi_{A}=\phi
on
\partial A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
and
\phi_{A}(L)=\phi(L\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
for any leaf
L\subset\partial A
. Then
\phi_{A}
is continuous, and
\phi_{A}(\partial A)=\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
. Thus,
\phi_{A}(\partial A)\subset T_{0}
is either a point or a closed curve. In the latter case, the curve is not self-intersecting since
\phi_{A}^{-1}(x)
is connected for any
x\in\phi_{A}(\partial A)
. Therefore, it is a circle in
T_{0}
.

Conversely, let
C\subset T_{0}
be a circle. For any point
x\in C
,
C\setminus\{x\}
is connected. Thus,
\phi^{-1}(C\setminus\{x\})
is contained in a component
A_{x}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}}\setminus H_{x}
, and
C\subset\phi(\overline{A_{x}}\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
. Let
A=\bigcap_{x\in C}\overline{A_{x}}
. Then
A
is a gap, and
C\subset\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
. From the discussion in the previous paragraph,
\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
is either a point or a circle. Thus, we have
C=\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
.

If
A^{\prime}\neq A
is another gap, then there exists a leaf
L\subset\partial A
that separates the interior of
A
from
A^{\prime}
. Thus,
\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})\cap\phi(A^{\prime}\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
contains at most one point, and then
\phi(A^{\prime}\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})\not=C
.

If
A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
is connected, then
\phi
is injective in the interior of
A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
, whose image contains no cut points, and
\phi
maps the two endpoints of
A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
to a cut point. Thus,
C
is an end circle.
Conversely, if
C
is an end circle, let
x\in C
be the unique cut point. Then
A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}=\overline{\phi^{-1}(C\setminus\{x\})}
is connected since
\phi^{-1}(y)
is a point for
y\in C\setminus\{x\}
.

(2) Denote
x_{n}=\phi(L_{n}\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
. Let
B_{n}
be the component of
T_{0}\setminus\{x_{n}\}
containing the point
x
. Then
B_{n+1}\subset B_{n}
, and the diameter of
B_{n}
tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
. Thus,
x
is an endpoint.

Conversely, if
x\in T_{0}
is an endpoint, then
\phi^{-1}(x)
consists of a single point
t\in\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
, and there exist no leaves landing on
t
. For each leaf
L
, denote by
|L|_{t}
the length of the component of
\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}\setminus L
containing the point
t
. Assume, by contradiction, that
\inf\{|L|_{t}\}>0
. Then there exists a leaf
L_{0}
such that
|L_{0}|_{t}=\inf\{|L|_{t}\}
since
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
is closed. Let
D_{0}
be the component of
\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}\setminus L_{0}
whose boundary contains the point
t
. Then there exist no leaves in
D_{0}
separating
L_{0}
from the point
t
. Thus, there exists a gap
A
containing the point
t
and the leaf
L_{0}
. By statement (1),
\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
is either a single point or a circle. Since
x\in\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
is an endpoint, we obtain
x=\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
, which contradicts the condition that
\phi^{-1}(x)
is a single point.

(3) By statement (1), the two endpoints of
I_{0}
are connected by a leaf in
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
. Denote by
{\mathcal{I}}
the collection of all open arcs
I\subset I_{0}
with
I\neq I_{0}
such that the two endpoints of
I
are connected by a leaf in
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
. Then any two arcs in
{\mathcal{I}}
are either disjoint or nested since any two distinct leaves are disjoint.

If
{\mathcal{I}}
is empty, then
\phi(\overline{I_{0}})
is an end circle by statement (1). If
|I|>|I_{0}|/2
for all
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
, then there exists a unique arc
I^{*}\in{\mathcal{I}}
such that
I^{*}\subset I
for all
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
. This implies that
\phi(\overline{I^{*}})
is an end circle. Otherwise, there exists an arc
I_{1}\in{\mathcal{I}}
such that
|I_{1}|\leq|I_{0}|/2
.

By iterating this process, we have to either stop at some step, yielding an end circle, or obtain an infinite sequence of arcs
\{I_{n}\}
such that
I_{n+1}\subset I_{n}
and
|I_{n+1}|\leq|I_{n}|/2
. By the definition of lamination, at most two leaves share a common endpoint. Thus,
t=\bigcap I_{n}
is a single point. By statement (2),
\phi(t)
is an endpoint.

(4) The proof is similar to that of statement (3).
∎

The following result is a direct consequence of Lemma
2.3
(3) and (4).

Corollary 2.1
.

Let
x\in T_{0}
be a point, and let
B
be a component of
T_{0}\setminus\{x\}
. Then either
\overline{B}
is an end circle, or
B
contains an end of
T_{0}
. Let
C\subset T_{0}
be a circle, and let
B
be a component of
T_{0}\setminus C
. Then
\overline{B}\cap C
is a singleton, and either
\overline{B}
is an end circle or
B
contains an end of
T_{0}
.

A circle-tree can be characterized by the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

A continuum
T\subset T_{0}
is a circle-tree of
T_{0}
if and only if each component of
\partial H_{T}\setminus\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
is a leaf in
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
, where
H_{T}
is the convex hull of
\phi^{-1}(T)
within
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}}
.

Proof.

For any circle
C\subset T_{0}
, there exists a unique gap
A
such that
\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})=C
by Lemma
2.3
(1). Since each component of
\partial H_{T}\setminus\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
is a leaf, either
A
is contained in
H_{T}
, or
A\cap H_{T}=\emptyset
, or
A\cap H_{T}
is a leaf. Thus, either
C\subset T
or
\#(T\cap C)\leq 1
. Therefore,
T
is a circle-tree of
T_{0}
.

Conversely, assume that
T
is a circle-tree of
T_{0}
. Let
I=(s,t)
be a component of
\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}\setminus\phi^{-1}(T)
. Denote
\phi(s)=x
and
\phi(t)=y
. Then
x,y\in T
.

If
x\not=y
, then
H_{x}\cap H_{y}=\emptyset
. Note that there exist no leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
in
\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}\setminus(H_{x}\cup H_{y})
separating
H_{x}
from
H_{y}
, since such a leaf would have an endpoint in
I
, which contradicts the connectivity of
T
. Thus, there exists a gap
A
such that
s,t\in A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
. By Lemma
2.3
(1),
\phi(A\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
is a circle in
T_{0}
, which contains the points
x,y\in T
. Thus, it is contained in
T
since
T
is a circle-tree, which implies that
A\subset H_{T}
. Hence,
I
is a component of
\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}\setminus A
. This implies that
s,t
are connected by a leaf in
\partial A
, and hence
x=y
, a contradiction.

Since
x=y
, either there exists a leaf joining the points
s
and
t
, or
H_{x}\cap I\neq\emptyset
. The latter cannot happen since
I\cap H_{T}=\emptyset
. Thus,
s
and
t
are connected by a leaf in
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
.
∎

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
T
be a circle-tree of
T_{0}
. Then
T
is locally connected, and there exists a simply connected domain
V\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
\partial V=T
.

Proof.

Note that
\partial H_{T}
is a Jordan curve. By Lemma
2.4
, each component of
\partial H_{T}\setminus\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
is a leaf. Define a map
\phi_{T}:\partial H_{T}\to T_{0}
by
\phi_{T}=\phi
on
\partial H_{T}\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
and
\phi_{T}(L)=\phi(L\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})
for any leaf
L\subset\partial H_{T}
. Then
\phi_{T}
is continuous, and
\phi_{T}(\partial H_{T})=T
. Thus,
T
is locally connected.

Let
V
be the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus T
containing
U
. Then
V
is a simply connected domain, and
\partial V\subset T
. On the other hand,
T\subset\overline{U}\subset\overline{V}
. Thus,
T\subset\partial V
. Hence, we have
\partial V=T
.
∎

The following result provides a basic tool for constructing circle-trees.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
x,y\in T_{0}
be two distinct points. Then there exists a unique circle-tree
T[x,y]
of
T_{0}
such that any circle-tree of
T_{0}
containing
x
and
y
contains
T[x,y]
. Moreover, each end of
T[x,y]
intersects
\{x,y\}
.

We call
T[x,y]
the
circle-tree spanned by
\boldsymbol{\{x,y\}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1
(1), there exists an arc
\gamma:[0,1]\to T_{0}
with
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma(1)=y
. Let
T_{1}
be the union of
\gamma
and all circles
C\subset T_{0}
with
\#(C\cap\gamma)\geq 2
. By Lemma
2.1
(2),
T_{1}
is a continuum.

We will show that
T_{1}
is a circle-tree. By definition, it suffices to prove that
for any circle
C\subset T_{0}
with
\#(C\cap T_{1})\geq 2
, it holds that
\#(C\cap\gamma)\geq 2
.

Suppose, to the contrary, that
\#(C\cap\gamma)\leq 1
. Let
x_{1},x_{2}\in C\cap T_{1}
be two distinct points, and let
\alpha
be an arbitrary component of
C\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
.

If
C\cap\gamma=\emptyset
, then there exist two distinct circles

C_{1},C_{2}\subset T_{1}
such that
x_{1}=C\cap C_{1}
and
x_{2}=C\cap C_{2}
. By the definition of
T_{1}
, there exists an arc
\gamma_{0}\subset\gamma
such that
y_{1}:=\gamma_{0}(0)\in C_{1},y_{2}:=\gamma_{0}(1)\in C_{2}
, and
\gamma_{0}(0,1)
is disjoint from
C_{1}\cup C_{2}
. For
i=1,2
, let
\beta_{i}
be a component of
C_{i}\setminus\{x_{i},y_{i}\}
such that
\beta_{1}\cap\beta_{2}=\emptyset
. Then
\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
, and
\gamma_{0}
are pairwise disjoint. It follows that

\alpha\cup\beta_{1}\cup\beta_{2}\cup\gamma_{0}\cup\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\}

is a circle in
T_{0}
, a contradiction to Lemma
2.1
(3).

If
\#(C\cap\gamma)=1
, we may assume
x_{1}
to be this intersection point, and there exists a circle
C_{2}\subset T_{1}
with
x_{2}=C\cap C_{2}
. A similar argument as above will also lead to a contradiction to Lemma
2.1
(3). Now, we have proved that
T_{1}
is a circle-tree.

Let
T_{2}
be a circle-tree containing the points
x
and
y
. Then there exists an arc
\gamma^{\prime}\subset T_{2}
joining
x
and
y
. For any component
\gamma_{1}
of
\gamma\setminus\gamma^{\prime}
, denote by
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
the sub-arc of
\gamma^{\prime}
with the same endpoints as those of
\gamma_{1}
. Thus,
\gamma_{1}\cup\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
is a circle in
T_{0}
. Since
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}\subset T_{2}
, it follows that
\gamma_{1}\cup\gamma_{1}^{\prime}\subset T_{2}
, and hence
\gamma\subset T_{2}
. By the definition of
T_{1}
, we have
T_{1}\subset T_{2}
. This implies the uniqueness of
T_{1}
.

By definition, any point of
T_{1}
belongs to either
\gamma
or a circle in
T_{0}
. Thus, an endpoint of
T_{1}
must be
x
or
y
.
If
C
is an end circle of
T_{1}
disjoint from
\{x,y\}
, then
T_{1}^{\prime}:=(T_{1}\setminus C)\cup\{z\}\subset T_{1}
is a circle-tree containing
x
and
y
, where
z
is the unique cut point of
T_{1}
on
C
. The uniqueness implies
T_{1}^{\prime}=T_{1}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be circle-trees of
T_{0}
such that
T_{1}\cap T_{2}\neq\emptyset
.

(1)

T_{1}\cap T_{2}
is either a singleton or a circle-tree of
T_{0}
.

(2)

T_{1}\cup T_{2}
is a circle-tree of
T_{0}
, and each end of
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
is an end of
T_{1}
or
T_{2}
.

Proof.

(1) For any two distinct points
x,y\in T_{1}\cap T_{2}
,
T[x,y]\subset T_{1}\cap T_{2}
by Lemma
2.5
. Thus,
T_{1}\cap T_{2}
is a continuum. For any circle
C\subset T_{0}
with
\#(C\cap T_{1}\cap T_{2})\geq 2
, we have
\#(C\cap T_{1})\geq 2
and
\#(C\cap T_{2})\geq 2
. Thus,
C\subset T_{1}\cap T_{2}
. Therefore,
T_{1}\cap T_{2}
is a circle-tree of
T_{0}
.

(2) By Lemma
2.4
, each component of
\partial H_{T_{1}}\setminus\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
and
\partial H_{T_{2}}\setminus\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
is a leaf in
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
. Since any two distinct leaves are disjoint in
\mathbb{D}
, each component of
\partial H_{T_{1}\cup T_{2}}\setminus\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
is a leaf in
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
. Thus,
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
is a circle-tree of
T_{0}
.

Let
x\in T_{1}\cup T_{2}
be a point disjoint from any circle in
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
. Assume
x\in T_{1}
. If
x
is a cut point of
T_{1}
, then there exists a Jordan curve in
U\cup\{x\}
that separates
T_{1}\setminus\{x\}
. Thus,
x
is a cut point of
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
. Therefore, if
x
is an endpoint of
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
, then it is an endpoint of
T_{1}
or
T_{2}
.

Let
C\subset T_{1}\cup T_{2}
be an end circle. Then either
C\subset T_{1}
or
C\subset T_{2}
. Assume
C\subset T_{1}
. If
C
contains two distinct cut points
x
and
y
of
T_{1}
, then
x
and
y
are also cut points of
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
. This is a contradiction. Thus,
C
is an end circle of
T_{1}
.
∎

For any finite set
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\subset T_{0}
with
n\geq 2
, denote

T[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]=T[x_{1},x_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup T[x_{1},x_{n}].

Furthermore, let
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},C_{1},\ldots,C_{m}\}
be a collection of points
x_{i}
and circles
C_{j}
in
T_{0}
. Pick two distinct points
y_{j},z_{j}\in C_{j}
for each circle
C_{j}
. Denote

T[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},C_{1},\ldots,C_{m}]=T[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m},z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}].

By Lemmas
2.5
and
2.6
, it is a finite circle-tree and also the minimal circle-tree of
T_{0}
containing
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},C_{1},\ldots,C_{m}
.
We call it the
circle-tree spanned by
\boldsymbol{\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},C_{1},\ldots,C_{m}\}}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
T
be a finite circle-tree of
T_{0}
, and let
T_{1}
be the circle-tree spanned by the ends of
T
. Then
T_{1}=T
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.5
,
T_{1}\subset T
. Assume that
x\in T\setminus T_{1}
is a point disjoint from all circles in
T
. Since
x
is not an endpoint of
T
, there exists a component
T^{\prime}
of
T\setminus\{x\}
disjoint from
T_{1}
. By Corollary
2.1
,
\overline{T^{\prime}}
contains an end of
T
, a contradiction.

Assume that
C\subset T
is a circle such that
C\cap T_{1}
contains at most one point. Then
C
is not an end circle of
T
. Thus,

T\setminus C
has a component
T^{\prime}
disjoint from
T_{1}
. By Corollary
2.1
,
\overline{T^{\prime}}
contains an end of
T
, also a contradiction.
∎

Let
T
be a finite circle-tree of
T_{0}
. By Corollary
2.2
, there exist a component
V
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus T
and a conformal map
\psi:\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}\setminus\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}}\to V
, which can be extended continuously to the boundary such that
\psi(\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$})=\partial V=T
. For each point
x\in T
, denote

\mu_{T}(x)=\#\psi^{-1}(x).

A point
x\in T
is called either a
cut point
of
T
if
\mu_{T}(x)\geq 2
, or a
branched point
of
T
if
\mu_{T}(x)\geq 3
, or a
locally branched point
of
T
if, for any sufficiently small neighborhood
W
of
x
,
(T\cap W)\setminus\{x\}
has at least three components. For any circle
C\subset T
, denote

{\mu}_{T}(C)=\#\{y\in C:\mu_{T}(y)\geq 2\}.

A circle
C\subset T
is called a
cut circle
of
T
if
\mu_{T}(C)\geq 2
, or a
branched circle
of
T
if
\mu_{T}(C)\geq 3
.

When
x\in T
is not contained in any circle in
T
, then
x
is a branched point if and only if it is a locally branched point. When
x\in T
is contained in a circle in
T
, then
x
is a locally branched point if and only if it is a cut point of
T
. If a circle
C\subset T
contains no branched points of
T
, then
{\mu}_{T}(C)
is the number of components of
T\setminus C
. In general,
{\mu}_{T}(C)
is the number of components of
\overline{T\setminus C}
.
Refer to Figure
3
for an example of finite circle-trees, where
p_{1}
is an endpoint,
p_{2}
is a cut point, and
p_{3}
is a branched point;
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are end circles,
C_{3}
and
C_{4}
are cut circles, and
C_{5}
is a branched circle.

p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{3}
p_{4}
C_{3}
C_{4}
C_{1}
C_{2}
C_{5}

Figure 3.
Classification of points and circles in a circle-tree

Note that any circle-tree
T\subset T_{0}
has at least one end by Corollary
2.1
. If
T
has only one end, then it is a circle.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
T
be a finite circle-tree of
T_{0}
with
n\geq 2
ends. Then
T
has exactly
k
branched points
\{x_{i}\}
and
l
branched circles
\{C_{j}\}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\mu_{T}(x_{i})-2)+\sum_{j=1}^{l}(\mu_{T}(C_{j})-2)=n-2.

Proof.

If
n=2
, the circle-tree
T
has neither branched points nor branched circles. In fact, if
z\in T
is a branched point, then
T
has at least three ends by Corollary
2.1
, a contradiction. Similarly, we obtain that
T
has no branched circles.

Assume, by induction, that the lemma holds for an integer
n\geq 2
. Let
T
be a circle-tree of
T_{0}
with
n+1
ends
X_{0},\ldots,X_{n}
. Denote
T^{\prime}=T[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
.

If
X_{0}\cap T^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, then
X_{0}
is an end circle, and
T^{\prime}
intersects
X_{0}
at a single point
y
.

If
X_{0}\cap T^{\prime}=\emptyset
, then there exists an arc
\gamma:[0,1]\to T
such that
\gamma(0)\in X_{0}
,
y=\gamma(1)\in T^{\prime}
, and
\gamma(t)\not\in T^{\prime}
for
t\in[0,1)
. We claim that
T[X_{0},y]\cap T^{\prime}=\{y\}
.

By the definition of
T[X_{0},y]
in the proof of Lemma
2.5
, it suffices to verify that for any circle
C\subset T_{0}
with
\#(C\cap\gamma)\geq 2
, either
C\cap T^{\prime}=\emptyset
or
C\cap T^{\prime}=\{y\}
. Since
\gamma[0,1)
lies in a component of
T\setminus\{y\}
disjoint from
T^{\prime}
, there exists an open arc
\beta\subset U

such that

\lim_{t\to 0}\beta(t)=\lim_{t\to 1}\beta(t)=y

and
\overline{\beta}
separates
\gamma[0,1)
from
T^{\prime}\setminus\{y\}
. Note that
C\subset T
and
C\cap\gamma[0,1)\not=\emptyset
. Then
C\setminus\{y\}
and
T^{\prime}\setminus\{y\}
are contained in distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{\beta}
.
Thus, the claim is proved.

In both cases,
y
is not an endpoint of
T^{\prime}
. If
y
is a cut point of
T^{\prime}
, then

\mu_{T}(y)=\mu_{T^{\prime}}(y)+1.

Otherwise,
y
is contained in a circle
C\subset T^{\prime}
that is not an end circle of
T^{\prime}
. Thus,

\mu_{T}(C)=\mu_{T^{\prime}}(C)+1.

For any branched point
x
of
T^{\prime}
with
x\neq y
, it is also a branched point of
T
with
\mu_{T}(x)=\mu_{T^{\prime}}(x)
. If
C_{1}\not=C
is a branched circle of
T^{\prime}
, then it is also a branched circle of
T
with
\mu_{T}(C_{1})=\mu_{T^{\prime}}(C_{1})
. Finally, by the claim above,
T\setminus T^{\prime}=T[X_{0},y]\setminus\{y\}
, which contains neither branched points nor branched circles of
T
. Thus, the lemma is proved.
∎

2.2.
Images of circle-trees

Let
f:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a branched covering, and let
U,V\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be simply connected domains such that
U
is a component of
f^{-1}(V)
and
\partial V
is locally connected. In particular, these conditions hold if
f
is a rational map with a connected and locally connected Julia set, and
U
is a Fatou domain of
f
.

A continuum
E\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is
full
if
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
is connected.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
C\subset\partial U
be a circle. Then
f(C)
is a finite circle-tree of
\partial V
. Moreover, each endpoint of
f(C)
is a critical value of
f
, and if
f:C\to f(C)
is not a homeomorphism, then each end circle of
f(C)
either contains a critical value or separates a critical value from
V
.

Proof.

Let
C^{\prime}\subset\partial V
be a circle such that
\#(f(C)\cap C^{\prime})\geq 2
. Denote
I_{1}=\{x\in C:f(x)\in C^{\prime}\}
and
I_{0}=C\setminus I_{1}
. Denote by
\{\alpha_{i}\}
the components of
I_{0}
. Then each
\alpha_{i}
is an open arc, and
f(\alpha_{i})
is contained in a component
B_{i}
of
\partial V\setminus C^{\prime}
. By Corollary
2.1
,
\overline{B_{i}}\cap C^{\prime}
consists of a single point, and hence
f(x_{i})=f(x^{\prime}_{i})
, where
x_{i}
and
x^{\prime}_{i}
are the endpoints of
\alpha_{i}
.

Figure 4.
The image of a circle.

Let
E_{i}
be the component of
(\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus V)\setminus C^{\prime}
containing
B_{i}
. Then
\overline{E_{i}}
is a full continuum, and
\overline{E_{i}}\cap C^{\prime}=\{f(x_{i})\}
. Moreover,
\overline{E_{i}}\cap\overline{E_{j}}=\emptyset
if
f(x_{i})\neq f(x_{j})
. We claim that
\overline{E_{i}}
contains critical values of
f
. Otherwise, there exists a disk
W\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
disjoint from the critical values of
f
such that
\overline{E_{i}}\subset W
. Thus,
f
is a homeomorphism on each component of
f^{-1}(W)
, which contradicts the assumption that
f(x_{i})=f(x^{\prime}_{i})
.

Denote by
Z
the set of points
f(x_{i})
for all components
\alpha_{i}
. Since
\overline{E_{i}}\cap\overline{E_{j}}=\emptyset
if
f(x_{i})\neq f(x_{j})
, we obtain
\#Z\leq 2d-2
by the above claim, where
d=\deg f
. For each point
z\in Z
, there exist at most
d
components
\alpha_{i}
such that
f(x_{i})=z
. Therefore,
I_{0}
has at most
d(2d-2)
components. Consequently,
I_{1}
has at most
d(2d-2)
components.

By Lemma
2.2
,
f(C)\cap C^{\prime}
is a continuum since
\#(f(C)\cap C^{\prime})\geq 2
. Then at least one component
\beta_{j}
of
I_{1}
is an arc. Since
f:\beta_{j}\to C^{\prime}
preserves the orientation induced by
U
and
V
, respectively, we obtain
f(I_{1})=C^{\prime}
. Thus,
C^{\prime}\subset f(C)
, and hence
f(C)
is a circle-tree of
\partial V
.

Assume that
f:C\to f(C)
is not a homeomorphism. Then each endpoint of
f(C)
is a critical value of
f
. Let
C^{\prime}
be an end circle of
f(C)
. We claim that
C^{\prime}
either contains a critical value or separates critical values from
V
. If this claim is false, each component of
f^{-1}(C^{\prime})
is a Jordan curve on which the restriction of
f
is injective. As above, denote
I_{1}=\{x\in C:f(x)\in C^{\prime}\}
. Since
C^{\prime}
is an end circle of
f(C)
,
I_{1}
has exactly one component
\beta
that is not a single point. Thus,
f(\beta)=C^{\prime}
. Since
f
is injective on each component of
f^{-1}(C^{\prime})
, it follows that
\beta=C
, and
f:C\to C^{\prime}
is a homeomorphism, a contradiction. The claim is proved.

There may exist infinitely many circles in
\partial V
containing critical values of
f
. However, for each critical value
v
of
f
, there exist at most
\deg f
circles of
\partial V
containing
v
, which are contained in
f(C)
. Therefore,
f(C)
is a finite circle-tree.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
T
be a finite circle-tree of
\partial U
. Then
f(T)
is a finite circle-tree of
\partial V
. Each endpoint of
f(T)
is either the image of an endpoint of
T
or a critical value of
f
. Each end circle of
f(T)
either is the image of an end circle of
T
, or contains a critical value of
f
, or separates a critical value of
f
from
V
.

Proof.

Let
C^{\prime}\subset\partial V
be a circle such that
\#(C^{\prime}\cap f(T))\geq 2
. We claim that there exists a circle
C\subset T
such that
C^{\prime}\subset f(C)
. By this claim,
C^{\prime}\subset f(T)
, and then
f(T)
is a circle-tree in
\partial V
.

To prove the claim, denote by
I_{0}\subset T
the set of points that are not contained in any circle in
\partial U
. Then
f(I_{0})\cap C^{\prime}=\emptyset
, for otherwise, there exists an open arc
\beta\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{V}
that joins a point in
f(I_{0})
to a point in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{V}
. Thus,
f^{-1}(\beta)
has a component in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U}
that joins a point in
I_{0}
to a point in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U}
, which is impossible.

Denote
I_{1}=T\setminus I_{0}
. Then each point of
I_{1}
is contained in a circle of
\partial U
.

Assume, by contradiction, that
C^{\prime}\not\subset f(C)
for any circle
C\subset T
. It follows that
\#(C^{\prime}\cap f(C))\leq 1
since
f(C)
is a circle-tree. Thus,
C^{\prime}\cap f(I_{1})
is a countable set, as
\partial U
has only countably many circles. Since
C^{\prime}\cap f(I_{0})=\emptyset
, we know that
C^{\prime}\cap f(T)=C^{\prime}\cap f(I_{1})
is a countable set. On the other hand, by Lemma
2.2
,
C^{\prime}\cap f(T)
is a continuum since
\#(C^{\prime}\cap f(T))\geq 2
, a contradiction. Thus, the claim is proved.

Immediately, each endpoint of
f(T)
is either a critical value of
f
or the image of an endpoint of
T
. Let
C^{\prime}
be an end circle of
f(T)
. By the claim above, there exists a circle
C\subset T
such that
C^{\prime}\subset f(C)
. Then
C^{\prime}
is also an end circle of
f(C)
. By Lemma
2.9
, either
f:C\to C^{\prime}
is a homeomorphism, or
C^{\prime}
contains a critical value, or
C^{\prime}
separates a critical value from
V
.

The number of circles
C^{\prime}
in the last case is clearly finite since
f
has a finite number of critical values. The circles
C^{\prime}
in the first case must be the images of end circles of
T
, and hence their number is finite. Note that there exist finitely many circles in
T
containing a pre-image of the critical values of
f
. Then the number of
C^{\prime}
in the second case is also finite.
Therefore,
f(T)
is a finite circle-tree in
\partial V
.
∎

2.3.
Invariant circle-trees

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map, and let
U
be a fixed Fatou domain of
f
. We will construct an
f
-invariant and finite circle-tree of
\partial U
. The process is similar to the construction of the Hubbard tree for PCF polynomials
[
14
]
.

We say a continuum
E

separates

P
if there exist two points of
P
in distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
.
A circle
C\subset\partial U
is called a
marked circle
(rel
P
) if
C
either intersects or separates
P
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Any eventually periodic point in
\partial U
receives finitely many internal rays in
U
. Consequently, there exist finitely many marked circles in
\partial U
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the lemma for a fixed point
z\in\partial U
. Let
\Theta\subset\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
be the set of angles corresponding to the internal rays in
U
landing at
z
. Then
\Theta
is compact, and
p_{d}:\Theta\to\Theta
is injective, where
p_{d}(z)=z^{d}
and
d=\deg f|_{U}
. By
[
37
, Lemma 18.8]
,
\Theta
is a finite set.

To show the finiteness of marked circles in
\partial U
, it suffices to prove that at most finitely many circles in
\partial U
pass through an eventually periodic point
z\in\partial U
. According to the previous discussion,
\partial U\setminus\{z\}
has finitely many components, each of which, together with the point
z
, contains at most one circle in
\partial U
passing through the point
z
. Thus, the lemma is proved.
∎

For two continua
E_{0}\subset E
, we call
E_{0}
a
skeleton
of
E
(rel
P
) if
E_{0}\cap P=E\cap P
and any two points of
P
in distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
also lie in distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{0}
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
T
be the finite circle-tree of
\partial U
spanned by
P\cap\partial U
together with all marked circles in
\partial U
. Then

(1)

each end of
T
is a marked point or a marked circle;

(2)

f(T)\subset T
, and
T
is a skeleton of
\partial U
rel
P
.

Proof.

By Lemmas
2.5
and
2.6
, each endpoint of
T
is contained in
P\cap\partial U
, and each end circle of
T
is a marked circle. By Lemma
2.10
, for each endpoint
y
of
f(T)
, either
y
is a critical value, or there exists an endpoint
x
of
T
such that
f(x)=y
. In both cases, we have
y\in P\cap\partial U
. For each end circle
C
of
f(T)
, either
C
is a marked circle, or
C
is the image of an end circle of
T
. In the latter case,
C
is also a marked circle. Therefore, each end of
f(T)
is contained in
T
. Thus,
f(T)\subset T
by Lemma
2.7
.

Immediately,
T\cap P=\partial U\cap P
. If two points
a,b\in P
are contained in distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\partial U
, then there exists a unique circle
C\subset\partial U
separating
a
from
b
. Thus,
C\subset T
since
C
is a marked circle. It follows that
T
is a skeleton of
\partial U
.
∎

The invariant circle-tree
T
obtained in Theorem
2.1
attracts every circle in
\partial U
.

Lemma 2.12
.

For any circle
C\subset\partial U
, there exists an integer
n\geq 0
such that
f^{n}(C)\subset T
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.9
and Theorem
2.1
, either
f(C)
is still a circle in
\partial U
, or
f(C)\subset T
. Thus, it suffices to show that
f^{N}(C)
is a marked circle for some integer
N\geq 0
, under the assumption that
f^{n}(C)
is always a circle for every
n\geq 0
. Otherwise, let
D_{n}
be the disk bounded by
f^{n}(C)
and disjoint from
U
for
n\geq 0
. Then
\overline{D_{n}}\cap P=\emptyset
. Thus,
f^{n}(D)=D_{n}
, which implies
D
is a Fatou domain of
f
. Consequently, there exists an integer
N\geq 0
such that
f^{N}(D)
is a periodic Fatou domain. Then
f^{N}(C)
is a marked circle, a contradiction.
∎

As a by-product, we obtain the following result regarding the locally branched points on the boundaries of Fatou domains. This generalizes a well-known fact for polynomials.

A circle
C\subset T
is called
regular
if it is neither a marked circle nor a branched circle of
T
. Note that
T
has only finitely many irregular circles.

Theorem 2.2
.

Every locally branched point of
\partial U
is eventually periodic.

Proof.

Let
x
be any locally branched point of
\partial U
.
We first claim that there exists an integer
N>1
such that
f^{N}(x)
is either a locally branched point of
T
or a point in
P_{f}\cap T
.

If
x
is contained in a circle
C
of
\partial U
, then there exists a component
E
of
\partial U\setminus C
such that
\overline{E}\cap C=\{x\}
. Since
\bigcup_{n>0}(f^{-n}(T)\cap\partial U)
is dense in
\partial U
, there exists a point
y\in E
such that
f^{n_{0}}(y)\in T
for some integer
n_{0}>0
. Then
x
is a locally branched point of
T_{1}=T[y,C]
. By Lemma
2.12
, there exists an integer
N\geq n_{0}
such that
f^{N}(x)\in f^{N}(C)\subset T
. It follows from Lemma
2.10
that
f^{N}(T_{1})
is a circle-tree whose ends are contained in
T
, and thus
f^{N}(T_{1})\subset T
by Lemma
2.5
. Therefore, the claim holds.

If
x
avoids any circle in
\partial U
, then
x
is a branched point of
\partial U
. Thus,
\partial U\setminus\{x\}
has at least three components
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
, and
E_{3}
.
By a similar argument as above, there exist a point
y_{i}\in E_{i}
and an integer
n_{i}>0
for each
i=1,2,3
such that
f^{n_{i}}(y_{i})\in T
, and the circle-tree
f^{N}(T_{1})
is contained in
T
with
T_{1}:=T[y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}]\ni x
and
N:=\max\{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\}
. Thus, the claim still holds.

Since
T
has only finitely many branched points by Lemma
2.8
, it follows from the above claim that either
x
is eventually periodic, or
f^{n}(x)
is a locally branched point but not a branched point of
T
for every
n\geq N
. It suffices to consider the latter. In this situation, each
f^{n}(x)
is a cut point of
T
and contained in a circle
C_{n}
of
T
for
n\geq N
.

If
C_{n_{i}}=C
for an infinite sequence
\{n_{i}\}
, then
x
is eventually periodic since each circle contains finitely many cut points of
T
by Lemma
2.8
. Thus, we may further assume that
C_{n},n\geq N
are pairwise different circles of
T
.

Since
T
has finitely many irregular circles, the circle
C_{n}
is regular for each sufficiently large integer
n
. For a regular circle
C
, there exists a dichotomy: either
D_{C}
contains a component of
f^{-1}(U)
, or
f:\overline{D_{C}}\to f(\overline{D_{C}})
is a homeomorphism, where
D_{C}
denotes the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus C
disjoint from
U
. Clearly, there exist finitely many regular circles of the first type in
T
. It follows that

C_{n+1}=f(C_{n})
and
D_{C_{n+1}}=f(D_{C_{n}})
for every sufficiently large integer
n
. This implies the existence of wandering Fatou domains, a contradiction.
∎

2.4.
A Fatou domain without invariant graphs on the boundary

In this subsection, we give an example of a PCF rational map with a fixed Fatou domain
U
, such that
\partial U
admits no invariant graphs.

Let
X\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a compact set. A continuous map
\phi:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is an
isotopy rel
\boldsymbol{X}
if each map
\phi_{s}=\phi(\cdot,s)
is a homeomorphism of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
and
\phi_{s}(z)=z
for every
z\in X
and
s\in[0,1]
. In this case, we say the homeomorphisms
\phi_{0}
and
\phi_{1}
are isotopic rel
X
. Sometimes, we write the isotopy
\phi
as
\{\phi_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
.

Moreover, we say two subsets
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
are
isotopic rel
\boldsymbol{X}
if there exists a homeomorphism
h:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
that is isotopic to the identity map rel
X
such that
h(E_{1})=E_{2}
. In this paper,
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are typically considered Jordan curves, (open) arcs, or graphs.

Theorem 2.3
.

There exist a cubic PCF rational map
f
and a fixed Fatou domain
U
of
f
such that
\partial U
contains infinitely many circles, and for any arc
\gamma\subset\partial U
,
f^{n}(\gamma)=\partial U
for some integer
n\geq 1
. Consequently, there exist no invariant graphs on
\partial U
.

Let
g(z)=z^{2}-2
. Its Julia set is
[-2,2]
. Let
D
be the disk with diameter
[-2,0]
, and let
B
be the domain bounded by the three external rays landing at the points
0
and
-2
. Then there exists a homeomorphism
\varphi
from
B\setminus\overline{D}
to
B\setminus[-2,0]
, and
\varphi
can be continuously extended to the boundary such that
\varphi=id
on the three external rays and
\varphi(x+\textbf{i}y)=x
on
\partial D
.

Figure 5.
The construction of
\tilde{f}
.

Let
h:D\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus[-2,2]
be a homeomorphism such that
h=g\circ\varphi
on
\partial D
. Define

\tilde{f}=\begin{cases}g&\text{ on }\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus B,\\
g\circ\varphi&\text{ on }B\setminus\overline{D},\\
h&\text{ on }\overline{D}.\end{cases}

Then
\tilde{f}
is a branched covering of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
with
\deg\tilde{f}=3
. It has three critical points
-2
,
0
, and
\infty
, with
\deg(\tilde{f}|_{\tilde{z}=0})=3
and
\deg(\tilde{f}|_{\tilde{z}=-2})=\deg(\tilde{f}|_{\tilde{z}=\infty})=2
. Its post-critical set is
P_{\tilde{f}}=\{-2,2,\infty\}
. Thus,
\tilde{f}
is combinatorially equivalent to a rational map
f
by the Thurston theorem (see
[
13
]
or
[
35
]
). This means there exists a pair of orientation-preserving homeomorphisms
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
\phi_{1}
is isotopic to
\phi_{0}
rel
P_{\tilde{f}}
, and
f:=\phi_{0}\circ\tilde{f}\circ\phi_{1}^{-1}
is a rational map.

Denote the
\phi_{0}
-image of
-2
,
0
,
2
, and
\infty
by
a
,
b
,
a_{1}
, and
c
, respectively. Then the critical points of
f
are
a,b,c
with
\deg(f|_{z=b})=3
and
\deg(f|_{z=a})=\deg(f|_{z=c})=2
. Moreover,

f(b)=a,\quad f(a)=a_{1}=f(a_{1}),\quad\text{and}\quad f(c)=c.

Thus,
P_{f}=\{a,a_{1},c\}
. The map
f
has exactly one periodic Fatou domain
U
containing
c
. Then
f(U)=U
and
\deg(f|_{U})=2
. Thus,
f^{-1}(U)
has another component,
U^{\prime}
, in addition to
U
.

Proposition 2.1
.

The lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
of
U
consists of leaves
L_{n},n\geq 1
such that the endpoints of
L_{n}
are
e^{{\pi\textup{\bf i}}/{2^{n}}}
and
e^{-\pi\textup{\bf i}/{2^{n}}}
.

Proof.

Let
W_{0}
be a round disk under the Böttcher coordinate of
U
that is compactly contained in
U
, and let
W_{n}
be the component of
f^{-n}(W_{0})
containing the fixed point
c
for
n\geq 1
. Then
W_{n}\subset W_{n+1}
, and
\bigcup_{n\geq 0}W_{n}=U
.

Denote by
R_{f}(\theta)
the internal ray of
f
in
U
with angle
\theta\in(-\pi,\pi]
, and by
R_{g}(\theta)
the external ray of
g
with angle
\theta\in(-\pi,\pi]
. Then

\tilde{f}(R_{g}(0))=R_{g}(0)
. We may assume that
\phi_{0}(R_{g}(0))
coincides with
R_{f}(0)
in
W_{0}
. Then
\phi_{1}(R_{g}(0))
coincides with
R_{f}(0)
in
W_{1}
since
f(\phi_{1}(R_{g}(0))=\phi_{0}(R_{g}(0))
. Thus, there exists an isotopy
\{\phi_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
rel
P_{\tilde{f}}
such that
\phi_{1}=\phi_{0}
on
R_{g}(0)\cap W_{0}
.

Lifting the isotopy
\{\phi_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
inductively by Lemma
A.2
, we get a sequence of homeomorphisms
\{\phi_{n}\}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
\phi_{n+1}
is isotopic to
\phi_{n}
rel
P_{\tilde{f}}
and
f\circ\phi_{n+1}=\phi_{n}\circ\tilde{f}
. Thus,
\phi_{n+1}(R_{g}(0))
coincides with
R_{f}(0)
in
W_{n+1}
, and
f(\phi_{n+1}(R_{g}(0))=\phi_{n}(R_{g}(0))
. By Lemma
A.1
,
\phi_{n}(R_{g}(0))
converges to
R_{f}(0)
. Thus,
R_{f}(0)
lands at the point
a_{1}
.

Since
f^{-1}(a_{1})=\{a,a_{1}\}
, the ray
R_{f}(\pi)
lands at the point
a
, and
f^{-1}(R_{f}(0))
has a component in
U^{\prime}
that joins the point
a
and the unique point
c^{\prime}
of
f^{-1}(c)
in
U^{\prime}
. Since
f^{-1}(a)=b
, both
R_{f}(\pm{\pi}/{2})
land at the point
b
, and a component of
f^{-1}(R_{f}(\pi))
in
U^{\prime}
connects
c^{\prime}
and the critical point
b
. Consequently,
a,b\in\partial U\cap\partial U^{\prime}
. It follows that
R_{f}(\theta_{1})
and
R_{f}(\theta_{2})
land at distinct points if
\theta_{1}\in(\pi/2,\pi)
and
\theta_{2}\in(-\pi,-\pi/2)
.

Consider the simply connected domain bounded by
R_{f}(\pi)
and
R_{f}(\pm{\pi}/{2})
. It contains no critical values of
f
. Thus, its pre-image has three components, one of which is bounded by
R_{f}(\pm{\pi}/{2})
and
R_{f}(\pm{\pi}/{4})
. Thus,
R_{f}(\pm{\pi}/{4})
land at the same point. Moreover,
R_{f}(\theta_{1})
and
R_{f}(\theta_{2})
land at distinct points if
\theta_{1}\in(\pi/4,\pi/2)
and
\theta_{2}\in(-\pi/2,-\pi/4)
.

Inductively taking pre-images as above, the rays
R_{f}(\pm{\pi}/{2^{n}})
land at the same point, but
R_{f}(\theta_{1})
and
R_{f}(\theta_{2})
land at distinct points if
\theta_{1}\in(\pi/2^{n},\pi/2^{n-1})
and
\theta_{2}\in(-\pi/2^{n-1},-\pi/2^{n})
for
n\geq 2
.

Now, we have proved that
L_{n}
is a leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
, and there exists no leaf that joins
e^{\textup{\bf i}\theta_{1}}
to
e^{\textup{\bf i}\theta_{2}}
if
\theta_{1}\in(\pi/2^{n},\pi/2^{n-1})
and
\theta_{2}\in(-\pi/2^{n-1},-\pi/2^{n})
for
n\geq 1
. It follows that if
L
is a leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
that joins
e^{\textup{\bf i}\theta_{1}}
to
e^{\textup{\bf i}\theta_{2}}
, then
|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}|<\pi/2
.

Assume that
L
is a leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
that joins
e^{\textup{\bf i}\theta_{1}}
to
e^{\textup{\bf i}\theta_{2}}
. Then there exists a leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}_{U}
that joins
e^{2^{n}\textup{\bf i}\theta_{1}}
to
e^{2^{n}\textup{\bf i}\theta_{2}}
for
n\geq 1
, except when
2^{n}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})\equiv 0\penalty\ \textup{mod}\penalty\ {2\pi}
. In particular, there exists an integer
n\geq 1
such that
\pi/2<2^{n}|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}|\leq\pi
. This is a contradiction.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.3
.

Denote by
\phi:\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}\to U
the inverse of the Böttcher coordinate for
U
. It can be extended continuously to the boundary. For any arc
\gamma\subset\partial U
, Proposition
2.1
implies that
\phi^{-1}(\gamma)
must contain a non-trivial interval. Thus,
f^{n}(\gamma)=\partial U
for some integer
n\geq 1
.
∎

Up to conformal conjugacy, the rational map
f
constructed above has the form

f(z)=(z^{2}-6z+9-8/z)/3

with the critical points
-1,2,
and
\infty
; see Figure
6
for its Julia set.

Figure 6.
The Julia set of
f(z)=(z^{2}-6z+9-8/z)/3
.

2.5.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map, and let
U
be a fixed Fatou domain of
f
. Let
T\subset\partial U
be the
f
-invariant circle-tree obtained in Theorem
2.1
. Our proof strategy is as follows. First, we will find a graph
G_{1}
serving as a skeleton of
T
rel
P
such that
f^{-1}(G_{1})
contains a graph
G_{2}
that is isotopic to
G_{1}
rel
P
. Then, by lifting, we obtain a sequence of graphs
\{G_{n}\}
, and finally, we will prove that
\{G_{n}\}
converges to an invariant graph
G
.

Let
X_{0}\subset T
be the union of
P
together with the set of cut points of
T
. Then
X_{0}
is a compact set containing all endpoints of
T
, and
f(X_{0})\subset X_{0}
. Each component of
T\setminus X_{0}
is an open arc in a circle of
T
. Denote
X_{n}:=f^{-n}(X_{0})
for
n\geq 0
. Then
X_{n}\subset X_{n+1}
.

Recall that a circle
C\subset T
is regular if it is neither a marked circle nor a branched circle of
T
. Thus, each regular circle
C
contains exactly two points of
X_{0}
, which cut
C
into two open arcs
C^{+}
and
C^{-}
. Set

G_{1}:=T\setminus\bigcup_{C}C^{-},

where
C
ranges over all regular circles in
T
. Then
G_{1}
is a graph since there exist finitely many irregular circles in
T
, and
G_{1}
is a skeleton of
\partial U
by Theorem
2.1
.

To construct
G_{2}\subset f^{-1}(G_{1})
, we need to go beyond
\partial U
. Let
\alpha_{1}
be a component of
G_{1}\setminus X_{1}
. Its image
f(\alpha_{1})
is a component of
T\setminus X_{0}
. Thus, there exists a circle
C\subset T
such that
f(\alpha_{1})
is a component of
C\setminus X_{0}
. If
C
is irregular, then
f(\alpha_{1})\subset C\subset G_{1}
. If
C
is regular, then
f(\alpha_{1})
equals either
C^{+}
or
C^{-}
.

•

If
f(\alpha_{1})=C^{+}
, we still have
f(\alpha_{1})\subset G_{1}
.

•

If
f(\alpha_{1})=C^{-}
, since
C^{+}
and
C^{-}
are isotopic rel
X_{0}
, there exists a unique component
\alpha_{1}^{+}
of
f^{-1}(C^{+})
isotopic to
\alpha_{1}
rel
X_{1}
. Let
B(\alpha_{1})
denote the closed disk bounded by
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{1}^{+}
disjoint from
U
. Then
B(\alpha_{1})\cap G_{1}=\overline{\alpha_{1}}
and
B(\alpha_{1})\cap X_{1}=\{\alpha_{1}(0),\alpha_{1}(1)\}
. Such a component
\alpha_{1}
of
G_{1}\setminus X_{1}
is called a
deformation arc
of
G_{1}
.

Define the graph
G_{2}
as

G_{2}:=\left(G_{1}\setminus\bigcup\alpha_{1}\right)\cup\bigcup\alpha^{+}_{1},

where the union is taken over all deformation arcs of
G_{1}
. From the previous discussion, we have
f(G_{2})\subset G_{1}
, and
there exists an isotopy
\Theta^{1}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
rel
P
such that
\Theta^{1}_{t}:=\Theta^{1}(\cdot,t)
satisfies

(1)

\Theta^{1}_{0}=id
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
;

(2)

\Theta^{1}_{t}(z)=z
on a neighborhood of the attracting cycles of
f
for
t\in[0,1]
;

(3)

if
z\in G_{1}
is not in any deformation arc, then
\Theta^{1}_{t}(z)=z
for
t\in[0,1]
; and

(4)

if
\alpha_{1}
is a deformation arc of
G_{1}
, then
\Theta^{1}_{1}(\alpha_{1})=\alpha_{1}^{+}
, and
\Theta^{1}(\overline{\alpha_{1}}\times[0,1])=B(\alpha_{1})
.

Consequently, we have
\theta_{1}(G_{1})=G_{2}
with
\theta_{1}:=\Theta^{1}_{1}
.

By inductively applying Lemma
A.2
, we obtain an isotopy
\Theta^{n}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
rel
P
and a graph
G_{n+1}
for each
n\geq 1
, such that
\Theta^{n}_{0}=id
and
\Theta^{n}_{t}\circ f(z)=f\circ\Theta^{n+1}_{t}(z)
for all
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
and
t\in[0,1]
, and that
G_{n+1}=\theta_{n}(G_{n})
with
\theta_{n}:=\Theta^{n}_{1}
.
Thus,
f(G_{n+1})\subset G_{n}
. In addition, there exist some components of
G_{n}\setminus X_{n}
, called the
deformation arcs
of
G_{n}
(under
\Theta^{n}
), such that

(a)

if
z\in G_{n}
is not in any deformation arc of
G_{n}
, then
\Theta^{n}_{t}(z)=z
for
t\in[0,1]
;

(b)

if
\alpha_{n}
is a deformation arc of
G_{n}
, then
the deformation of
\overline{\alpha_{n}}
under
\Theta^{n}
, denoted by
B(\alpha_{n})
, is a closed disk satisfying
B(\alpha_{n})\cap G_{n}=\overline{\alpha_{n}}
and
B(\alpha_{n})\cap X_{n}=\{\alpha_{n}(0),\alpha_{n}(1)\}
.

Figure 7.
The images of
\{G_{n}\}
.

Denote
\phi_{n}=\theta_{n-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{0}
for
n\geq 1
with
\theta_{0}:=id
. Then
G_{n}=\phi_{n}(G_{1})
. By Lemma
A.3
,
\{\phi_{n}\}
uniformly converges to a quotient map
\varphi
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Consequently,
f(G)\subset G
, where
G
is defined as
G:=\varphi(G_{1})
.
In order to show that
G
is a graph, we need to clarify the relation between the deformation arcs of
G_{m}
and
G_{n}
for
m>n\geq 1
.

Fix a deformation arc
\alpha_{n}
of
G_{n}
with
n\geq 1
. Set
\alpha_{n-k}:=f^{k}(\alpha_{n})
for
0\leq k\leq n
. From the lifting construction of
\Theta^{n}
, it follows that, for
0\leq k\leq n-1
,
\alpha_{n-k}
is a deformation arc of
G_{n-k}
and
f^{k}(B(\alpha_{n}))=B(\alpha_{n-k})
, and that
\alpha_{0}=C^{-}
for a regular circle
C
of
T
and

f^{n}:B(\alpha_{n})\to B(\alpha_{0})
is a homeomorphism. Here,
B(\alpha_{0})=B(C^{-})
refers to the closure of the component of
\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus C
disjoint from
U
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\alpha_{m}
and
\beta_{n}
be distinct deformation arcs of
G_{m}
and
G_{n}
, respectively, with
m\geq n\geq 1
. Then either
B(\alpha_{m})\subset B(\beta_{n})
, or
B(\alpha_{m})\cap B(\beta_{n})=\emptyset
, or
B(\alpha_{m})
intersects
B(\beta_{n})
at a single point of
X_{n}
.

Proof.

Set
\beta_{0}:=f^{n}(\beta_{n})
and
\alpha_{m-n}:=f^{n}(\alpha_{m})
. By definition,
B(\beta_{0})
is the closure of a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U}
, and the interior of
B(\alpha_{m-n})
is contained in a component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U}
. Then by Lemma
2.1
, either
\overline{D}=B(\beta_{0})
, or
\overline{D}\cap B(\beta_{0})=\emptyset
, or
\overline{D}\cap B(\beta_{0})
is a singleton in
X_{0}
. It follows that either
B(\alpha_{m-n})\subset B(\beta_{0})
, or
B(\alpha_{m-n})\cap B(\beta_{0})=\emptyset
, or
B(\alpha_{n-m})
intersects
B(\beta_{0})
at a single point of
X_{0}
. Thus, this proposition can be proved by a pullback argument.
∎

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
m>n\geq 1
be integers, and let
\alpha_{n}
be any deformation arc of
G_{n}
.

(1)

Let
x\in G_{1}
be a point such that
\phi_{n}(x)\in\alpha_{n}
. Then
\phi_{m}(x)\in B(\alpha_{n})
. Consequently, if
\phi_{m}(x)
is contained in a deformation arc
\alpha_{m}
of
G_{m}
, then
B(\alpha_{m})\subset B(\alpha_{n})
.

(2)

Let
\alpha\subset G_{1}
be an open arc such that
\phi_{n}(\alpha)=\alpha_{n}
. Then
G_{m}\cap B(\alpha_{n})=\phi_{m}(\overline{\alpha})
.

Proof.

(1) Let
n=n_{1}<\cdots<n_{s}<n_{s+1}:=m
be all integers such that
\phi_{n_{i}}(x)
belongs to a deformation arc
\alpha_{n_{i}}
of
G_{n_{i}}
for
i=1,\ldots,s
. For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
and any
n_{i}<k\leq n_{i+1}
, it follows from the definition of
\phi_{n}
and properties (a) and (b) of
\Theta^{n}
that

\phi_{k}(x)=\theta_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{n_{i}}\circ\phi_{n_{i}}(x)=\theta_{n_{i}}\circ\phi_{n_{i}}(x)\in\theta_{n_{i}}(\alpha_{n_{i}})\subset B(\alpha_{n_{i}}).

Thus,
\phi_{n_{i+1}}(x)\in B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
, and furthermore
\phi_{n_{i+1}}(x)\in B(\alpha_{n_{i}})\cap B(\alpha_{n_{i+1}})
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,s-1\}
.
This implies
B(\alpha_{n_{i+1}})\subset B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,s-1\}
by Proposition
2.2
, since

\phi_{n_{i+1}}(x)\in\alpha_{n_{i+1}}
, which is disjoint from
X_{n_{i+1}}
. Therefore,
\phi_{m}(x)=\phi_{n_{s+1}}(x)\in B(\alpha_{n_{s}})\subset\cdots\subset B(\alpha_{n})
.

(2) By statement (1), we immediately get that
\phi_{m}(\overline{\alpha})\subset B(\alpha_{n})
. Therefore, to prove
\phi_{m}(\overline{\alpha})=G_{m}\cap B(\alpha_{n})
, it suffices to show that
\phi_{m}(z)\not\in B(\alpha_{n})
for any
z\in G_{1}\setminus\overline{\alpha}
.

First, note that
\phi_{n}(z)\notin B(\alpha_{n})
since
G_{n}\cap B(\alpha_{n})=\overline{\alpha}_{n}
. If
\phi_{k}(z)
does not belong to any deformation arc of
G_{k}
for every
n\leq k<m
, then
