\phi_{m}(z)=\phi_{m-1}(z)=\cdots=\phi_{n}(z)\notin B(\alpha_{n}).

Otherwise, let
n_{1}\in[n,m)
be the smallest integer such that
\phi_{n_{1}}(z)
belongs to a deformation arc
\alpha_{n_{1}}
of
G_{n_{1}}
. Then
\phi_{n}(z),\phi_{m}(z)\in B(\alpha_{n_{1}})
by statement (1). Since
\phi_{n}(z)\not\in B(\alpha_{n})
, it follows from Proposition
2.2
that
B(\alpha_{n})\cap B(\alpha_{n_{1}})
is either empty or a singleton in
X_{n}
. Note also that
B(\alpha_{n})\cap X_{n}=\{\alpha_{n}(0),\alpha_{n}(1)\}
by property (b) above. Thus,
\phi_{m}(z)\not\in B(\alpha_{n})
.
∎

Corollary 2.3
.

For each point
z\in G\setminus\partial U
, there exist an integer
n\geq 1
and a component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U}
, such that
f^{n}(z)\in\overline{D}
and
\partial D
is a regular circle of
\partial U
.

Proof.

Let
x\in G_{1}
be a point such that
\varphi(x)=z
. Since
z\not\in\partial U
, there exists a smallest integer
n_{0}\geq 1
such that
\phi_{n_{0}}(x)
belongs to a deformation arc
\alpha_{n_{0}}
of
G_{n_{0}}
. It then follows from Proposition
2.3
(1) that
z=\varphi(x)\in B(\alpha_{n_{0}})
. By the discussion before Proposition
2.2
,
f^{n_{0}}(B(\alpha_{n_{0}}))
is the closure of a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U}
bounded by a regular circle of
\partial U
.
∎

The following result is a key part of the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proposition 2.4
.

For any two distinct points
x,y\in G_{1}
with
\varphi(x)=\varphi(y)
, there exists an arc
\beta\subset G_{1}
connecting
x
and
y
such that
\varphi(\beta)=\varphi(x)
.

Proof.

A point
z\in G_{1}
is called
finitely deforming
(
under
\{\phi_{n}\}
) if there exists an integer
n(z)\geq 1
such that
\phi_{n}(z)
does not belong to any deformation arc of
G_{n}
for every
n\geq n(z)
.
Thus, if
z\in G_{1}
is
infinitely deforming
, we can find an increasing sequence
\{n_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
such that
\phi_{n_{i}}(z)
belongs to a deformation arc
\alpha_{n_{i}}
of
G_{n_{i}}
for all
i\geq 1
. In this case, it holds that
B(\alpha_{n_{i+1}})\subset B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
by Proposition
2.3
(1). According to Lemma
A.4
, the homotopic diameters of
B(C)
for all regular circles
C
of
T
are bounded above. Thus, Lemma
A.1
implies
\bigcap_{i\geq 1}B(\alpha_{n_{i}})=\{\varphi(z)\}
.

Since
\varphi(x)=\varphi(y)
, at least one of
\{x,y\}
, say
x
, is infinitely deforming. As above, there exist an increasing sequence
\{n_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
and a deformation arc
\alpha_{n_{i}}
of
G_{n_{i}}
for each
i\geq 1
, such that
\phi_{n_{i}}(x)\in\alpha_{n_{i}}
,
B(\alpha_{n_{i+1}})\subset B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
, and
\bigcap_{i\geq 1}B(\alpha_{n_{i}})=\{\varphi(x)\}
.

Case 1. The point
y
is finitely deforming.

In this case, we have
\varphi(y)=\phi_{n}(y)
for every
n\geq n(y)
. Since
\varphi(x)=\varphi(y)
, it follows that
\phi_{n_{i}}(y)=\varphi(y)\in B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
for
n_{i}>n(y)
. Then
\phi_{n_{i}}(y)\in G_{n_{i}}\cap B(\alpha_{n_{i}})=\overline{\alpha_{n_{i}}}
. Hence,
\phi_{n_{i}}(y)
is an endpoint of
\alpha_{n_{i}}
.

Let
\gamma_{i}
be the sub-arc of
\alpha_{n_{i}}
connecting
\phi_{n_{i}}(y)
and
\phi_{n_{i}}(x)
. Then
\beta_{i}:=\phi_{n_{i}}^{-1}(\gamma_{i})
is an arc in
G_{1}
connecting
x
and
y
. Since there exist only finitely many distinct arcs in
G_{1}
connecting
x
and
y
, by passing to a subsequence of
\{i\}
, we have
\beta=\beta_{i}
and
\phi_{n_{i}}(\beta)=\gamma_{i}\subset\alpha_{n_{i}}
for every
i\geq 1
. This implies
\varphi(\beta)=\varphi(x)
.

Case 2. The point
y
is infinitely deforming.

In this case, we obtain another increasing sequence
\{m_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
and a deformation arc
\delta_{m_{j}}
of
G_{m_{j}}
for each
j
, such that
\phi_{m_{j}}(y)\in\delta_{m_{j}}
,
B(\delta_{m_{j+1}})\subset B(\delta_{m_{j}})
, and
\{\varphi(y)\}=\bigcap_{j\geq 1}B(\delta_{m_{j}})
. Since
\varphi(x)=\varphi(y)
, it follows from Proposition
2.2
and Proposition
2.3
(1) that, if
m_{j}\geq n_{i}
, either
B(\delta_{m_{j}})\subset B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
, or
B(\delta_{m_{j}})
intersects
B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
at a single point in
X_{n_{i}}
.

Case 2.1. There exist
m_{j}\geq n_{i}
such that
B(\delta_{m_{j}})\cap B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
is a singleton
w\in X_{n_{i}}
.

Since
\phi_{n}(x)\in B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
and
\phi_{n}(y)\in B(\delta_{m_{j}})
for each sufficiently large integer
n
by Proposition
2.3
(1), it follows that
\varphi(x)\in B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
and
\varphi(y)\in B(\delta_{m_{j}})
. Thus,
\varphi(x)=\varphi(y)=w
. Assume
\phi_{n_{i}}(z)=w
. Then
\varphi(z)=\phi_{n}(z)=w
for every
n\geq n_{i}
. By applying Case 1 to
\{x,z\}
and
\{z,y\}
, respectively, we obtain the required arc
\beta
.

Case 2.2. For each pair
m_{j}\geq n_{i}
, it holds that
B(\delta_{m_{j}})\subset B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
.

Let
\gamma_{i}:=\phi_{n_{i}}^{-1}(\alpha_{i})\subset G_{1}
be the arc containing
x
. For any pair
m_{j}\geq n_{i}
, by Proposition
2.3
(2), we have
\phi_{m_{j}}(x)\in\phi_{m_{j}}({\overline{\gamma_{i}}})=G_{m_{j}}\cap B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
. Note also that
\phi_{m_{j}}(y)\in G_{m_{j}}\cap B(\delta_{m_{j}})\subset G_{m_{j}}\cap B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
. Thus,
\phi_{m_{j}}(x),\phi_{m_{j}}(y)\in\phi_{m_{j}}({\overline{\gamma_{i}}})
. This implies that there exists a sub-arc
\beta_{i}\subset\gamma_{i}
joining
x
to
y
such that
\phi_{m_{j}}(\beta_{i})\subset B(\alpha_{n_{i}})
.
Since there exist finitely many arcs in
G_{1}
joining
x
to
y
, by passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
\beta_{i}=\beta
for all
i\geq 1
. Then
\varphi(\beta)=\lim_{j\to\infty}\phi_{m_{j}}(\beta)
coincides with
\bigcap_{i\geq 1}B(\alpha_{n_{i}})=\{\varphi(x)\}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Clearly,
G=\lim_{n\to\infty}G_{n}=\varphi(G_{1})
is an
f
-invariant continuum. Note that
G_{n+1}
lies in the component
E_{n}
of
f^{-n}(\overline{U})
containing
\overline{U}
. Then
G\subset K_{U}=\overline{\bigcup_{n\geq 1}E_{n}}
.

We claim that
\varphi(\alpha)
is not a singleton for any component
\alpha
of
G_{1}\setminus X_{1}
. If
\alpha
has two distinct endpoints, then the claim is immediate since
\varphi=id
on
X_{1}\cap G_{1}
. In the remaining case,
\overline{\alpha}
is a circle in
G_{1}
. If
\phi_{n}(x)
does not belong to the deforming arcs of
G_{n}
for any
x\in\alpha
and every
n\geq 1
, we have
\varphi(\overline{\alpha})=\overline{\alpha}
, and the claim holds. Otherwise, there exist a point
x\in\alpha
and a smallest integer
n_{0}\geq 1
such that
\phi_{n_{0}}(x)
belongs to a deformation arc
\alpha_{n_{0}}
of
G_{n_{0}}
. Then
\alpha_{n_{0}}\subset\alpha
, and
\phi_{n_{0}}=id
on
\alpha_{n_{0}}
. This implies that
\varphi=id
on the two endpoints of
\alpha_{n_{0}}
. Thus, the claim is proved.

Since
\varphi
is the identity on
X_{1}\cap G_{1}
, which divides
G_{1}
into open arcs, by Proposition
2.4
and the claim above, the pre-image of each point of
G
under
\varphi|_{G_{1}}
is either a singleton or an arc in
G_{1}
. This implies that
G
is a graph homeomorphic to
G_{1}
.

Finally, to prove that
G
is isotopic to
G_{1}
rel
P
, it suffices to show
G_{1}\cap P=G\cap P
, as
G_{n}
is isotopic to
G_{1}
rel
P
for every
n\geq 1
. Since
\varphi
is the identity on
P
and
G_{1}\cap P=\partial U\cap P
, it follows that

G_{1}\cap P=\partial U\cap P\subset G\cap\partial U\cap P\subset\partial U\cap P=G_{1}\cap P
. On the other hand, we have
(G\setminus\partial U)\cap P=\emptyset
by Corollary
2.3
. Thus,
G_{1}\cap P=G\cap P
.
∎

3.
Fatou chains

In this section, we establish some basic properties of Fatou chains and prove Theorem
1.3
.

Throughout this section, let
f
be a rational map with
J_{f}\not=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Recall that a level-
0
Fatou chain of
f
is the closure of a Fatou domain of
f
. By induction, define a continuum
K\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
as a level-
(n+1)
Fatou chain of
f
if there exists a sequence
\{E_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
of continua, each composed of finitely many level-
n
Fatou chains, such that

E_{k}\subset E_{k+1}\quad\text{and}\quad K=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}}.

Definition 3.1
.

A level-
n

(n\geq 0)
Fatou chain
K
is called a level-
n

extremal (Fatou) chain
if any level-
n
Fatou chain that intersects
K
at a point in
F_{f}
is contained in
K
.

By definition, each level-
0
extremal chain is the closure of a Fatou domain.

Lemma 3.1
.

For every
n>0
and any Fatou domain
U
of
f
, there exists a unique level-
n
extremal chain
K
containing
U
. Moreover, there exists a sequence
\{E_{k}\}
of continua, each of which is the union of finitely many level-
(n-1)
extremal chains, such that
E_{k}\subset E_{k+1}
and
K=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}}
.

Proof.

We first prove the lemma in the case of
n=1
.

Let
\Sigma(U)
denote the collection of Fatou domains
U^{\prime}
for which both
U
and
U^{\prime}
are contained in a continuum
E(U,U^{\prime})
consisting of finitely many level-
0
chains.

Enumerate the elements of
\Sigma(U)
by
U_{i}
,
i\geq 0
, and fix
E(U,U_{i})
for each
i
. For every
k\geq 0
, define

E_{k}=\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k}E(U,U_{i})\quad\text{and}\quad K=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}}.

Then
K
is a level-
1
Fatou chain by definition. It remains to verify that
K
is extremal.

Now, consider any other level-
1
Fatou chain
K^{\prime}
such that
(K^{\prime}\cap K)\cap F_{f}\neq\emptyset
. Then
K^{\prime}\cap K
contains a Fatou domain
V
. By definition, assume
K^{\prime}=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E^{\prime}_{m}}
, where
E^{\prime}_{m}
is the union of a finite number of level-
0
Fatou chains, and
E^{\prime}_{m}\subset E^{\prime}_{m+1}
for every
m\geq 0
.

Since
V\subset K^{\prime}
, it follows that
V\subset E^{\prime}_{m}
for any sufficiently large integer
m
. Similarly, we have
V\in\Sigma(U)
.
Hence, each level-
0
Fatou chain in
E^{\prime}_{m}
is contained in
\Sigma(U)
.
By the construction of
E_{k}
, we obtain
E^{\prime}_{m}\subset E_{k}
for a sufficiently large integer
k
. This implies
K^{\prime}\subset K
. Therefore,
K
is a level-
1
extremal chain.

Assume that the lemma holds for some
n\geq 1
. Then there exists a unique level-
n
extremal chain
\sigma
containing
U
. Similarly, as in the case of
n=1
, let
\Sigma(\sigma)
be the collection of all level-
n
extremal chains
\sigma^{\prime}
for which both
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are contained in a continuum
E(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})
consisting of finitely many level-
n
extremal chains.

Note that
\Sigma(\sigma)
is a finite or countable collection. Thus,
\Sigma(\sigma)=\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
. Fix
E(\sigma,\sigma_{i})
for each
\sigma_{i}
. For every
k\geq 0
, define

E_{k}=\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k}E(\sigma,\sigma_{i})\quad\text{and}\quad K=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}}.

By definition,
K
is a level-
(n+1)
Fatou chain.
Finally, applying a similar argument as in the case of
n=1
, we can show that
K
is an extremal chain of level-
(n+1)
.
∎

Here are some examples of extremal chains.
For a polynomial, the entire Riemann sphere
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is its level-
1
extremal chain.
On the other hand, any level-
n
extremal chain (
n\geq 0
) of a Sierpiński rational map is the closure of a Fatou domain.

If
f
is a Newton map, the union of the attracting basins for all attracting fixed points is contained in a level-
1
extremal chain of
f
. This chain contains
J_{f}
. Thus,
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is a level-
2
extremal chain of
f
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
K\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a level-
n
extremal chain
(n\geq 0)
of
f
. Then

(1)

f(K)
is also a level-
n
extremal chain; and

(2)

f^{-1}(K)
has a unique decomposition
f^{-1}(K)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}K_{i}
such that each
K_{i}
is a level-
n
extremal chain with
f(K_{i})=K
.

Moreover,
\deg(f|_{K_{i}}):=\#(f^{-1}(w)\cap K_{i})
is constant if
w\in K\cap F_{f}
is not a critical value.

Proof.

If
n=0
, the lemma holds since any level-
0
extremal chain is the closure of a Fatou domain.

Suppose that the lemma holds for level-
n
extremal chains with
n\geq 0
. Let
K
be a level-
(n+1)
extremal chain. By Lemma
3.1
, there exists a sequence of continua
\{E_{k}\}
such that each
E_{k}
consists of finitely many level-
n
extremal chains,
E_{k}\subset E_{k+1}
, and
K=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}}
.

(1) By induction, each
f(E_{k})
consists of finitely many level-
n
extremal chains. Then
f(K)=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}f(E_{k})}
is a level-
(n+1)
Fatou chain and is contained in a level-
(n+1)
extremal chain, denoted by
K^{\prime}
. Lemma
3.1
implies

K^{\prime}=\overline{\bigcup_{j\geq 0}E^{\prime}_{j}},

where each
E^{\prime}_{j}
consists of finitely many level-
n
extremal chains and
E^{\prime}_{j}\subset E^{\prime}_{j+1}
.
Thus, there exists an integer
j_{0}\geq 0
such that
f(E_{0})\subset E^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\geq j_{0}
.

Let
E_{j}^{\prime\prime}
be the component of
f^{-1}(E^{\prime}_{j})
containing
E_{0}
. By induction, the continuum
E_{j}^{\prime\prime}
consists of finitely many level-
n
extremal chains and thus forms a level-
(n+1)
Fatou chain. Since
K
is extremal, we have
{E}_{j}^{\prime\prime}\subset K
. Consequently,
E^{\prime}_{j}=f(E_{j}^{\prime\prime})\subset f(K)
for all
j\geq j_{0}
. It follows that
f(K)=K^{\prime}
is a level-
(n+1)
extremal chain.

(2) Let
m(k)
denote the number of components of
f^{-1}(E_{k})
. Then
m(k)
is decreasing. Thus, there exists an integer
k_{0}\geq 0
such that
m(k)=m
is constant for
k\geq k_{0}
. Let
E_{i,k}
,
1\leq i\leq m
, be the components of
f^{-1}(E_{k})
such that
E_{i,k}\subset E_{i,k+1}
. It follows that
d_{i}:=\deg(f|_{E_{i,k}})
is constant for
k\geq k_{0}
.

Set
K_{i}:=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq k_{0}}E_{i,k}}
. Then
f^{-1}(K)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}K_{i}
, and
f(K_{i})=K
. By induction, each
E_{i,k}
is the union of finitely many level-
n
extremal chains, so
K_{i}
is a level-
(n+1)
Fatou chain.

Let
K^{\prime}_{i}
denote the level-
(n+1)
extremal chain containing
K_{i}
. Then
f(K^{\prime}_{i})\supset f(K_{i})=K
. By statement (1), the continuum
f(K^{\prime}_{i})
is a level-
(n+1)
extremal chain. Thus,
f(K^{\prime}_{i})=f(K_{i})=K
, which implies
\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}K^{\prime}_{i}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}K_{i}
. Since
E_{i,k}
is disjoint from
E_{j,k}
if
i\neq j
, any level-
n
extremal chain in
K_{i}
is disjoint from that in
K_{j}
if
i\neq j
. Thus, we obtain
K^{\prime}_{i}=K_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m
.

Finally, let
w
be a point in
K\cap F_{f}
. Then
w\in E_{k}
for every sufficiently large integer
k
. Furthermore, if
w
is not a critical value, we have

\#(f^{-1}(w)\cap K_{i})=\#(f^{-1}(w)\cap E_{i,k})=\deg(f|_{E_{i,k}})=d_{i}.

Thus, the lemma is proved.
∎

According to Lemma
3.2
, every level-
n
extremal chain is eventually periodic. Moreover, for any level-
n
extremal chain
K\neq\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, its boundary and interior are contained in the Julia set and Fatou set of
f
, respectively. To see this, first note that
\partial K\subset J_{f}
. If the interior of
K
contains a point in the Julia set, then
f^{m}(K)=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
for a sufficiently large integer
m
. Since
f^{m}(K)
is a level-
n
extremal chain, we obtain
K=f^{m}(K)=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
by Definition
3.1
.

The following result provides a dynamical construction of periodic extremal chains.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
K
be a periodic level-
(n+1)
extremal chain of
f
with period
p\geq 1
, and let
E_{0}
be the union of all periodic level-
n
extremal chains in
K
. Then
E_{0}
is connected,
f^{p}(E_{0})=E_{0}
, and

K=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}},

where
E_{k}
is the component of
f^{-kp}(E_{0})
containing
E_{0}
.

Proof.

First, note that
f^{p}(E_{0})=E_{0}
since the image of a periodic level-
n
extremal chain is also a periodic level-
n
extremal chain. By Lemma
3.1
,
E_{0}
is contained in a continuum
E\subset K
that is the union of finitely many level-
n
extremal chains. Since
f^{p}(E_{0})=E_{0}
, it follows that
E_{0}\subset f^{kp}(E)
for every
k>0
. On the other hand, since each level-
n
extremal chain is eventually periodic, we obtain
f^{k_{0}p}(E)\subset E_{0}
for some integer
k_{0}\geq 0
. Therefore,
E_{0}=f^{k_{0}p}(E)
is connected. By Lemma
3.2
(2), each
E_{k}
is a level-
(n+1)
Fatou chain, and
E_{0}\subset E_{k}
contains Fatou domains. Thus,
\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}}\subset K
by the definition of extremal chains.

Conversely, for any level-
n
extremal chain
\sigma\subset K
, there exists a continuum
E^{\prime}
such that
E_{0}\cup\sigma\subset E^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}
is the union of finitely many level-
n
extremal chains. As above, we have
f^{k_{1}p}(E^{\prime})\subset E_{0}
for an integer
k_{1}>0
. Then
\sigma\subset E^{\prime}\subset E_{k_{1}}
, and therefore
K\subset\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}}
.
∎

By definition, every level-
n
extremal chain is contained in a level-
(n+1)
extremal chain. The following result shows that the growth of extremal chains will stop at a certain level.

Lemma 3.4
.

There exists an integer
N\geq 0
such that any level-
n
extremal chain of
f
is a level-
N
extremal chain for
n\geq N
.

Proof.

Let
k(n)
denote the number of periodic level-
n
extremal chains of
f
. Then
k(n)
is decreasing. Thus, there exists an integer
n_{0}
such that
k(n)
is constant for
n\geq n_{0}
.
This implies that two distinct periodic level-
n
extremal chains are disjoint for
n\geq n_{0}
.

For each periodic Fatou domain
U
of
f
with period
p\geq 1
, denote by
K_{n}(U)
the level-
n
extremal chain containing
U
. Then
f^{p}(K_{n}(U))=K_{n}(U)
, and
K_{n}(U)
is the unique periodic level-
n
extremal chain contained in
K_{n+1}(U)
for
n\geq n_{0}
. If
K_{n}(U)
is not a component of
f^{-p}(K_{n}(U))
, we have

\deg(f^{p}|_{K_{n+1}(U)})>\deg(f^{p}|_{K_{n}(U)})

by Lemmas
3.2
and
3.3
. On the other hand, since
\deg(f|_{K_{n+1}}(U))\leq\deg f
, there exists an integer
n(U)\geq n_{0}
such that
\deg(f^{p}|_{K_{n}(U)})
is constant for
n\geq n(U)
. Thus,
K_{n}(U)
must be a component of
f^{-p}(K_{n}(U))
for
n\geq n(U)
. It then follows from Lemma
3.3
that
K_{n+1}(U)=K_{n}(U)
for
n\geq n(U)
.

Let
N_{1}
be the maximum of
\{n(U)\}
for all periodic Fatou domains
U
of
f
. Then every periodic level-
n
extremal chain is a level-
N_{1}
extremal chain for
n\geq N_{1}
.

For any level-
N_{1}
extremal chain
K
, there exists an integer
q\geq 0
such that
f^{q}(K)
is a periodic level-
N_{1}
extremal chain. Let
K_{i}
denote the level-
(N_{1}+i)
extremal chain containing
K
for
i>0
. Then
f^{q}(K_{i})
is a periodic level-
(N_{1}+i)
extremal chain containing
f^{q}(K)
, and hence
f^{q}(K_{i})=f^{q}(K)
. Applying Lemma
3.2
(2) to
f^{q}
, we obtain that
K_{i}=K_{1}
for
i\geq 1
. Therefore, the lemma holds if we define
N:=N_{1}+1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

By Lemma
3.4
, there exists an integer
N\geq 0
such that any level-
n
extremal chain is a level-
N
extremal chain for every
n\geq N
. For any Fatou domain
U
of
f
, let
K(U)
denote the level-
N
extremal chain containing
U
. If a Fatou chain
K
intersects
K(U)
, then
K\cup K(U)
is contained in an extremal chain of level
N+1
. This implies
K\subset K(U)
. Thus,
K(U)
is a maximal Fatou chain.
By Lemma
3.2
, the image and components of the pre-image of a maximal Fatou chain are still maximal Fatou chains.
∎

4.
Decompositions of rational maps

In this section, we establish the
cluster-exact decomposition
(Theorem
4.2
) for marked rational maps. This decomposition theorem corresponds to Theorem
1.7
(1) and (2), and the remaining part of Theorem
1.7
follows from Theorem
1.6
, which
will be proved in the next section.

In Section 4.1, we study the combinatorics of planar continua and domains by their
branched numbers
. In Section 4.2, we characterize the dynamics of stable sets by proving Theorem
1.5
. In Section 4.3, we obtain an important result, called the
exact decomposition
, which serves as a key step toward the cluster-exact decomposition. Finally, we complete the proof of the cluster-exact decomposition in Section 4.4.

4.1.
Branched numbers

Let
P\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a finite marked set, and let
E\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a connected open or closed set.
Recall that
E
is simple-type (rel
P
) if there exists a simply connected domain
D\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
E\subset D
and
\#(D\cap P)\leq 1
; or annular-type if
E
is not simple-type and there exists an annulus
A\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P
such that
E\subset A
; or complex-type otherwise.

The
branched number
of
E
(rel
P
) is defined by

b(E):=\#(E\cap P)+\kappa(E),

where
\kappa(E)
is the number of components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
that intersect
P
. By definition,
E
is complex-type if and only if
b(E)\geq 3
, and
b(E)=2
if
E
is annular-type.

Let
K_{0}\subset K
be continua in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Recall that
K_{0}
is a skeleton of
K
(rel
P
) if
K_{0}\cap P=K\cap P
and any two points of
P
in distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K
are contained in distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K_{0}
. It is easy to verify that

(4.1)

\text{$K_{0}$ is a skeleton of $K$ $\Longleftrightarrow$ $b(K_{0})=b(K)$ and $\#(K_{0}\cap P)=\#(K\cap P)$}.

Lemma 4.1
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

For any continuum
E\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, there exists a domain
U\supset E
such that
b(U)=b(E)
;

(2)

For any domain
U\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, there exists a continuum
E\subset U
such that
b(U)=b(E)
.

Proof.

(1) Let
V_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, be the components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
containing points of
P
. Then there exists a full continuum
K_{i}\subset V_{i}
such that
P\cap K_{i}=P\cap V_{i}
. Set
U=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}K_{i}
. Then
U\supset E
is a domain, and
b(U)=b(E)
.

(2) Let
E_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq m
, be the components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus U
that intersect
P
. Then there exist disks
V_{j}\supset E_{j}
with pairwise disjoint closures such that
\partial V_{j}\subset U
and
P\cap E_{j}=P\cap V_{j}
. Since
U
is a domain, there exists a graph
E\subset U
containing
P\cap U
and all
\partial V_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
. It follows that
b(U)=b(E)
.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose that
V\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is a complex-type domain and
{\mathcal{K}}\subset V
is a compact set. Let
\mathscr{E}
be the collection of all complex-type components of either
V\setminus{\mathcal{K}}
or
{\mathcal{K}}
. Then

\sum_{E\in\mathscr{E}}(b(E)-2)=b(V)-2.

Proof.

There exist at most
\#P
elements of
\mathscr{E}
intersecting
P
and at most
\#P-2
elements disjoint from
P
since each divides
P
into at least three parts. Thus,
\mathscr{E}
is a finite collection.

In order to prove the equality, define a graph
T
as follows. Let
\mathscr{E}_{1}
be the collection of all components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus V
intersecting
P
. There exists a bijection
v
from
\mathscr{E}_{1}\cup\mathscr{E}
onto the set of vertices of
T
. Two vertices
v(E_{1})
and
v(E_{2})
of
T
are connected by an edge if and only if
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are
adjacent
, i.e., no elements of
\mathscr{E}
separate
E_{1}
from
E_{2}
. Then
T
is a tree.

Note that for any element
E\in\mathscr{E}_{1}\cup\mathscr{E}
, the number of edges of
T
connecting to the vertex
v(E)
is exactly
\kappa(E)
, i.e., the number of components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
intersecting
P
. Thus,
v(E)
is an endpoint of
T
precisely if
\kappa(E)=1
. In particular,
v(E)
is an endpoint if
E\in\mathscr{E}_{1}
.

Let
k_{0}\geq 0
denote the number of elements of
\mathscr{E}
with
\kappa(E)=1
. Then
T
has exactly
\kappa(V)+k_{0}
endpoints. Since
T
is a tree, we have

\kappa(V)+k_{0}-2=\sum(\kappa(E)-2),

where the summation is taken over all elements of
\mathscr{E}
with
\kappa(E)\geq 2
.
It follows immediately that

\kappa(V)-2=\sum(\kappa(E)-2),

where the summation is taken over all elements of
\mathscr{E}
. Thus, the lemma holds if
V\cap P=\emptyset
.

In the general case, without loss of generality, we assume that all marked points in
{\mathcal{K}}
are interior points of
{\mathcal{K}}
. Then there exists a small number
r>0
such that
\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}(z,3r)\subset V

for each point
z\in P\cap V
, and
\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}(z,3r)\subset{\mathcal{K}}
for
z\in P\cap{\mathcal{K}}
.

Set
V^{\prime}:=V\setminus\bigcup_{z\in P\cap V}\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}(z,r)}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{K}}\setminus\bigcup_{z\in P\cap{\mathcal{K}}}\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}(z,2r)
. Let
\mathscr{E}^{\prime}
be the collection of all complex-type components of either
V^{\prime}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
or
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
. It follows that

•

\sum_{E^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}^{\prime}}(b(E^{\prime})-2)=b(V^{\prime})-2
since
V^{\prime}\cap P=\emptyset
; and

•

b(V)=b(V^{\prime})
and each
E^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}^{\prime}
is contained in a unique element
E\in\mathscr{E}
with
b(E^{\prime})=b(E)
.

Therefore, we have
\sum_{E\in\mathscr{E}}(b(E)-2)=b(V)-2
. The lemma is proved.
∎

Corollary 4.1
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

Let
K_{0}\subset K
be continua in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Then
b(K_{0})\leq b(K)
.

(2)

Let
\{K_{n}\}
be a sequence of continua in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
K_{n}\subset K_{n+1}
for all
n\geq 0
. Then there exists
N\geq 0
such that
b(K_{n})=b(K_{N})
, and
K_{N}
is a skeleton of
K_{n}
for every
n\geq N
.

(3)

Let
\{K_{n}\}
be a sequence of continua in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
K_{n+1}\subset K_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
, and set
K:=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}K_{n}
. Then
b(K)=b(K_{n})
for sufficiently large
n
.

Proof.

(1) By Lemma
4.1
, there exists a domain
U\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
b(U)=b(K)
. It follows from Lemma
4.2
that
b(K_{0})\leq b(U)=b(K)
.

(2) Note that the numbers
b(K_{n})
and
\#(K_{n}\cap P)
are increasing and bounded above by
\#P
. Thus, there exists an integer
N\geq 0
such that both
b(K_{n})
and
\#(K_{n}\cap P)
are constant for every
n\geq N
. By relation (
4.1
),
K_{N}
is a skeleton of
K_{n}
for every
n\geq N
.

(3) By statement (1), the number
b(K_{n})
is decreasing. Thus,
b(K_{n})
becomes a constant
b\geq 1
for sufficiently large
n
. Since
K
is a connected closed set, we have
b(K)\leq b
. On the other hand, by Lemma
4.1
, there exists a domain
U\supset K
such that
b(U)=b(K)
. Since
K_{n}\subset U
for every sufficiently large integer
n
, it follows from Lemma
4.2
that
b(K)=b(U)\geq b(K_{n})=b
.
∎

Now, let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map. Since
f(P)\subset P
, we immediately obtain the following
pullback principle
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map. Suppose that
E\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is a connected open or closed set. If
E
is simple-type, then each component of
f^{-1}(E)
is simple-type. If
E
is annular-type, then each component of
f^{-1}(E)
is either annular-type or simple-type.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map. Let
E\subset E^{\prime}
be connected open or closed sets in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
with
b(E)=b(E^{\prime})
. Let
E_{1}^{\prime}
be a component of
f^{-1}(E^{\prime})
. Then
E_{1}:=E_{1}^{\prime}\cap f^{-1}(E)
is connected. Moreover, if
E
is a skeleton of
E^{\prime}
, then
E_{1}
is a skeleton of
E^{\prime}_{1}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.1
, there exist a domain
V\supset E^{\prime}
and a compact connected set
K\subset E
such that
b(V)=b(K)
. Let
V_{1}
be the component of
f^{-1}(V)
containing
E_{1}^{\prime}
.

According to Lemma
4.2
, each component
U
of
V\setminus K
is either simple-type or annular-type, and
\partial U
has exactly one component contained in
K
. Consequently, any component of
f^{-1}(U)
is either simple-type or annular-type by Lemma
4.3
, and its boundary has exactly one component contained in
f^{-1}(K)
. This implies that
V_{1}
contains exactly one component
K_{1}
of
f^{-1}(K)
and
b(V_{1})=b(K_{1})
. Thus, the former part of the lemma holds.

Furthermore, if
E
is a skeleton of
E^{\prime}
, then
E\cap P=E^{\prime}\cap P
, which implies
E_{1}\cap P=E_{1}^{\prime}\cap P
. Note also that
b(K_{1})\leq b(E_{1})\leq b(E_{1}^{\prime})\leq b(V_{1})=b(K_{1})
. Thus,
E_{1}
is a skeleton of
E_{1}^{\prime}
by (
4.1
).
∎

4.2.
Stable sets

Recall that a stable set
{\mathcal{K}}
of a rational map
f
is a non-empty and finite disjoint union of continua such that
f({\mathcal{K}})\subset{\mathcal{K}}
and each component of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}})
is either a component of
{\mathcal{K}}
or disjoint from
{\mathcal{K}}
. By definition, each component of
{\mathcal{K}}
is eventually periodic, and
\partial{\mathcal{K}}
is also a stable set of
f
provided that
{\mathcal{K}}\not=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
.

Throughout this subsection, let
f
be a given PCF rational map.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
K\subsetneq\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a connected stable set of
f
. Then
\partial K\subset J_{f}
.

Proof.

Choose a domain
W\supset K
such that
b(K)=b(W)
. Then each component of
f^{-1}(W)
contains exactly one component of
f^{-1}(K)
by Lemma
4.4
. In particular, the component
W_{1}
of
f^{-1}(W)
containing
K
is disjoint from
f^{-1}(K)\setminus K
.

Suppose, to the contrary, that
\partial K\cap F_{f}\neq\emptyset
. Since
K
is a component of
f^{-1}(K)
, we have
f(\partial K)=\partial K
. Thus, there exists a super-attracting periodic point
a\in\partial K
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
f(a)=a
. Let
U
be the Fatou domain containing
a
. Then there exists a disk
\Delta\subset U
such that it is a round disk in the Böttcher coordinate and
\Delta\subset W
. This implies that if
z\in K\cap\Delta
, then
f^{-1}(z)\cap U\subset K
.

Let
\gamma_{t}\subset\Delta
be the Jordan curve corresponding to the circle with radius
t\in(0,1)
in the Böttcher coordinate. Since
K
is connected and
a\in K
, there exists a point
t_{0}\in(0,1)
such that
\gamma_{t_{0}}\cap K\neq\emptyset
and
\gamma_{t_{0}}\subset\Delta
. It follows that
\gamma_{t}\cap K\neq\emptyset
for all
t\in(0,t_{0})
since
\gamma_{t}
separates
\gamma_{t_{0}}
from
a
. In particular, given any
t\in(0,t_{0})
,
f^{k}(\gamma_{t})\cap K\neq\emptyset
for all
k\geq 1
.

Pick a point
z_{k}\in f^{k}(\gamma_{t})\cap K
. Then
f^{-k}(z_{k})\cap U\subset\gamma_{t}\cap K
. Since
\gamma_{t}\cap K
is compact and
\bigcup_{k\geq 1}(f^{-k}(z_{k})\cap U)
is dense in
\gamma_{t}
, we obtain
\gamma_{t}\subset K
for all
t\in(0,t_{0})
, a contradiction.
∎

The following lemma offers a way to obtain stable sets.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\{V_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
be a sequence of domains in
\overline{\mathbb{C}}
such that
V_{n+1}\subset V_{n}
and
f:V_{n+1}\to V_{n}
is proper. If, for any
n\geq 0
, there exists an integer
m>n
such that
\overline{V_{m}}\subset V_{n}
, then
K=\bigcap_{n>0}V_{n}
is a stable set of
f
when
K
is not a singleton.

Proof.

It follows from the known condition that
K
is a component of
f^{-1}(K)
. Hence,
K
is a stable set unless it is a singleton.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Let
\widehat{K}
be the union of
K
and all components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K
disjoint from
P_{f}
. If
\widehat{K}=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, then
f^{-1}(K)=K
, and thus
\widehat{K}=K=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, which contradicts the condition that
K\neq\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
.

Now, assume
\widehat{K}\not=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Let
\mathscr{D}
denote the collection of components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\widehat{K}
. Define a self-map
f_{*}
on
\mathscr{D}
as follows. If
D\in\mathscr{D}
is disjoint from
f^{-1}(K)
, then
f(D)\in\mathscr{D}
and we set
f_{*}(D):=f(D)
. Otherwise, let
D^{\prime}
be the component of
D\setminus f^{-1}(K)
such that
\partial D^{\prime}\supset\partial D
. In this case,
f(D^{\prime})
is an element of
\mathscr{D}
, and we define
f_{*}(D):=f(D^{\prime})
.

Since
\mathscr{D}
is a finite collection, each of its elements is eventually periodic under
f_{*}
. Assume that
D_{i},0\leq i<p,
forms a cycle in
\mathscr{D}
with
D_{i}=f_{*}^{i}(D_{0})
and
D_{0}=f_{*}^{p}(D_{0})
. Since
f
is expanding in a neighborhood of
J_{f}
under the orbifold metric, and
\partial K\subset J_{f}
by Lemma
4.5
, for each
0\leq i<p
, there exists an annulus
A_{D_{i}}=A_{i}\subset D_{i}\setminus P_{f}
with
\partial D_{i}\subset\partial A_{i}
, such that
\overline{A_{i}^{1}}\subset A_{i}\cup\partial D_{i}
, where
A_{i}^{1}
is the component of
f^{-1}(A_{i+1})
(with
A_{p}=A_{0}
) such that
\partial A_{i}^{1}\supset\partial D_{i}
. Applying a similar argument, we can assign an annulus
A_{D}
to every periodic element
D\in\mathscr{D}
.

If
D^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}
is not
f_{*}
-periodic but
f_{*}(D^{\prime})=D
is periodic, we assign an annulus
A_{D^{\prime}}\subset D^{\prime}\setminus P_{f}
with
\partial D^{\prime}\subset\partial A_{D^{\prime}}
, such that
\overline{A_{D}^{1}}\subset A_{D^{\prime}}\cup\partial D^{\prime}
, where
A_{D}^{1}
is the component of
f^{-1}(A_{D})
with
\partial D^{\prime}\subset A_{D}^{1}
. Repeating this process, we assign an annulus
A_{D}
to each element
D\in\mathscr{D}
.

Let
V
be the union of
\widehat{K}
and
A_{D}
for all
D\in\mathscr{D}
. Then
V
is a finitely connected domain with
V\cap P_{f}=K\cap P_{f}
. Moreover, the component
U
of
f^{-1}(V)
containing
K
is compactly contained in
V
by the construction of
A_{D}
. Since
K
is not a singleton, it follows from
[
37
, Lemma 18.8]
that
\deg f|_{K}\geq 2
. Thus,
f:U\to V
is a rational-like map (see
[
9
, Definition 4]
). Then the theorem follows directly from
[
9
, Theorem 5.2]
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\{{\mathcal{K}}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
be a sequence of stable sets of
f
such that
{\mathcal{K}}_{n+1}\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
. Then there exists an integer
N\geq 0
such that
{\mathcal{K}}_{n}={\mathcal{K}}_{N}
for every
n\geq N
.

Proof.

By the pullback principle (Lemma
4.3
), we can split each stable set
{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
into two stable sets,
{\mathcal{K}}^{0}_{n}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}
, such that each periodic component of
{\mathcal{K}}^{0}_{n}
is either simple-type or annular-type, and each periodic component of
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}
is complex-type. Then
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n+1}\subset{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}
by Corollary
4.1
(1).

We first assume that the components of
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}
are all complex-type for every
n\geq 0
. The branched number of
{\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\prime}
is defined by

b({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n})=\sum(b(K)-2)+2,

where the summation is taken over all components of
{\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\prime}
. Then
b({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n+1})\leq b({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n})
by Lemma
4.2
. Thus, there exists an integer
n_{1}\geq 0
such that
b({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n})
is constant for
n\geq n_{1}
. This implies that, for
n\geq n_{1}
, each component of
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}
contains at least one component of
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n+1}
.

Let
k(n)
be the number of components of
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}
for
n\geq n_{1}
. As argued above,
k(n)
is increasing. However, Lemma
4.2
implies
k(n)\leq\#P_{f}-2
. Thus, there exists an integer
n_{2}\geq n_{1}
such that
k(n)
is constant for
n\geq n_{2}
. Consequently, each component
K_{n}
of
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}
contains exactly one component
K_{n+1}
of
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n+1}
for
n\geq n_{2}
. Since
b({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n})
is constant for
n\geq n_{2}
, it follows that
b(K_{n})=b(K_{n+1})
.

To complete the proof, we need to show that for each periodic component
K_{n}
of
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}
, it holds that
K_{n+1}=K_{n}
for sufficiently large
n>n_{2}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
f(K_{n})=K_{n}
. Then
f(K_{n+1})=K_{n+1}
.

By Theorem
1.5
and Lemma
4.4
, we know that
\bigcup_{k\geq 0}(f|_{K_{n}})^{-k}(\partial K_{n+1})=\partial K_{n+1}
is dense in
\partial K_{n}
. Hence,
\partial K_{n+1}=\partial K_{n}
. If
K_{n+1}\neq K_{n}
, it implies that
K_{n}\setminus K_{n+1}\subset F_{f}
. Since
f
has at most
2\deg f-2
cycles of Fatou domains, the inequality
K_{n+1}\neq K_{n}
can occur only finitely many times. Hence, there exists an integer
n_{3}\geq n_{2}
such that
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}={\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n_{3}}
for
n\geq n_{3}
.

In general, let
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
be the union of all complex-type components of
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}
. Then
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
is also a stable set of
f
, and
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{n+1}\subset{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
. Based on the previous discussion, we can find an integer
N_{0}\geq 0
such that
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{n}={\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{N_{0}}
for every
n\geq N_{0}
.

Note that
{\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
contains all periodic components in
{\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\prime}
, which means that any component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\prime}
is eventually iterated into
{\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
. Thus, for any
m\geq N_{0}
and any component
K
of
{\mathcal{K}}_{m}^{\prime}
, either
K
is a component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\prime}
for every
n\geq m
, or
K\cap{\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\prime}=\emptyset
for sufficiently large
n
.
Consequently, the number
l(n)
of components of
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}
(for
n\geq N_{0}
) is decreasing. Therefore, there exists an integer
N\geq N_{0}
such that
l(n)=l(N)
for every
n\geq N
. This implies
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}={\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{N}
for
n\geq N
.

Since
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{n}={\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{N}
for
n\geq N
, it follows that
{\mathcal{K}}^{0}_{n+1}\subset{\mathcal{K}}^{0}_{n}
for
n\geq N
. For any periodic component
K
of
{\mathcal{K}}^{0}_{n}
, the renormalization of
f^{p}
on
K
is conformally conjugate to either
z\mapsto z^{d}
or
z\mapsto 1/z^{d}
with
d\geq 2
. Thus,
K
is either a Jordan curve or the closure of a periodic Fatou domain of
f
. In the former case, the cycle of
K
contains no other stable set of
f
except itself. In the latter case, the cycle of
\partial K
is the unique stable set of
f
properly contained in the cycle of
K
. Thus, we have
{\mathcal{K}}^{0}_{n+1}={\mathcal{K}}^{0}_{n}
for sufficiently large
n\geq N
.
∎

4.3.
Exact decomposition

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map.
Suppose that
{\mathcal{K}}
is a stable set of
f
. Let
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
be the union of all complex-type components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}
and
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}})
, respectively. By the pullback principle (Lemma
4.3
), it holds that
f({\mathcal{V}}_{1})\subset{\mathcal{V}}
.

We say
{\mathcal{K}}
induces an
exact decomposition
of
(f,P)
if either
{\mathcal{V}}=\emptyset
, or
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
is an exact sub-system of
(f,P)
, i.e., each component of
{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
is a full continuum disjoint from
P
; see Definition
1.3
.

The following result serves as a key step toward the cluster-exact decomposition.
By an
exceptional stable set
, we mean a stable set containing the Julia set.

Theorem 4.1

(Exact decomposition)
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map, and let
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
be a non-exceptional stable set of
f
. Then there exists a non-exceptional stable set
{\mathcal{K}}\supset{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
that induces an exact decomposition of
(f,P)
. Moreover, if each component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
intersects or separates
P

(
as defined before Lemma
2.11
)
, then so does each component of
{\mathcal{K}}
.

The condition that each component of
\mathcal{K}_{0}
intersects or separates
P
is equivalent to
\kappa(U)=\#\textup{Comp}(\partial U)
for any component
U
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{0}
. In particular, annular-type components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\mathcal{K}_{0}
are annuli. Recall that
\kappa(U)
denotes the number of components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus U
intersecting
P
, and
{\rm Comp}(\cdot)
denotes the collection of all components of the corresponding set.

We can always choose an
f
-invariant and finite set
P_{1}\supset P
such that
P_{1}\setminus P\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
and each component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
intersects or separates points of
P_{1}
. Immediately, any complex-type domain rel
P
is still complex-type rel
P_{1}
. By definition, if
{\mathcal{K}}
induces an exact decomposition of
(f,P_{1})
, it also induces an exact decomposition of
(f,P)
.
Thus, it suffices to prove the theorem for
(f,P_{1})
. Therefore, we can assume that each component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
intersects or separates
P
.

For any stable set
{\mathcal{B}}
of
f
, denote by
{\mathcal{B}}^{n}
the union of all components of
f^{-n}({\mathcal{B}})
that intersect or separate
P
. By Lemma
4.3
, each
{\mathcal{B}}^{n}
is a stable set of
f
, and
{\mathcal{B}}^{n}\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{n+1}
.

For each
n\geq 0
, let
{\mathcal{U}}_{n}
be the union of all complex-type components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}^{n}_{0}
. It follows immediately that
{\mathcal{U}}_{n+1}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{n}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Assume that
{\mathcal{U}}_{n}\neq\emptyset
for all
n\geq 0
. Then there exists a positive integer
N_{0}
such that any component
U_{N_{0}}
of
{\mathcal{U}}_{N_{0}}
contains a unique component
U_{n}
of
{\mathcal{U}}_{n}
for every
n\geq N_{0}
. Moreover, it holds that

\#(U_{n}\cap P)=\#(U_{N_{0}}\cap P)\quad\text{and}\quad\#{\rm Comp}(\partial U_{n})=\#{\rm Comp}(\partial U_{N_{0}}).

Proof.

Let
k(n)
denote the number of complex-type components of
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}^{n}
. Then
k(n)
is increasing, and
k(n)\leq\#P-2

by Lemma
4.2
. Thus, there exists an integer
n_{0}
such that
k(n)=k(n_{0})
for all
n\geq n_{0}
. Therefore,
{\mathcal{U}}_{n_{0}}
contains no complex-type components of
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}^{n}
for all
n>n_{0}
.

Fix a component
U_{n}
of
{\mathcal{U}}_{n}
with
n\geq n_{0}
. Since
U_{n}
contains no complex-type components of
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}^{m}
for
m>n
, it follows from Lemma
4.2
that

\sum(b(U)-2)=b(U_{n})-2>1,

where the summation is taken over all components of
{\mathcal{U}}_{m}
contained in
U_{n}
. Thus,
U_{n}
contains at least one component of
{\mathcal{U}}_{m}
. Consequently, the number
v(n)
of components of
{\mathcal{U}}_{n}
is increasing for
n\geq n_{0}
.

Note that
\#({\mathcal{U}}_{n}\cap P)
is decreasing. Then there exists an integer
n_{1}\geq n_{0}
such that both
v(n)
and
\#({\mathcal{U}}_{n}\cap P)
are constant for
n\geq n_{1}
. Thus, each component
U_{n_{1}}
of
{\mathcal{U}}_{n_{1}}
contains a unique component
U_{n}
of
{\mathcal{U}}_{n}
for every
n>n_{1}
such that

\#(U_{n}\cap P)=\#(U_{n_{1}}\cap P)
. Since
b(U_{n})
is decreasing, there exists an integer
N_{0}>n_{1}
such that
b(U_{n})=b(U_{N_{0}})
for all
n\geq N_{0}
.

Finally, since each component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}^{n}
intersects or separates
P
, all complementary components of
U_{n}
intersect
P
, i.e.,
\#{\rm Comp}(\partial U_{n})=\kappa(U_{n})
. It follows that
\#{\rm Comp}(\partial U_{n})=b(U_{n})-\#(U_{n}\cap P)
is constant for
n\geq N_{0}
by the choice of
N_{0}
.
∎

According to Lemma
4.8
, any component
U_{N_{0}}
of
{\mathcal{U}}_{N_{0}}
and any component
\lambda_{N_{0}}
of
\partial U_{N_{0}}
determine a sequence of pairs
(U_{n},\lambda_{n})
for
n\geq N_{0}
, where
U_{n}
is the component of
{\mathcal{U}}_{n}
contained in
U_{N_{0}}
, and
\lambda_{n}
is the component of
\partial U_{n}
such that either
\lambda_{n+1}=\lambda_{n}
, or
\lambda_{n+1}
is disjoint from
\lambda_{n}
but separates
\lambda_{n}
from
U_{n+1}
.

Since
{\mathcal{U}}_{N_{0}}
has finitely many components, all of which are finitely connected, there exists an integer
N\geq N_{0}
such that, for any determined sequence
\{(U_{n},\lambda_{n}),n\geq N\}
, exactly one of the following two cases occurs:

•

\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{N}
for all
n\geq N
;

•

for any
n\geq N
, there exists an integer
m>n
such that
\lambda_{m}
is disjoint from
\lambda_{n}
and separates
\lambda_{n}
from
U_{m}
.

We call
\lambda_{N}
an
exact boundary component
of
U_{N}
in the first case.

From now on, write
{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{U}}_{N}
, and denote by
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
the union of all complex-type components of
f^{-n}({\mathcal{V}})
. Then
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
coincides with the union of all complex-type components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus f^{-n}({\mathcal{K}}^{N}_{0})
. This implies
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{N+n}
.

Note that any component of
f^{-n}({\mathcal{K}}^{N}_{0})\setminus{\mathcal{K}}^{N+n}_{0}
neither intersects nor separates
P
, while each component of
\partial{\mathcal{U}}_{N+n}
intersects or separates
P
. It follows that
{\mathcal{U}}_{N+n}\setminus{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
consists of pairwise disjoint full continua disjoint from
P
.
Therefore,

(1)

each component
V=U_{N}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
contains a unique component
V_{n}
of
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
such that
U_{N+n}\setminus V_{n}
consists of pairwise disjoint full continua that avoid
P
;

(2)

for any boundary component
\lambda
of
V
, there exists a unique boundary component
\lambda_{n}
of
V_{n}

parallel to

\lambda
in the sense that either
\lambda_{n}=\lambda
or
\lambda_{n}
separates
\lambda
from
V_{n}
.

We say
V
is an
exact
(resp.,
renormalizable
) component of
{\mathcal{V}}
if all components of
\partial V
are exact (resp., non-exact) boundary components of
V
; see Figure
8
(where the pants represent
V
, and the domains colored yellow correspond to
V_{1}
).

renormalizable
exact
mixing

Figure 8.
Classification of components of a sub-system.

If
V=U_{N}
is exact, then
V=U_{N+1}
. By this point and statement (1) above, it follows that
V\setminus V_{1}
consists of full continua disjoint from
P
. This immediately implies the following:

Proposition 4.1
.

The stable set
{\mathcal{K}}^{N}_{0}
induces an exact decomposition of
(f,P)
if every component of
{\mathcal{V}}
is exact.

Let
\mathscr{V}
be the collection of all components of
{\mathcal{V}}
. Then
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
induces a self-map
f_{\#}
on
\mathscr{V}
, defined by
f_{\#}(V):=f(V_{1})
, where
V_{1}
is the unique component of
{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
contained in
V
. Since
\mathscr{V}
is a finite collection, each component of
{\mathcal{V}}
is eventually
f_{\#}
-periodic.

The map
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
also induces a self-map
f_{*}
on the collection
\partial\mathscr{V}
of the boundary components of
V
for all
V\in\mathscr{V}
. This self-map is defined by
f_{*}(\lambda):=f(\lambda_{1})
, where
\lambda_{1}
is the unique boundary component of
V_{1}
parallel to
\lambda
. Since
\partial\mathscr{V}
is a finite collection, its elements are eventually
f_{*}
-periodic.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
V
be a component of
{\mathcal{V}}
, and let
\lambda
be a component of
\partial V
.
Then
\lambda
is an exact boundary component of
V
if and only if
f_{*}(\lambda)
is an exact boundary component of
f_{\#}(V)
.
Consequently, if
V
is non-exact, then
f_{\#}(V)
is also non-exact.

Proof.

For each
n\geq 0
, denote by
V_{n}
the unique component of
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
contained in
V
, and by
\lambda_{n}
the unique boundary component of
\partial V_{n}
parallel to
\lambda
.
Set
W=f_{\#}(V)
and
\eta=f_{*}(\lambda)
. Similarly, we define
W_{n}
and
\eta_{n}
for
n\geq 0
. By definition, it holds that
f(V_{1})=W
and
f(\lambda_{1})=\eta
.

If
\lambda
is exact, then
\lambda_{n+1}=\lambda
and
\eta=f(\lambda_{n+1})=\eta_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
. Thus,
\eta
is exact.

If
\lambda
is non-exact, there exists an
n\geq 0
such that
\lambda_{n+1}\cap\lambda_{1}=\emptyset
.
Choose an annulus
A\subset W\setminus P
that is bounded by
\eta
and a Jordan curve in
W_{n}
. Since
b(W_{n})=b(W)
, it follows from Lemma
4.4
that
f^{-1}(W_{n})\cap V_{1}=V_{n+1}
.

Let
A_{1}\subset V_{1}
be the component of
f^{-1}(A)
with
\lambda_{1}\subset\partial A_{1}
. Then
A_{1}
is an annulus disjoint from
P
and the boundary component of
A_{1}
other than
\lambda_{1}
is contained in
V_{n+1}
. Since
\lambda_{n+1}\cap\lambda_{1}=\emptyset
, we have
\lambda_{n+1}\subset A_{1}
. It follows that
A
contains a boundary component of
W_{n}
parallel to
\eta
, which can only be
\eta_{n}
. Thus,
\eta
is non-exact
by the choice of
N
.
∎

According to Proposition
4.1
, if all components of
{\mathcal{V}}
are exact, then Theorem
4.1
holds by defining
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}^{N}_{0}
. If the components of
{\mathcal{V}}
are either exact or renormalizable, denote by
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
the union of all renormalizable components of
{\mathcal{V}}
, and by
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}^{\prime}
the union of all components of
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
within
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
. By Proposition
4.2
, the map
f_{\#}
is invariant on both the collection of all renormalizable components and the collection of all exact components of
{\mathcal{V}}
. Thus,
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\setminus{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{V}}\setminus{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
is an exact sub-system, and
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}:=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}{\mathcal{V}}_{n}^{\prime}
is a stable set of
f
disjoint from
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
by Lemma
4.6
. Therefore, Theorem
4.1
holds if we set
{\mathcal{K}}:={\mathcal{K}}^{N}_{0}\cup{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
.

However,
{\mathcal{V}}
might contain components that are neither exact nor renormalizable; see Figure
8
. In this case, we need to combine these components to obtain a renormalization domain.

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose that
V
is an
f_{\#}
-periodic and non-exact component of
{\mathcal{V}}
. Then there exists a non-exceptional stable set
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
of
f
, whose components are all complex-type, such that

\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\overline{V_{n}}\subset{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},

where
V_{n}
denotes the component of
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
contained in
V
. Moreover, each component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
is either contained in
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
or disjoint from
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
.

We can quickly deduce Theorem
4.1
from Lemma
4.9
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We adhere to the notations mentioned above.
If
{\mathcal{V}}=\emptyset
or
{\mathcal{V}}
contains only exact components, the theorem holds by taking
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}^{N}_{0}
, according to Proposition
4.1
. Otherwise,
{\mathcal{V}}
has an
f_{\#}
-periodic and non-exact component
V
by Proposition
4.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
be the non-exceptional stable set obtained in Lemma
4.9
. Then there exists a sufficiently large integer
N^{\prime}
such that
({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})^{N^{\prime}+1}\setminus({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})^{N^{\prime}}
is disjoint from
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
.

Set
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}_{0}\cup({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})^{N^{\prime}}
. It is a non-exceptional stable set of
f
, and its components all intersect or separate
P
. Since
\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\overline{V_{n}}
is a complex-type continuum (by Corollary
4.1
(3)) not contained in
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
, it follows from Lemma
4.2
that

b({\mathcal{K}}_{0}):=\sum(b(K)-2)+2<b({\mathcal{K}}_{1}):=\sum(b(K_{1})-2)+2,

where the first and second summations are taken over all complex-type components of
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
, respectively.

If
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}^{N_{1}}
induces an exact decomposition of
(f,P)
for an integer
N_{1}
, the theorem holds by taking
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}_{1}^{N_{1}}
. Otherwise, we can repeat the argument above by replacing
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
with
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and obtain a non-exceptional stable set
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}\supset{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
such that
b({\mathcal{K}}_{2})>b({\mathcal{K}}_{1})
and each component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
intersects or separates
P
.

By iterating this process, we obtain an increasing sequence of non-exceptional stable sets
\{{\mathcal{K}}_{n}\}
such that
b({\mathcal{K}}_{n+1})>b({\mathcal{K}}_{n})
. Since
b({\mathcal{K}}_{n})\leq\#P
by Lemma
4.2
, this process must stop after a finite number of steps. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.9
.

According to Proposition
4.2
, there exists an
f_{*}
-periodic and non-exact boundary component
\lambda
of
V
. Its period is denoted by
p
.

For each
0\leq i<p
, set
V_{i,0}:=f_{\#}^{i}(V)
and
\lambda_{i}:=f_{*}^{i}(\lambda)
. Then
f^{p}_{\#}(V_{i,0})=V_{i,0}
, and each
\lambda_{i}
is a non-exact boundary component of
V_{i,0}
by Proposition
4.2
. For every
n\geq 0
, denote by
V_{i,n}
the unique complex-type component of
f^{-np}(V_{i,0})
contained in
V_{i,0}
. Equivalently,
V_{i,n}
is the component of
{\mathcal{V}}_{np}
contained in
V_{i,0}
.

Let
D_{i,0}
be the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\lambda_{i}
containing
V_{i,0}
. Then
f^{-p}(D_{i,0})
has a unique component
D_{i,1}
containing
V_{i,1}
, and
\overline{{D}_{i,1}}\subset D_{i,0}
since
\lambda_{i}
is non-exact. Inductively, for each
n\geq 1
,
f^{-p}(D_{i,n})
has a component
D_{i,n+1}
containing
V_{i,n+1}
, and
\overline{D_{i,n+1}}\subset D_{i,n}
. By Corollary
4.1
,

K_{i}:=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}D_{i,n}

is a complex-type continuum. Moreover, it is a stable set of
f^{p}
by Lemma
4.6
, and
K_{i}\not\supset J_{f}
since
\lambda_{i}
is disjoint from
\overline{D_{i,k}}
for a sufficiently large integer
k
. Thus,
\partial K_{i}\subset J_{f}
by Lemma
4.5
.

Let
r\in[1,p]
be the smallest integer such that
K_{0}=K_{r}
. From the above construction, we obtain that
K_{i+1}=f(K_{i})
and
K_{i+r}=K_{i}
for every
i\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}
. Then each of
K_{0},\ldots,K_{r-1}
is a stable set of
f^{r}
, and
r
is a factor of
p
. Moreover,
K_{0},\ldots,K_{r-1}
are pairwise distinct. In order to obtain a stable set of
f
, we need to consider the intersections of
K_{i}
with
K_{j}
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose
K_{i}\cap K_{j}\neq\emptyset
for distinct
i,j\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}
. Then

(1)

\lambda_{j}\subset D_{i,0}
and
\lambda_{i}\subset D_{j,0}
;

(2)

V_{i,n}\cup V_{j,n}\subset D_{i,n}\cap D_{j,n}
for all
n\geq 0
; and

(3)

if
K_{\ell}
intersects
K_{i}
for some
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}
, then
K_{\ell}
also intersects
K_{j}
.

Proof.

We first claim that
D_{i,n}\nsubseteq D_{j,0}
for any
n\geq 0
. Assume, by contradiction, that
D_{i,m}\subseteq D_{j,0}
for some
m\geq 0
. Then, for all
n\geq 1
,
D_{i,m+n}
lies in a component of
f^{-np}(D_{j,0})
. This component must be
D_{j,n}
, for otherwise, it would contradict the condition that
K_{i}\cap K_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Therefore, we have
D_{i,m+n}\subset D_{j,n}
for all
n
. This implies
K_{i}\subset K_{j}
.

Since
\deg(f^{p}|_{{K_{i}}})=\deg(f^{p}|_{{K_{j}}})
and both
K_{i}
and
K_{j}
are stable sets of
f^{p}
, we have

\bigcup_{n>0}(f^{p}|_{K_{j}})^{-n}(K_{i})=K_{i}.

Furthermore, since
f^{p}:\partial K_{j}\to\partial K_{j}
is quasi-conformally conjugate to the restriction of a rational map on its Julia set (Theorem
1.5
), the set
\bigcup_{k>0}(f^{p}|_{K_{j}})^{-k}(\partial K_{i})
is dense in
\partial K_{j}
. This implies
\partial K_{i}=\partial K_{j}
.
Then each component of
K_{j}\setminus K_{i}
, if it exists, would be a Fatou domain. However, since
\overline{D_{i,n+1}}\subset D_{i,n}
, no component of
\partial D_{i,n}
for any
n\geq 0
forms the boundary of a Fatou domain in
K_{j}\setminus K_{i}
. Thus,
K_{i}=K_{j}
.
The claim is proved.

(1) Since
K_{i}\cap K_{j}\neq\emptyset
, we have either
D_{i,0}\subset D_{j,0}
, or
D_{j,0}\subset D_{i,0}
, or
\lambda_{j}\subset D_{i,0}
and
\lambda_{i}\subset D_{j,0}
. Then statement (1) follows directly from the above claim by setting
n=0
.

(2) It suffices to show that
V_{i,n}\subset D_{j,n}
for all
n\geq 0
. By statement (1), we have
V_{i,0}\subset D_{j,0}
. Consequently, for each
n>0
, either
V_{i,n}\subset D_{j,n}
or
V_{i,n}\cap D_{j,n}=\emptyset
.
If
V_{i,n}\cap D_{j,n}=\emptyset
for some
n>0
, according to the construction of
V_{i,n}
and
D_{j,n}
, there exists a component
\eta
of
\partial D_{j,n}
that separates
D_{j,n}
from
V_{i,n}
. In particular,
\eta
separates
D_{j,n}
from
\lambda_{i}
. By statement (1), it follows that
D_{j,n}\subset D_{i,0}
, which contradicts the claim above.

(3) Without loss of generality, we assume that
K_{\ell}
is distinct from both
K_{i}
and
K_{j}
. Then by applying statement (2) to
\{K_{i},K_{j}\}
and
\{K_{i},K_{\ell}\}
, we obtain that
V_{i,n}\subset D_{j,n}\cap D_{\ell,n}
for all
n>0
. This implies
K_{j}\cap K_{\ell}\not=\emptyset
.
∎

Let
s\in[1,r]
be the smallest integer such that
K_{0}\cap K_{s}\not=\emptyset
. Then
s
is a factor of
r
. Set
Z:=\{ks:0\leq k<r/s\}
. By Proposition
4.3
(3), we have

(a)

K_{i}\cap K_{j}\not=\emptyset
for any pair
i,j\in Z
; and

(b)

K_{i}\cap K_{\ell}=\emptyset
if
i\in Z
and
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}\setminus Z
.

Let
D_{0}
be the intersection of all
D_{i,0}
with
i\in Z
. Applying Proposition
4.3
(1) to each pair
\{K_{i},K_{j}\}
with distinct
i,j\in Z
, we conclude that
D_{0}
is the domain with boundary components
\{\lambda_{i}:i\in Z\}
, and
V_{i,0}\subset D_{0}
for every
i\in Z
.

For every
n\geq 1
, denote by
D_{n}
the component of
f^{-pn}(D_{0})
containing
V_{0,n}
. By point (a) above and Proposition
4.3
(2),
it holds that
\bigcup_{i\in Z}V_{i,n}\subset\bigcap_{i\in Z}D_{i,n}
for every
n\geq 0
. Moreover, since

f^{np}\big(\bigcap_{i\in Z}D_{i,n}\big)\subset\bigcap_{i\in Z}D_{i,0}=D_{0}
and
f^{np}(D_{n})=D_{0}
, it follows that

\bigcup_{i\in Z}V_{i,n}\subset\bigcap_{i\in Z}D_{i,n}\subset D_{n}

for all
n\geq 0
.
This inclusion also implies
D_{n}\subset D_{i,n}
for any
i\in Z
and
n\geq 0
. Thus

(c)

for every
n\geq 0
, the equality
\bigcap_{i\in Z}D_{i,n}=D_{n}
holds.

This equality implies
\overline{{D}_{n_{1}}}\subset D_{n_{2}}
for sufficiently large
n_{2}-n_{1}
. Then

E:=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}D_{n}=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\overline{D_{n}}

is a stable set of
f^{p}
by Lemma
4.6
. Moreover,
\partial D_{n}
is disjoint from
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
for every sufficiently large integer
n
. Thus, each component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
is either contained in
E
or disjoint from
E
. Since
E
contains
\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\overline{V_{0,n}}
, it follows from Corollary
4.1
that
E
is complex-type. Additionally, since
\lambda_{0}=\lambda\subset J_{f}
is disjoint from
E
, we have
J_{f}\not\subset E
.

Finally, point (c) implies
E=\bigcap_{i\in Z}K_{i}
. Therefore,
f^{s}(E)\subset E
, and hence
E
is also a stable set of
f^{s}
.
Combining this with point (b) above, we deduce that
E,f(E),\ldots,f^{s-1}(E)
are pairwise disjoint. Thus,
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{i=0}^{s-1}f^{i}(E)
is a stable set of
f
and satisfies all the conditions of Lemma
4.9
according to the previous discussion.
∎

4.4.
Cluster-exact decomposition

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map. A continuum
K\subset J_{f}
is called a
cluster
if it is a stable set of
f^{p}
for some
p\geq 1
, and the renormalization of
f^{p}
on
K
is a cluster rational map, i.e., the sphere is a Fatou chain of this rational map.

Theorem 4.2

(Cluster-exact decomposition)
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map with
J_{f}\neq\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, and let
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}
be the intersection of
J_{f}
with the union of all maximal Fatou chains of
f
intersecting
P
. Then there exists a stable set
{\mathcal{K}}
of
f
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}\subset{\mathcal{K}}\subset J_{f}
such that

(1)

every periodic component of
{\mathcal{K}}
is a cluster; and

(2)

{\mathcal{K}}
induces an exact decomposition of
(f,P)
.

Moreover, each component of
{\mathcal{K}}
intersects or separates
P
.

Proof.

If
J_{f}={\mathcal{M}}_{f}
, the theorem holds by taking
{\mathcal{K}}=J_{f}
. Thus, we assume
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}\subsetneq J_{f}
. Note that
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}
is a stable set of
f
.
Then by applying Theorem
4.1
to
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}={\mathcal{M}}_{f}
, we obtain a stable set
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\subsetneq J_{f}
such that
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
induces an exact decomposition of
(f,P)
, and each component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
intersects or separates
P
.

If every periodic component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
is a cluster, the theorem holds by taking
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}_{1}
.

Now, suppose that
K_{*}
is a periodic component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
with period
p\geq 1
such that
K_{*}
is not a cluster. By Theorem
1.5
, there exist a marked rational map
(g,Q)
and a quasiconformal map
\phi
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, such that
J_{g}=\phi(K_{*})
and
\phi\circ f^{p}=g\circ\phi
on
K_{*}
.
Here,
Q
is the union of
\phi(P\cap K_{*})
together with all centers of Fatou domains
U
of
g
such that
\phi^{-1}(U)
contains a point of
P
. In particular,
g
is not a cluster rational map.

As before, we can define
{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
for
(g,Q)
. Then
{\mathcal{M}}_{g}\subsetneq J_{g}
.
By applying Theorem
4.1
to
(g,Q)
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
, we obtain a stable set
{\mathcal{K}}_{g}
of
g
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{g}\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{g}\subsetneq J_{g}
such that
{\mathcal{K}}_{g}
induces an exact decomposition of
(g,Q)
, and each component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{g}
intersects or separates
Q
. Set
{\mathcal{E}}=\phi^{-1}({\mathcal{K}}_{g})
. Then
{\mathcal{E}}\subsetneq K_{*}
is a stable set of
f^{p}
, and we have the following commutative diagram:

(4.2)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}(K_{*},{\mathcal{E}})&\xrightarrow{\ \ f^{p}\ \ }&(K_{*},{\mathcal{E}})\\
\phi\Big\downarrow&&\Big\downarrow\phi\\
(J_{g},{\mathcal{K}}_{g})&\xrightarrow{\ \ g\ \ }&(J_{g},{\mathcal{K}}_{g})\vskip-2.84544pt.\end{array}

From the choice of
Q
, it follows that each component of
{\mathcal{E}}
intersects or separates
P
. It is worth noting that
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}\cap K_{*}
is also a stable set of
f^{p}
.

For any continuum
E\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, denote by
\widehat{E}
the union of
E
and all components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
disjoint from
P
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Both
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}\cap K_{*}
and
\partial\widehat{K}_{*}
are contained in
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
\phi({\mathcal{M}}_{f}\cap K_{*})
and
\phi(\partial\widehat{K}_{*})
are contained in
{\mathcal{M}}_{g}\,(\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{g})
. Recall that
\phi
sends a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K_{*}
onto a Fatou domain of
g
.

Let
B
be a marked maximal Fatou chain of
(f,P)
such that
\partial B
is a component of
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}
contained in
K_{*}
. Note that each component of
B\setminus\partial B
is a Fatou domain of
f
, and hence a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K_{*}
. This implies that
\phi(B)
lies in a marked maximal Fatou chain of
(g,Q)
. Hence,
\phi(\partial B)=\partial\phi(B)\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
.

For any point
z\in\partial\widehat{K}_{*}
, there exists a component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\widehat{K}_{*}
with
z\in\partial D
, and such a
D
must intersect
P
. Then
\phi(\partial D)
is the boundary of a marked Fatou domain of
(g,Q)
. It follows immediately that
\phi(z)\in{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
.
∎

Let
K_{1},\ldots,K_{m}
be all components of
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
whose orbits pass through
K_{*}
. For each
K_{i}
, there exists a smallest integer
k_{i}\geq 0
such that
f^{k_{i}}(K_{i})=K_{*}
. Thus,
K_{i}
is a component of
f^{-k_{i}}(K_{*})
. Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
denote the union of all components of
f^{-k_{i}}({\mathcal{E}})\cap K_{i}
that either intersect or separate
P
. Then both
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}\cap K_{i}
and
\partial\widehat{K}_{i}
are contained in
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
by Proposition
4.4
. Set

{\mathcal{K}}_{2}=\bigg({\mathcal{K}}_{1}\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}K_{i}\bigg)\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{E}}_{i}.

The previous discussion shows that
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
is a stable set of
f
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{2}\subsetneq J_{f}
, and each component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
intersects or separates
P
. Moreover, it holds that

(4.3)

\bigcup_{K\in{\rm\,Comp}({\mathcal{K}}_{1})}\partial\widehat{K}\ \subset\ {\mathcal{K}}_{2}\subsetneq{\mathcal{K}}_{1}.

Proposition 4.5
.

The stable set
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
induces an exact decomposition of
(f,P)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{B}}
is a stable set of
f
. From the definitions, we deduce the following:

(1)

The stable set
{\mathcal{B}}
induces an exact decomposition of
(f,P)
if and only if, for any complex-type component
V
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\mathcal{B}
, whenever a component
B_{1}
of
f^{-1}(\mathcal{B})
lies in
V
, it neither intersects nor separates
P
;

(2)

For any component
B
of
\mathcal{B}
, a component
B_{1}
of
f^{-1}(\mathcal{B})
that intersects
\widehat{B}
is either equal to
B
or contained in a component of
\widehat{B}\setminus B
, which is simply connected and avoids
P
.

We shall use statement (1) to prove this proposition.

Let
V
be any complex-type component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
. By the construction of
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
and the inclusion relation (
4.3
), the domain
V
is either a complex-type component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
or a complex-type component of
\widehat{K}_{i}\setminus{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
.

Let
E
be a component of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}}_{2})
that lies in
V
.
Since
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
, the continuum
E
is contained in a component of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}}_{1})
, denoted by
K(E)
. The purpose is to verify that
E
neither intersects nor separates
P
.

Case 1. The domain
V
is also a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
.
Since
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
induces an exact decomposition of
(f,P)
, by statement (1) above,
K(E)
neither intersects nor separates
P
. So does
E
.

Case 2. The domain
V
is a complex-type component of
\widehat{K}_{i}\setminus{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq m
.
In this case,
K(E)
intersects
\widehat{K}_{i}
.
Then by statement (2), either
K(E)=K_{i}
, or
K(E)
is contained in a component
D
of
\widehat{K}_{i}\setminus K_{i}
. The domain
D
is simply connected and disjoint from
P
. Moreover, we have
D\subset V
since
E\subset V
. Thus, it suffices to consider the former case.

The equality
K(E)=K_{i}
implies that
E\subset K_{i}
and
f(E)\subset f(K_{i})=K_{j}
for some
j
. Thus,
f(E)
is a component of
{\mathcal{E}}_{j}\subset K_{j}
.
Since
E\subset V
is disjoint from
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
, by the definition of
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
, exactly one of the following two situations occurs:

•

K_{i}\not=K_{*}
, and
E
neither intersects nor separates
P
;

•

K_{i}=K_{*}
, and
E
is a component of
(f^{p}|_{K_{*}})^{-1}({\mathcal{E}})
that lies in
V
.

Thus, it suffices to deal with the second situation.

By the commutative diagram (
4.2
),
\phi(E)
is a component of
g^{-1}({\mathcal{K}}_{g})
.
Note also that
\phi(V)
is a complex-type component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}_{g}
. Since
{\mathcal{K}}_{g}
induces an exact decomposition of
(g,Q)
, it follows from statement (1) that
\phi(E)
neither intersects nor separates
Q
. Thus,
E
neither intersects nor separates
P
.
∎

By Proposition
4.5
, if every periodic component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
is a cluster, then Theorem
4.2
holds by choosing
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}_{2}
. Otherwise, we can repeat the above argument by replacing
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
with
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
and obtain a
stable set
{\mathcal{K}}_{3}
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{3}\subsetneq{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
such that
{\mathcal{K}}_{3}
induces an exact decomposition of
(f,P)
, and each component of
{\mathcal{K}}_{3}
intersects or separates
P
.

By iterating this process, we obtain a sequence of stable sets
\{{\mathcal{K}}_{n}\}
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{f}\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{n}\subsetneq{\mathcal{K}}_{n-1}
. This process must stop after a finite number of steps by Lemma
4.7
. This completes the proof of Theorem
4.2
.
∎

The subsequent corollary of Theorem
4.2
will be used in Section 8.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map with
J_{f}\neq\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Then there exist an
f
-invariant and finite set
P^{\prime}\supset P
and a stable set
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}\subset J_{f}
such that

(1)

the stable set
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
induces a cluster-exact decomposition of
(f,P^{\prime})
, and each of its components intersects
P^{\prime}
;

(2)

every complex-type component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
rel
P^{\prime}
is disjoint from attracting cycles of
f
;

(3)

every simple-type component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
rel
P^{\prime}
is a simply connected domain; and

(4)

every annular-type component
A
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
rel
P^{\prime}
is an annulus, and moreover, if
A\cap f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
, then
A
contains an annular-type component of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{K}}
be the stable set obtained in Theorem
4.2
.
Consider a finite and
f
-invariant set
Q_{0}\subset{\mathcal{K}}
such that each component of
{\mathcal{K}}
contains at least two points of
Q_{0}
. It is important to note that the complex-type components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}
rel
P
coincide with those rel
P\cup Q_{0}
.
Hence, items (1)–(3) and the former part of
(4)
hold for the stable set
{\mathcal{K}}
rel
P\cup Q_{0}
.

If the latter part of item (4) is false for an annular-type component
A
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}
rel
P\cup Q_{0}
, let
K_{A}
be a component of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}})\cap A
. We can select two points from
f^{-1}(Q_{0})
within
K_{A}
and denote by
Q_{1}
the union of these two points with
Q_{0}
. Then the stable set
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}:={\mathcal{K}}\cup K_{A}
satisfies items (1)–(3) and the former part of
(4)
rel
P\cup Q_{1}
.
Moreover, the number of annular-type components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
rel
P\cup Q_{1}
is bounded above by that of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}
rel
P\cup Q_{0}
.

If the latter part of item (4) is still false for
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
rel
P\cup Q_{1}
, we can repeat the argument above, replacing
{\mathcal{K}}
and
Q_{0}
with
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and
Q_{1}
, respectively. Thus, we obtain a sequence of stable sets
\{{\mathcal{K}}_{n}\}
and a sequence of
f
-invariant finite sets
\{Q_{n}\}
such that
{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
satisfies items (1)–(3) and the former part of
(4)
rel
P\cup Q_{n}
, and the number of annular-type components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
rel
P\cup Q_{n}
is strictly decreasing as
n
increases.
Consequently, this process must stop after
N
steps for an integer
N\geq 0
. Then
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{K}}_{N}
and
P^{\prime}=P\cup Q_{N}
satisfy items (1)–(4).
∎

5.
Blow-up of an exact sub-system

In this section, we will prove Theorem
1.6
and complete the proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Throughout this section, let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map, and let
V\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a domain such that
\partial V\subset J_{f}
consists of finitely many pairwise disjoint continua. We also assume that
f:V_{1}\to V
is an exact sub-system of
(f,P)
, i.e.,
V_{1}
is a component of
f^{-1}(V)
contained in
V
, and each component of
V\setminus V_{1}
is a full continuum disjoint from
P
.

For two topological spaces
X
and
Y
, a
homotopy
from
X
to
Y
is a continuous map
\xi:X\times[0,1]\to Y
. We usually write the homotopy as
\{\xi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
.

5.1.
Construction of the blow-up map

Let
\lambda
be a component of
\partial V
. Since
V\setminus V_{1}
is compact, we have
\lambda\subset\partial V\subset\partial V_{1}
. Thus,
f(\lambda)
is also a component of
\partial V
. Let
E_{\lambda}
be the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus V
containing
\lambda
. If
E_{f(\lambda)}
is disjoint from
P
, then
f(E_{\lambda})=E_{f(\lambda)}
, and
E_{\lambda}
is also disjoint from
P
.

Let
\lambda
be a periodic component of
\partial V
with period
p\geq 1
. Since
f
is expanding in a neighborhood of
J_{f}
under the orbifold metric, there exists an annulus
A\subset V\setminus P
such that
\lambda
is a component of
\partial A
, and
\overline{A_{1}}\subset A\cup\lambda
, where
A_{1}
is the component of
f^{-p}(A)
with
\lambda\subset\partial A_{1}
. A folklore argument implies that
E_{\lambda}
is locally connected and
E_{\lambda}\cap P\neq\emptyset
.
Since each component
\lambda
of
\partial V
is eventually periodic, it follows that each component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus V
is locally connected.

Now, we begin to construct the blow-up map. Let
\chi
be a conformal map from
V
onto a circular domain
\hat{\Omega}\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, i.e., each component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\hat{\Omega}
is a closed round disk in
\mathbb{C}
. Let
\hat{\Omega}_{1}:=\chi(V_{1})
. Then

\hat{g}:=\chi\circ f\circ\chi^{-1}:\hat{\Omega}_{1}\to\hat{\Omega}

is a holomorphic and proper map, which can be continuously extended to
\partial\hat{\Omega}
such that
\hat{g}(\partial\hat{\Omega})\subset\partial\hat{\Omega}
. By the symmetry principle and the expanding property of
f
, the map
\hat{g}
is holomorphic and expanding in a neighborhood of
\partial\hat{\Omega}
.

Denote
\hat{\mathcal{D}}=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\hat{\Omega}
. Define a map
\wp:\hat{\mathcal{D}}\to\hat{\mathcal{D}}
by
\wp(\hat{D}_{i})=\hat{D}_{j}
if
\hat{g}(\partial\hat{D}_{i})=\partial\hat{D}_{j}
, where
\hat{D}_{i}
and
\hat{D}_{j}
are components of
\hat{{\mathcal{D}}}
, and

\wp(z)=r_{j}\left(\frac{z-a_{i}}{r_{i}}\right)^{d_{i}}+a_{j}\quad\text{ if }z\in\hat{D}_{i},

where
a_{i}
and
r_{i}
are the center and the radius of the closed round disk
\hat{D}_{i}
, respectively, and
d_{i}=\deg(\hat{g}|_{{\partial\hat{D}_{i}}})
. Since
\hat{g}
is expanding on
\partial\hat{\Omega}=\partial\hat{{\mathcal{D}}}
, if
\partial\hat{D}_{i}
is periodic with period
p_{i}\geq 1
, then there exists a quasi-symmetric map
w_{i}:\partial\hat{D}_{i}\to\partial\hat{D}_{i}
such that
\wp^{p_{i}}\circ w_{i}=w_{i}\circ{\hat{g}}^{p_{i}}
on
\partial\hat{D}_{i}
. By pullback, we obtain a quasi-symmetric map
w:\partial\hat{\Omega}\to\partial\hat{\Omega}
such that

\wp\circ w=w\circ{\hat{g}}\quad\text{ on }\partial\hat{\Omega}.

Consider the conformal welding induced by
w
. There exist two conformal maps
\zeta:\hat{\Omega}\to\tilde{\Omega}\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\textup{ and }\eta:{\rm int}(\hat{{\mathcal{D}}})\to{\rm int}(\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}})
such that
\zeta=\eta\circ w
on
\partial\hat{\Omega}
, where
\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}}:=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}
, and the notation
{\rm int}(\cdot)
represents the interior of the corresponding set. Define

\tilde{g}_{0}:=\begin{cases}\zeta\circ\hat{g}\circ\zeta^{-1}&\text{ on }\zeta(\hat{\Omega}_{1})\subset\tilde{\Omega},\\
\eta\circ\wp\circ\eta^{-1}&\text{ on }\eta(\hat{{\mathcal{D}}})=\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}}.\end{cases}

Then
\tilde{g}_{0}
is a holomorphic map on
\zeta(\hat{\Omega}_{1})\cup\eta(\hat{{\mathcal{D}}})
. Set
\xi_{0}:=\chi^{-1}\circ\zeta^{-1}:\tilde{\Omega}\to V
, and continuously extend it to a quotient map (defined in
A.2
) of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, due to the local connectivity of
\partial V
. Then

\xi_{0}\circ\tilde{g}_{0}=f\circ\xi_{0}\quad\text{ on }\tilde{\Omega}_{1}^{*}:=\zeta(\hat{\Omega}_{1}).

For each
n\geq 1
, set
V_{n}:=(f|_{V_{1}})^{-1}(V)
. Then
f:V_{n+1}\to V_{n}
is an exact sub-system for each
n\geq 1
.
By replacing
V
with some
V_{n}
, we may assume that
V\setminus V_{1}
is disjoint from
f^{-1}(P)
. This means that
f
sends a neighborhood of each component of
V\setminus V_{1}
homeomorphically onto a neighborhood of a complementary component of
V
.

For each component of
\tilde{\Omega}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}_{1}^{*}
, we pick a small disk in
\tilde{\Omega}\setminus\xi_{0}^{-1}(P\cap V)
as a neighborhood of this component, such that these disks have pairwise disjoint closures. Let
{\mathcal{N}}
denote their union. Then
\tilde{g}_{0}
is injective on
\partial{{\mathcal{N}}}
. Define a new map
\tilde{g}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
\tilde{g}
is continuous and injective on
{\mathcal{N}}
, and
\tilde{g}(z)=\tilde{g}_{0}(z)
for all
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}
.

It is easy to verify that
\tilde{g}
is a PCF branched covering with
{\rm deg}(\tilde{g})=\deg f|_{V_{1}}
and it is holomorphic on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{{\mathcal{N}}}
. Note that the interior of each component
\tilde{D}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}
contains a unique eventually periodic point
z(\tilde{D})
of
\tilde{g}
. Set

\tilde{Z}=\{z(\tilde{D}):\xi_{0}(\tilde{D})\cap P\neq\emptyset\}\quad\text{ and }\quad\tilde{Q}=\xi_{0}^{-1}(P\cap V)\cup\tilde{Z}.

It follows that
\tilde{g}(\tilde{Z})\subset\tilde{Z}
,
\tilde{g}(\tilde{Q})\subset\tilde{Q}
, and
P_{\tilde{g}}\subset\tilde{Q}
.

\theta
\xi_{0}
id
\xi_{0}
\xi_{1}=\xi_{0}\circ\theta
f
\tilde{g}_{0}
\tilde{g}
V_{1}
V
\tilde{\Omega}_{1}^{*}
\tilde{\Omega}
\tilde{\Omega}_{1}
\tilde{\Omega}
\mathcal{N}
\tilde{g}_{0}(\partial\mathcal{N})

Figure 9.
The construction of
\tilde{g}_{0}
,
\tilde{g}
,
\xi_{0}
, and
\xi_{1}
.

Denote
\tilde{\Omega}_{1}=\tilde{g}^{-1}(\tilde{\Omega})
. Then
\tilde{\Omega}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}_{1}
consists of pairwise disjoint closed disks in
{\mathcal{N}}
. Moreover, by lifting, there exists a homeomorphism
\theta:\tilde{\Omega}_{1}\to\tilde{\Omega}_{1}^{*}
such that
\theta=id
on
\tilde{\Omega}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}
and
\tilde{g}=\tilde{g}_{0}\circ\theta
on
\tilde{\Omega}_{1}
; see Figure
9
. Since each component of
\partial\tilde{\Omega}_{1}
is a Jordan curve and
\tilde{g}
is injective on
\partial\tilde{\Omega}_{1}\setminus\partial\tilde{\Omega}
, we can continuously extend
\theta
to a quotient map of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. This extended map, still denoted by
\theta
, sends
\tilde{\Omega}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}_{1}
onto
\tilde{\Omega}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}_{1}^{*}
.

Define
\xi_{1}:=\xi_{0}\circ\theta
. Then
\xi_{1}
is a quotient map of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
\xi_{1}(\tilde{\Omega}_{1})=V_{1}
,
\xi_{1}=\xi_{0}
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}
, and

\xi_{0}\circ\tilde{g}=f\circ\xi_{1}\quad\text{ on }\tilde{\Omega}_{1}.

Moreover, there exists a homotopy
\xi_{t}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
,
t\in[0,1]
, such that
\xi_{t}
is a quotient map of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
and
\xi_{t}(z)=\xi_{0}(z)
for all
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}
and
t\in[0,1]
. In particular,
\xi_{t}(\tilde{Q}\cap\tilde{\Omega})=P\cap V
.

Since
\tilde{g}:\tilde{\Omega}_{1}\setminus\tilde{g}^{-1}(\tilde{Q})\rightarrow\tilde{\Omega}\setminus\tilde{Q}
and
f:V_{1}\setminus f^{-1}(P)\rightarrow V\setminus P
are both coverings, and

\{\xi_{t}^{-1}(z):t\in[0,1]\}

is a singleton in
\tilde{Q}\cap\tilde{\Omega}
for every
z\in P\cap V
, the homotopy
\xi_{t}:\tilde{\Omega}\setminus\tilde{Q}\to V\setminus P
can be lifted by
f
and
\tilde{g}
to a homotopy
\xi_{t}:\tilde{\Omega}_{1}\setminus\tilde{g}^{-1}(\tilde{Q})\to V_{1}\setminus f^{-1}(P)
,
t\in[1,2]
, by the general homotopy lifting theorem; see
[
22
, Proposition 1.30]
. Furthermore, this homotopy can be extended to a homotopy

\xi_{t}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
,
t\in[1,2],
such that each
\xi_{t}
is a quotient map and
\xi_{t}(z)=\xi_{1}(z)
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{g}^{-1}({\mathcal{N}})
for every
t\in[1,2]
.

Inductively applying the above argument, we obtain a sequence of quotient maps
\{\xi_{n}\}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
\xi_{n}(\tilde{\Omega}_{n})=V_{n}
,
\xi_{n+1}=\xi_{n}
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{g}^{-n}({\mathcal{N}})
, and

\xi_{n}\circ\tilde{g}=f\circ\xi_{n+1}\quad\text{ on }\tilde{\Omega}_{n+1},

where
\tilde{\Omega}_{n}=\tilde{g}^{-n}(\tilde{\Omega})
and
V_{n}=(f|_{V_{1}})^{-n}(V)
.

Proposition 5.1
.

The marked branched covering
(\tilde{g},\tilde{Q})
is combinatorially equivalent to a marked rational map
(g,Q)
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma=\{{\gamma_{k}}\}
be a multicurve of
(\tilde{g},\tilde{Q})
. Its transition matrix
(a_{kl})
is defined by

a_{kl}=\sum\frac{1}{\deg\tilde{g}:\delta\to{\gamma}_{l}},

where the summation is taken over all components
\delta
of
\tilde{g}^{-1}({\gamma}_{l})
isotopic to
{\gamma}_{k}
rel
\tilde{Q}
.

Since each component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}
contains at most one point of
\tilde{Q}
, we may require that each curve in
\Gamma
is contained in
\tilde{\Omega}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}
. Thus

\xi_{0}\circ\tilde{g}=f\circ\xi_{0}
on
\tilde{g}^{-1}({\gamma_{k}})
for each
{\gamma}_{k}\in{\Gamma}
. By the choice of
\tilde{Q}
, the collection of curves
\xi_{0}(\Gamma)=\{\xi_{0}({\gamma}_{k})\}
forms a multicurve of the rational map
f
