. Moreover, each entry of the transition matrix of
\xi_{0}(\Gamma)
under
f
is greater than or equal to the corresponding entry of the transition matrix of
\Gamma
under
(\tilde{g},\tilde{Q})
. Then
(\tilde{g},\tilde{Q})
has no Thurston obstruction since
f
has no Thurston obstruction by
[
4
, Theorem 2.3]
. Therefore,
(\tilde{g},\tilde{Q})
is combinatorially equivalent to a marked rational map
(g,Q)

by
[
4
, Theorem 2.1]
.
∎

5.2.
Dynamics of the blow-up map

According to Proposition
5.1
, there exists an isotopy
\phi_{t}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
rel
\tilde{Q}
,
t\in[0,1]
such that
\phi_{0}(\tilde{Q})=Q
and
g\circ\phi_{1}=\phi_{0}\circ\tilde{g}
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Recall that
\tilde{Z}=\tilde{Q}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}
and set
Z=\phi_{0}(\tilde{Z})
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Each Fatou domain of
g
with the center in
Z
is a disk whose boundary is disjoint from
Q
, and any two such Fatou domains have disjoint closures. In particular,
g
is a Sierpiński rational map if its attracting periodic points are all contained in
Z
.

To prove this proposition, we need a combinatorial criterion to determine whether the boundary of a Fatou domain contains marked points, whether it is a Jordan curve, and whether two Fatou domains have disjoint closures.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
R
be a PCF rational map, and let
U
be a periodic Fatou domain of
R
with center
a
.

(1)

A repelling periodic point
b
lies in
\partial U
if and only if there exists an open arc
\beta\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{R}
joining
a
and
b
, such that
R^{-p}(\beta)
has a component isotopic to
\beta
rel
P_{R}
for some
p\geq 1
.

(2)

Let
U^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be another periodic Fatou domain of
R
with center
a^{\prime}
. Then
\partial U\cap\partial U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
if and only if there exists an open arc
\beta\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{R}
joining
a
and
a^{\prime}
, such that
R^{-p}(\beta)
has a component isotopic to
\beta
rel
P_{R}
for some integer
p\geq 1
.

(3)

Assume that
\partial U\cap P_{R}=\emptyset
. Then
U
is not a disk if and only if there exists an open arc
\beta\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{R}
that joins
a
to itself, such that
\overline{\beta}
separates
P_{R}
, and
R^{-p}(\beta)
has a component isotopic to
\beta
rel
P_{R}
for some integer
p\geq 1
.

Proof.

(1) If
b\in\partial U
, the internal ray in
U
that lands at
b
satisfies the condition.

Conversely, the arc
\beta
can be decomposed into two sub-arcs
\beta=\alpha\cup\delta
, such that
\alpha\subset U
and
\overline{\delta}
is disjoint from the super-attracting cycles of
R
. By successive lifting,
R^{-kp}(\beta)
has a component
\beta_{k}
isotopic to
\beta
rel
P_{R}
, and
\beta_{k}
has a decomposition
\beta_{k}=\alpha_{k}\cup\delta_{k}
such that
R^{kp}(\alpha_{k})=\alpha
and
R^{kp}(\delta_{k})=\delta
. Observe that
\alpha_{k}\subset U
, and
{\rm diam}(\delta_{k})\to 0
as
k\to\infty
by Lemma
A.1
. Hence,
b\in\partial U
.

(2) First, assume that
\partial U\cap\partial U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. We choose an open arc
\beta^{\prime}
that joins
a
and
a^{\prime}
and passes through a point
z\in\partial U\cap\partial U^{\prime}
such that
\beta^{\prime}\setminus\{z\}
consists of two internal rays in
U
and
U^{\prime}
, respectively.

If
R^{k}(z)\notin P_{R}
for all
k\geq 1
, since
\#P_{R}<\infty
, there exist integers
q,p\geq 1
such that
R^{q+p}(\beta^{\prime})
is isotopic to
R^{p}(\beta^{\prime})
rel
P_{R}
. Let
\beta=R^{q+p}(\beta^{\prime})
. Then
R^{-p}(\beta)
has a component isotopic to
\beta
rel
P_{R}
.

If
R^{k}(z)\in P_{R}
for some integer
k\geq 1
, then by Lemma
2.11
, there exist integers
q,p\geq 1
such that
R^{q+p}(\beta^{\prime})=R^{q}(\beta^{\prime})
. Note that
R^{q}(z)
is a repelling periodic point in
P_{R}
. Let
\beta
be an open arc obtained by modifying
R^{q}(\beta^{\prime})
in a small neighborhood of the point
R^{q}(z)
such that
R^{q}(z)\notin\beta
. Then
R^{-2p}(\beta)
has a component isotopic to
\beta
rel
P_{R}
.

Conversely, we decompose
\beta
into three sub-arcs
\beta=\alpha\cup\delta\cup\alpha^{\prime}
, such that
\alpha\subset U
,
\alpha^{\prime}\subset U^{\prime}
, and
\overline{\delta}
is disjoint from the super-attracting cycles of
g
. By successive lifting,
R^{-kp}(\beta)
has a component
\beta_{k}
isotopic to
\beta
rel
P_{R}
, and
\beta_{k}
can be decomposed as
\beta_{k}=\alpha_{k}\cup\delta_{k}\cup\alpha_{k}^{\prime}
such that
R^{kp}(\alpha_{k})=\alpha
,
R^{kp}(\delta_{k})=\delta
, and
R^{kp}(\alpha_{k}^{\prime})=\alpha^{\prime}
. Observe that
\alpha_{k}\subset U
,
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\subset U^{\prime}
, and
{\rm diam}(\delta_{k})\to 0
as
k\to\infty
by Lemma
A.1
. Thus,
\partial U\cap\partial U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.

(3) First, assume that
U
is not a disk. Then there exist two internal rays in
U
landing at a common point
z\in\partial U
. Let
\beta^{\prime}
be the union of these two internal rays together with the point
z
. For simplicity, we assume
R(U)=U
. Since
\partial U\cap P_{R}=\emptyset
, it follows that all
R^{k}(\beta^{\prime})
are open arcs in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{R}
with the same endpoints
a
.

If
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus R^{k+1}(\overline{\beta^{\prime}})
has a component
D_{k+1}
disjoint from
P_{R}
, then
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus R^{k}(\overline{\beta^{\prime}})
also has a component
D_{k}
disjoint from
P_{R}
, and
R(D_{k})=D_{k+1}
. It follows that
R^{k}(\overline{\beta^{\prime}})
separates
P_{R}
for each sufficiently large integer
k
. Otherwise, there would be a sequence
\{k_{n}\}
of integers tending to
\infty
such that
R^{k_{n}}(D_{1})\cap P_{R}=\emptyset
for all
n\geq 1
. This is impossible as
D_{1}\cap J_{R}\neq\emptyset
.

Since
\#P_{R}<\infty
, there exist integers
q,p\geq 1
such that
R^{q+p}(\beta^{\prime})
is isotopic to
R^{p}(\beta^{\prime})
rel
P_{R}
. Let
\beta=R^{q+p}(\beta^{\prime})
. Then
R^{-p}(\beta)
has a component isotopic to
\beta
rel
P_{R}
.

Conversely, by a similar argument as in the proof of statement (2), we can obtain two distinct internal rays in
U
with the same landing point. Hence,
U
is not a disk.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

To prove the proposition, it suffices to verify the combinatorial conditions in Lemma
5.1
for the branched covering
\tilde{g}
. Let
a\in\tilde{Z}
be a periodic point of
\tilde{g}
.

Let
\beta\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{Q}
be an open arc joining the point
a
to a repelling periodic point
b\in\tilde{Q}
that belongs to
\tilde{\Omega}
. Assume, by contradiction, that
\tilde{g}^{-p}(\beta)
has a component
\beta_{1}
isotopic to itself rel
\tilde{Q}
for some integer
p\geq 1
. By isotopy lifting,
\tilde{g}^{-kp}(\beta)
has a component
\beta_{k}
isotopic to
\beta
rel
\tilde{Q}
.

We adjust the arc
\beta
within its isotopic class so that
\beta=\alpha\cup\delta
with
\alpha\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}
and
\delta\subset\tilde{\Omega}
. This allows us to write
\beta_{k}=\alpha_{k}\cup\delta_{k}
with
\alpha_{k}\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{g}^{-kp}(\tilde{\Omega})
and
\delta_{k}\subset\tilde{g}^{-kp}(\tilde{\Omega})
, where
\tilde{g}^{kp}(\alpha_{k})=\alpha
and
\tilde{g}^{kp}(\delta_{k})=\delta
. In particular, one endpoint of
\delta_{k}
lies in
\partial\tilde{\Omega}
and the other is
b
.

Recall that
\{\xi_{n}\}
is a sequence of quotient maps of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
\xi_{0}(\tilde{Q}\cap\tilde{\Omega})=P\cap V
,
\xi_{n}(\tilde{\Omega}_{n})=V_{n}
,
\xi_{n+1}=\xi_{n}
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}_{n}
, and

\xi_{n}\circ\tilde{g}=f\circ\xi_{n+1}\quad\text{ on }\tilde{\Omega}_{n+1},

where
\tilde{\Omega}_{n}=\tilde{g}^{-n}(\tilde{\Omega})
and
V_{n}=(f|_{V_{1}})^{-n}(V)
. Thus,
\xi_{kp}(\delta_{k})
is a component of
f^{-kp}(\xi_{0}(\delta))
, such that one endpoint of
\xi_{kp}(\delta_{k})
lies in
\partial V
and the other is
\xi_{0}(b)
. By Lemma
A.1
, the diameter of
\xi_{kp}(\delta_{k})
tends to
0
as
k\to\infty
. It follows that
\xi_{0}(b)\in\partial V
, which contradicts the assumption that
b\in\tilde{\Omega}
. Hence, condition (1) holds.

The verification of conditions (2) and (3) is similar. Thus, we omit the details.
∎

5.3.
Fibers of the semi-conjugacy

Recall that
\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}}=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}
consists of pairwise disjoint closed disks, and

\tilde{g}
is holomorphic in a neighborhood of
\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}}
with
\tilde{g}(\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}})\subset\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}}
. Each component of
{\rm int}(\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}})
contains a unique preperiodic point of
\tilde{g}
.
Moreover, there exists a small neighborhood
\tilde{{\mathcal{N}}}_{a}
of the attracting cycles of
\tilde{g}
that are contained in
\tilde{\Omega}
such that
\tilde{g}:\tilde{{\mathcal{N}}}_{a}\to\tilde{{\mathcal{N}}}_{a}
is holomorphic.

Recall also that the marked branched covering
(\tilde{g},\tilde{Q})
is combinatorially equivalent to a marked rational map
(g,Q)
by a pair of homeomorphisms
\phi_{0},\phi_{1}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, which are connected by an isotopy

\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
rel
\tilde{Q}
.

By Proposition
5.2
, the homeomorphism
\phi_{0}
sends the preperiodic points of
\tilde{g}
in
{\rm int}(\tilde{\mathcal{D}})
to the centers of some Fatou domains of
g
, which are disks with pairwise disjoint closures. Note that the closure
{\mathcal{D}}
of the union of these Fatou domains is invariant under
g
.

We may specify the isotopy
\phi_{t}
such that
\phi_{0}
is holomorphic in
\tilde{{\mathcal{N}}}_{a}\cup{\rm int}(\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}})
with
\phi_{0}(\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}})={\mathcal{D}}
, and
\phi_{t}=\phi_{0}
on
\tilde{{\mathcal{N}}}_{a}\cup\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}}
for
t\in[0,1]
.

By successively applying Lemma
A.2
, for every
n\geq 0
, we have an isotopy
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in[n,n+1]}
rel
\tilde{g}^{-n}(\tilde{{\mathcal{D}}}\cup\tilde{{\mathcal{N}}}_{a}\cup\tilde{Q})
, such that
\phi_{n}\circ\tilde{g}=g\circ\phi_{n+1}
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Set
\Omega_{n}:=\phi_{n}(\tilde{\Omega}_{n})
.

Recall that in Section
5.1
, we obtained a homotopy
\{\xi_{t}\}_{t\in[n,n+1]}
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
for every
n\geq 0
, such that
\xi_{n}(\tilde{\Omega}_{n})=V_{n}
,
\xi_{n}=\xi_{n+1}
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{\Omega}_{n}
, and

\xi_{n}\circ\tilde{g}=f\circ\xi_{n+1}\text{ on }\tilde{\Omega}_{n+1},

where
\tilde{\Omega}_{n}=\tilde{g}^{-n}(\tilde{\Omega})
and
V_{n}=(f|_{V_{1}})^{-n}(V)
. Then we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\Omega_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\tilde{\Omega}_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{n+1}}
\textstyle{V_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\Omega_{n}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\tilde{\Omega}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{n}}
\textstyle{V_{n}}

Set
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}:=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus V_{n}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}:=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\Omega_{n}
, and
{\mathcal{N}}_{a}:=\phi_{0}(\tilde{{\mathcal{N}}}_{a})
.
Then for every
n\geq 0
, the family of maps
\{h_{t}:=\xi_{t}\circ\phi_{t}^{-1}\}_{t\in[n,n+1]}
is a homotopy on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

h_{t}(z):\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is a quotient map;

(2)

h_{t}(z)=h_{n}(z)
for
z\in{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\cup g^{-n}({\mathcal{N}}_{a})\cup g^{-n}(Q)
;

(3)

h_{t}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}_{n})={\mathcal{D}}_{n}
;

(4)

h_{n}\circ g=f\circ h_{n+1}
on
\Omega_{n+1}
.

Proposition 5.3
.

The sequence of maps
\{h_{n}\}
uniformly converges to a quotient map of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
.

Proof.

The argument is similar as in
[
8
, Theorem 1.1]
. By
[
8
, Lemma 3.1]
, the limit of a sequence of quotient maps is still a quotient map. Thus, it suffices to show that there exist constants
M>0
and
\rho>1
such that
\text{dist}(h_{n+1}(z),h_{n}(z))\leq M\rho^{-n}
for every
n\geq 1
.

Recall that the homotopic length of a curve
\gamma
is the infimum among the lengths of smooth curves homotopic to
\gamma
rel
P
with endpoints fixed under the orbifold metric; see Appendix
A.1
.

For any point
z\in\Omega\setminus({\mathcal{N}}_{a}\cup Q)
, define a curve
\gamma_{z}:[0,1]\to V\setminus P
as
\gamma_{z}(t):=h_{t}(z)
for
t\in[0,1]
.
Since the homotopic length of
\gamma_{z}
is continuous with respect to
z
and converges to zero as
z\to\partial\Omega\cup\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{a}\cup Q
, it is bounded above by a constant
M_{1}
for all points
z\in\Omega\setminus({\mathcal{N}}_{a}\cup Q)
.

Fix an integer
n\geq 1
and a point
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. If
z\in{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\cup g^{-n}({\mathcal{N}}_{a})\cup g^{-n}(Q)
, then

{\rm dist}(h_{n}(z),h_{n+1}(z))=0

by point (2) above.
If
z\in\Omega_{n}\setminus(g^{-n}({\mathcal{N}}_{a})\cup g^{-n}(Q))
, then
w=f^{n}(z)\in\Omega\setminus({\mathcal{N}}_{a}\cup Q)
.
In this case, the curve
\beta=\{h_{t}(z):t\in[n,n+1]\}
is a lift of
\gamma_{w}
by
f^{n}
based at
h_{n}(z)
. Consequently,

\text{dist}(h_{n}(z),h_{n+1}(z))\leq C\cdot L_{\omega}[\beta]\leq CM_{1}\rho^{-n}

by (
A.3
) and Lemma
A.1
. This completes the proof of Proposition
5.3
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

Let
\pi
be the limit quotient map of the sequence
\{h_{n}\}
, and set
K_{g}=\bigcap_{n>0}\overline{\Omega_{n}}
. By Proposition
5.3
, we have
\pi(\overline{\Omega_{n}})\subset\overline{V_{n}}
,
\pi({{\mathcal{D}}_{n}})={\mathcal{B}}_{n}
, and
\pi(\partial{\mathcal{D}}_{n})=\partial{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
for all
n>0
. It follows that
\pi(K_{g})\subset E:=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\overline{V_{n}}
. Since
\pi
is surjective, we obtain
\pi(K_{g})=E
. Moreover, the properties of
h_{n}
also imply that
\pi\circ g=f\circ\pi
on
K_{g}
and that
\pi:K_{g}\cap F_{g}\to E\cap F_{f}
is a conformal homeomorphism.

Suppose that
B
is a component of
{\mathcal{B}}
such that
f^{p}(\partial B)=\partial B
. Due to the properties of
\pi
mentioned above, there exists a unique component
D
of
{\mathcal{D}}
such that
\partial D\subset\pi^{-1}(\partial B)\cap K_{g}
, and
\pi^{-1}(\partial B)\cap K_{g}\subset J_{g}
is a stable set of
g^{p}
of simple type. Then by Theorem
1.5
,
\pi^{-1}(\partial B)\cap K_{g}
is the boundary of a Fatou domain of
g
, which implies
\pi^{-1}(\partial B)\cap K_{g}=\partial D
. Since
\pi({D})=B
, it follows that
\pi^{-1}(B)=D
. By pullback, we obtain
\pi^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}_{n})={\mathcal{D}}_{n}
for every
n>0
.

Now, consider an arbitrary point
z\in\bigcap_{n>0}V_{n}
. Then
\pi^{-1}(z)\subset\bigcap_{n>0}\Omega_{n}
is a full and connected compact set of simple type. If
z\in F_{f}
, then
\pi^{-1}(z)
is a singleton. If
z\in J_{f}
is eventually periodic, then
\pi^{-1}(z)\subset J_{f}
is eventually periodic under
g
, and thus a singleton by Lemma
A.1
.

Assume that
z\in J_{f}
is wandering, i.e.,
f^{i}(z)\not=f^{j}(z)
for any
i\neq j\geq 0
. Then the
\omega
-limit set
\omega(z)
contains infinitely many points. Otherwise, since
f(\omega(z))\subset\omega(z)
, the orbit of
z
would converge to repelling cycles, a contradiction. Thus, we may choose a point
z_{\infty}\in\omega(z)\setminus P
and a subsequence
\{f^{n_{k}}(z)\}
such that
f^{n_{k}}(z)\to z_{\infty}
as
k\to\infty
.

Let
U
be a disk such that
z_{\infty}\in U
and
\overline{U}\cap P=\emptyset
. Then
f^{n_{k}}(z)\in U
for every sufficiently large integer
k
. It follows that
g^{n_{k}}(\pi^{-1}(z))\subset\pi^{-1}(\overline{U})
for every sufficiently large integer
k
. Since
\pi^{-1}(\overline{U})
is a full continuum disjoint from
P_{g}
, by Lemma
A.1
, the diameters of components of
g^{-n}(\pi^{-1}(\overline{U}))
tend to
0
as
n\to\infty
. Thus,
\pi^{-1}(z)
is a singleton.

Finally, the uniqueness of the rational map
g
is deduced directly from
[
13
, Theorem 1]
. Then we complete the proof of Theorem
1.6
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

By Theorem
4.2
, there exists a stable set
{\mathcal{K}}
of
f
that induces a cluster-exact decomposition of
(f,P)
. Moreover, the union
{\mathcal{V}}
of all complex-type components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}
avoids the attracting cycles of
f
. It then follows from Theorem
1.6
that each blow-up of the induced exact sub-system
f:{\mathcal{V}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
has the Sierpiński carpet Julia set.
∎

6.
Topology of growing continua

To construct invariant graphs in extremal chains, we first study their topology.

Let
f
be a rational map with
J_{f}\not=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Suppose that
K
is a periodic level-
(n+1)
(
n\geq 0
) extremal chain of
f
with period
p\geq 1
, and
E
is the union of all periodic level-
n
extremal chains contained in
K
. By Lemma
3.3
,
E
is an
f^{p}
-invariant continuum, and
K
is generated by
E
in the sense that

K=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}},

where
E_{k}
is the component of
f^{-kp}(E)
containing
E
.

Due to the inductive construction mentioned above, all results about extremal chains can be proved by induction on levels. To improve the clarity of the proofs and ensure wider accessibility, we will adopt a more general framework for our discussions in this section.

By a
growing continuum
of
f
, we mean a continuum
K\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
together with a continuum
E\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
\partial E\subset J_{f}
,
f(E)\subset E
, and

(6.1)

K=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}},

where
E_{k}
is the component of
f^{-k}(E)
containing
E
. We call
E
the
generator
of
K
.

Let
P
be a finite marked set. Since
E_{k}\subset E_{k+1}
, according to Corollary
4.1
(2), there exists an integer
k_{0}\geq 0
such that
E_{k_{0}}
is a skeleton of
E_{k}
rel
P
for all
k>k_{0}
. Note that
f(E_{k_{0}})\subset E_{k_{0}}
. Then
K
is also a growing continuum generated by
E_{k_{0}}
. Therefore, we may always assume that
E
is a skeleton of
E_{k}
for all
k>0
.

6.1.
Local connectivity of extremal chains

Let
f
be a PCF rational map. By Theorem
1.5
, the maximal Fatou chains of
f
are locally connected since they are stable sets. In this subsection, we aim to prove the local connectivity of extremal chains, or more generally, growing continua.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
K\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a growing continuum generated by
E
. Suppose that
E
is locally connected. Then
K
is locally connected.

According to Lemma
A.4
, we need to consider the components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K
. It is worth noting that any component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K
is contained in a unique component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{k}
for every
k\geq 0
.

A nested sequence
\{\Omega_{k}\}
is called an
end
of
K
if
\Omega_{k}
is a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{k}
and
\Omega_{k+1}\subset\Omega_{k}
for every
k\geq 0
. An end
\{\Omega_{k}\}
is called
marked
if
\Omega_{k}\cap P_{f}\not=\emptyset
for all
k\geq 0
. There exist finitely many marked ends.

Since
E_{k+1}
is a component of
f^{-1}(E_{k})
, for each component
\Omega_{k+1}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{k+1}
, there exists a unique component
\Omega_{k}^{\prime}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{k}
such that
f(\partial\Omega_{k+1})=\partial\Omega_{k}^{\prime}
. Moreover,
f:\Omega_{k+1}\to\Omega_{k}^{\prime}
is a homeomorphism if
\Omega_{k}^{\prime}\cap P_{f}=\emptyset
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\{\Omega_{k}\}
be an end of
K
. For each
k\geq 0
, let
\Omega_{k}^{\prime}
be the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{k}
such that
f(\partial\Omega_{k+1})=\partial\Omega_{k}^{\prime}
. Then
\Omega^{\prime}_{k+1}\subset\Omega^{\prime}_{k}
for every sufficiently large integer
k
.

Proof.

There exists an integer
k_{0}\geq 0
such that, either
\Omega_{k+1}
avoids
f^{-1}(E_{k})
for each
k\geq k_{0}
and hence
f(\Omega_{k+1})=\Omega^{\prime}_{k}
, or
\Omega_{k+1}
contains a component of
f^{-1}(E_{k})
for each
k\geq k_{0}
.

In the former case, it is clear that
\Omega^{\prime}_{k+1}\subset\Omega^{\prime}_{k}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
.

In the latter case, let
W_{k}
be the component of
\Omega_{k+1}\setminus f^{-1}(E_{k})
whose boundary contains
\partial\Omega_{k+1}
. Then
f:W_{k}\to\Omega^{\prime}_{k}
is proper, and
W_{k}
contains critical points of
f
. Note that there exists an integer
k_{1}\geq k_{0}
such that each
W_{k}
contains the same critical points of
f
for all
k\geq k_{1}
. Thus, all
\Omega^{\prime}_{k}
share common critical values of
f
. This implies that
\Omega^{\prime}_{k+1}\subset\Omega^{\prime}_{k}
for
k\geq k_{1}
.
∎

By Proposition
6.1
, we obtain a self-map
f_{\star}
on the collection of ends of
K
. This map is defined by
f_{\star}\{\Omega_{k}\}=\{\Omega_{k}^{\prime}\}
if
f(\partial\Omega_{k+1})=\partial\Omega_{k}^{\prime}
for each sufficiently large integer
k
. The proof of Proposition
6.1
shows that the image of a marked end remains marked. Hence, marked ends are eventually
f_{\star}
-periodic. Moreover, if
\{\Omega_{k}^{\prime}\}=f_{\star}^{N}\{\Omega_{k}\}
is not marked, then for each sufficiently large integer
k
, the map
f^{N}:\Omega_{k+N}\to\Omega^{\prime}_{k}
is conformal.

Lemma 6.2
.

There exist constants
M>0
and
\rho>1
with the following properties. Let
\{\Omega_{k}\}
be an end of
K
such that
f_{\star}^{N}\{\Omega_{k}\}
is not marked for an integer
N\geq 1
. Then

\textup{diam}\bigg(\bigcap_{k\geq 0}\overline{\Omega_{k}}\bigg)\leq M\rho^{-N}.

Consequently,
\bigcap_{k\geq 0}\overline{\Omega_{k}}
is a singleton if
\{\Omega_{k}\}
is
f_{\star}
-wandering.

Proof.

Recall that
E
is a skeleton of each
E_{k}
rel
P_{f}
.
By Lemma
A.4
and the fact that
E_{1}
is locally connected, the homotopic diameters of the components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{1}
that avoid
P_{f}
are bounded above by a constant
M_{1}
. Since
f_{\star}^{N}\{\Omega_{k}\}
is not marked, there exists an integer
k_{0}\geq 1
such that
f^{N}(\Omega_{k})\cap P_{f}=\emptyset
for every
k\geq k_{0}
.

Fix any integer
k>k_{0}
.
For each
0\leq i\leq k
, we denote
W_{i}
as the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{i}
such that
\partial W_{i}=f^{k-i}(\partial\Omega_{k})
. Let

n_{k}\geq 1
be the minimal integer with
W_{n_{k}}\cap P_{f}=\emptyset
, and let
D_{1}
be the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{1}
containing
W_{n_{k}}
.

We claim that
D_{1}\cap P_{f}=\emptyset
. If
n_{k}=1
, then
D_{1}=W_{n_{k}}
, and this claim is true. If
n_{k}>1
, we have
W_{n_{k}-1}\cap P_{f}\neq\emptyset
by the choice of
n_{k}
. Let
D
denote the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
containing
W_{n_{k}-1}
. Since
E
is a skeleton of
E_{n_{k}-1}
, it follows that
D\cap P_{f}=W_{n_{k}-1}\cap P_{f}
. Thus, there exist an annulus
A\subset D\setminus P_{f}
bounded by
\partial D
and a Jordan curve in
W_{n_{k}-1}
. Let
A_{1}
be the component of
f^{-1}(A)
containing
\partial W_{n_{k}}
. Then
A_{1}\cap P_{f}=\emptyset
and
A_{1}\cup W_{n_{k}}=D_{1}
. The claim is proved.

By this claim, the homotopic diameter of
D_{1}
is bounded above by
M_{1}
. Due to the choices of
k
and
n_{k}
, the map
f^{k-n_{k}}:\Omega_{k}\to W_{n_{k}}
is conformal, and
k-n_{k}\geq N
. Thus, this lemma follows directly from Lemma
A.1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

Given any component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K
, let
\{\Omega_{k}(D)\}
be the end of
K
such that
D\subset\Omega_{k}(D)
for all
k\geq 0
. By Lemma
6.2
, the end
\{\Omega_{k}(D)\}
is eventually
f_{\star}
-periodic and marked.

First, assume that
\{\Omega_{k}\}=\{\Omega_{k}(D)\}
is periodic under
f_{\star}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that the period is one and that
f(\partial\Omega_{k})=\partial\Omega_{k-1}
for every
k\geq 1
. Let
\gamma_{0}\subset\Omega_{0}
be a Jordan curve separating
\partial\Omega_{0}
from
P_{f}\cap\Omega_{0}
. Then there exists a unique component
\gamma_{1}
of
f^{-1}(\gamma_{0})
contained in
\Omega_{1}
that separates
\partial\Omega_{1}
from
P_{f}\cap\Omega_{1}=P_{f}\cap\Omega_{0}
. Thus, there exists a homeomorphism
\theta_{0}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
isotopic to
id
rel
P_{f}
, such that
\theta_{0}(\gamma_{0})=\gamma_{1}
. By lifting (Lemma
A.2
), we obtain a sequence of homeomorphisms
\{\theta_{k}\}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
isotopic to
id
rel
P_{f}
, such that

f\circ\theta_{k+1}=\theta_{k}\circ f\quad\text{ on }\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}.

Set
\phi_{k}=\theta_{k}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{0}
. Then
\gamma_{k+1}=\phi_{k}(\gamma_{0})
. By Lemma
A.3
,
\{\phi_{k}\}
uniformly converges to a quotient map
\varphi
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Denote
\gamma=\varphi(\gamma_{0})
. Then
f(\gamma)=\gamma
, and
\gamma
is locally connected.

According to Lemma
A.1
, the Hausdorff distance between
\partial\Omega_{k}
and
\gamma_{k}
converges to zero. Consequently,
\partial\Omega_{k}\to\gamma
as
k\to\infty
. Thus,
\gamma\subset K
. Then
D
lies in a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\gamma
.

We claim that
D
is simply a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\gamma
. If this is false, there exist a point
z\in\partial D
not in
\gamma
and a neighborhood
W
of
z
disjoint from
\partial\Omega_{k}
for every sufficiently large integer
k
. Since
W\cap D\neq\emptyset
, it follows that
W\subset\Omega_{k}
for every
k\geq 0
. In particular,
W
is disjoint from every
E_{k}
, and hence avoids
K=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}}
. Thus,
W\subset D
, a contradiction.

This claim implies that
\partial D
is locally connected since
\gamma
is locally connected.

Now, suppose that
\{\tilde{\Omega}_{k}\}=\{\Omega_{k}(D)\}
is strictly eventually periodic under
f_{\star}
. Let
q>0
be the smallest integer such that
\{\Omega_{k}\}=f_{\star}^{q}(\{\tilde{\Omega}_{k}\})
is periodic.

Let
\tilde{\gamma}_{q}
be the component of
f^{-q}(\gamma_{0})
contained in
\tilde{\Omega}_{q}
that separates
\partial\tilde{\Omega}_{q}
from
\tilde{\Omega}_{q}\cap f^{-q}(P_{f})
. For all
k\geq 0
, define a homeomorphism
\tilde{\phi}_{k}:=\theta_{q+k}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{q}
.
Then

(1)

f^{q}\circ\tilde{\phi}_{k}(z)={\phi}_{k}\circ f^{q}(z)
for every
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
;

(2)

\tilde{\gamma}_{q+k+1}:=\tilde{\phi}_{k}(\tilde{\gamma}_{q})
is contained in
\tilde{\Omega}_{q+k+1}
and isotopic to
\tilde{\gamma}_{q}
rel
f^{-q}(P_{f})
.

By a similar argument as in the periodic case, we can prove that the map
\tilde{\phi}_{k}
uniformly converges to a quotient map
\tilde{\varphi}
, and
D
is a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\tilde{\varphi}(\tilde{\gamma}_{q})
. Thus,
\partial D
is locally connected.

It remains to show that the diameters of the components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K
tend to
0
.

Given any
\epsilon>0
, there exist only finitely many ends
\{\Omega_{k}\}
with
{\rm diam}(\bigcap_{k\geq 0}\overline{\Omega_{k}})\geq\epsilon
by Lemma
6.2
. Therefore, we simply need to consider the components
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K
for which
\{\Omega_{k}(D)\}
are such ends.
As shown above,
D
is a complementary component of a curve
\gamma_{D}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\partial\Omega_{k}(D)
. Since there exist finitely many curves
\gamma_{D}
, and only finitely many components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\gamma_{D}
have diameters larger than
\epsilon
, we complete the proof of the lemma.
∎

Theorem 6.1
.

Every extremal chain of a PCF rational map is locally connected.

Proof.

Every level-
0
extremal chain of a PCF rational map
f
is clearly locally connected. Inductively, for
n\geq 0
, assume that level-
n
extremal chains are locally connected. If
K
is a periodic level-
(n+1)
extremal chain, then it is locally connected by Lemma
6.1
and the induction.

Now, suppose that
K^{\prime}
is a strictly preperiodic level-
(n+1)
extremal chain such that
f^{q}(K^{\prime})=K
, which is periodic with period
p
. Let
E
be the union of all periodic level-
n
extremal chains contained in
K
, and let
E_{k}
denote the component of
f^{-pk}(E)
containing
E
for every
k\geq 0
.
We may assume that
E
is a skeleton of every
E_{k}
rel
P_{f}
. Then for each
k\geq 0
, there exists a unique component
E_{k}^{\prime}
of
f^{-q}(E_{k})
contained in
K^{\prime}
such that
E_{k}^{\prime}\subset E_{k+1}^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime}=\overline{\bigcup_{k\geq 0}E_{k}^{\prime}}
.

The ends for
K^{\prime}
can be similarly defined as in the periodic case. If
\{\Omega_{k}^{\prime}\}
is an end of
K^{\prime}
, then there exists a unique end
\{\Omega_{k}\}
of
K
such that
f^{q}(\partial\Omega_{k}^{\prime})=\partial\Omega_{k}
for every sufficiently large integer
k
. Therefore, applying a similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, we can establish the local connectivity of
K^{\prime}
. The details are omitted.
∎

6.2.
Growing curves

Let
f
be a PCF rational map, and let
K
be a growing continuum generated by an
f
-invariant continuum
E
. As before,
E_{k}
denotes the component of
f^{-k}(E)
containing
E
, and
E
is assumed to be a skeleton of
E_{k}
(rel
P_{f}
) for every
k\geq 0
.

A curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to K
is called a
growing curve
if, for any small number
\epsilon>0
, there exists an integer
k\geq 0
such that
\gamma[0,1-\epsilon]\subset E_{k}
. The point
\gamma(1)
is called the
terminal
of
\gamma
.

By definition, any curve in
E_{k}
is growing, including the trivial ones. Here, a curve is
trival
if its image is a singleton. Moreover, the image or lift of a growing curve in
K
under
f
is also a growing curve.

Growing curves will be crucial in constructing invariant graphs on a maximal Fatou chain in the next section. To this end, we aim to establish their existence through the following lemma.

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose that
E
is locally connected. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

Any point of
K
is the terminal of a growing curve in
K
;

(2)

For any two points
a
and
b
in distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K
, there exist two growing curves
\delta_{\pm}\subset K
with the same terminal, such that
E\cup\delta_{+}\cup\delta_{-}
separates
a
from
b
.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}:[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be two curves with
\gamma_{1}(1)=\gamma_{2}(0)
. The
concatenation

\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma_{2}
is a curve parameterized by

\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma_{2}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\gamma_{1}(2t)&\hbox{if $t\in[0,1/2]$,}\\
\gamma_{2}(2t-1)&\hbox{if $t\in[1/2,1]$.}\end{array}\right.

If
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}
can be successively concatenated, their concatenation is parameterized by

(6.2)

\text{$\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{n}(t):=\gamma_{1}\cdot(\gamma_{2}\cdot(\cdots(\gamma_{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{n})))(t)$,\quad$t\in[0,1]$}.

Proposition 6.2
.

Suppose that
E
is locally connected. Then there exists a family
\Gamma
of growing curves in
K
such that any point of
K
is the terminal of an element in
\Gamma
, and that
\Gamma
is sequentially compact under uniform convergence, i.e., any infinite sequence in
\Gamma
has a convergent subsequence whose limit is also in
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Since
E_{1}
is locally connected, each point
w\in E_{1}
can be joined to
E
by a curve
\beta_{w}\subset E_{1}
with the following conditions: if
w\in E
, then
\beta_{w}\equiv w
; otherwise, it holds that
\beta_{w}(0)\in E
and
\beta_{w}(0,1]\cap E=\emptyset
. By Lemma
A.6
, we can require that
\Gamma_{0}=\{\beta_{w}:w\in E_{1}\}
is equicontinuous. Thus, the homotopic diameters of curves in
\Gamma_{0}
are bounded above by a constant.

For any integer
k\geq 1
and any point
z\in E_{k+1}
, set
w:=f^{k}(z)\in E_{1}
. If
w\in E_{0}
, define
\beta_{z}\equiv z
. Otherwise, since
E
is a skeleton of
E_{k}
rel
P_{f}
, we have
\beta_{w}(0,1]\cap P_{f}=\emptyset
. This implies that
\beta_{w}
has a unique lift by
f^{k}
based at
z
, which is defined as
\beta_{z}
. Since
\Gamma_{0}
is equicontinuous, the collection
\Gamma_{k}:=\{\beta_{z},z\in E_{k+1}\}
is also equicontinuous. According to Lemma
A.1
, each curve in
\Gamma_{k}
has a diameter bounded above by
M/\rho^{k}
for some constants
M>0
and
\rho>1
.

Now, for every
k\geq 1
and any point
z\in E_{k+1}
, we obtain a growing curve
\gamma_{z}:=\beta_{0}\cdot\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}
that joins
E
to
z
such that
\beta_{i}\in\Gamma_{i}
for every
i=0,\ldots,k
. By its parameterization given in (
6.2
), it follows that

(6.3)

\gamma_{z}\bigg[0,1-\frac{1}{2^{k}}\bigg]\subset E_{k}\quad\text{ for every }k\geq 1.

We claim that the family of curves
\Gamma_{\infty}:=\{\gamma_{z}:z\in\bigcup_{k\geq 1}E_{k}\}
is equicontinuous.
Given any
\epsilon>0
, there exists an integer
N>0
such that
M/(\rho^{N-1}(\rho-1))<\epsilon
. Moreover, for every
k\geq 0
, there exists
\delta_{k}>0
such that
|\beta(t_{1})-\beta(t_{2})|<\epsilon
if
|t_{1}-t_{2}|<\delta_{k}
for any curve
\beta\in\Gamma_{k}
. Set
\delta:=\min\{\delta_{0},\ldots,\delta_{N}\}
. Let
\gamma=\beta_{0}\cdot\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}
be any element in
\Gamma_{\infty}
. If
k\leq N
, according to the parameterization of
\gamma
, we have

(6.4)

|\gamma(t_{1})-\gamma(t_{2})|<2\epsilon\quad\text{ as }|t_{1}-t_{2}|<\delta/2^{N+1}.

In the case of
k>N
, the diameter of
\gamma[1-1/2^{N},1]=\beta_{N}\cdots\beta_{k}
is bounded above by
M/\rho^{N}+\cdots+M/\rho^{k}<M/(\rho^{N-1}(\rho-1))<\epsilon
. Thus,
|\gamma(t_{1})-\gamma(t_{2})|<\epsilon
when
t_{1},t_{2}\geq 1-1/2^{N}
. If
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,1-1/2^{N+1}]
, then (
6.4
) holds. Thus, the claim is proved.

Let
\Gamma
be the union of
\Gamma_{\infty}
and the limit of every uniformly convergent sequence in
\Gamma_{\infty}
. Then
\Gamma
is also equicontinuous. By the Ascoli-Arzelà theorem,
\Gamma
is a normal family. If
\gamma
is the
limit of a uniformly convergent sequence in
\Gamma
, then there exists a sequence of curves in
\Gamma_{\infty}
that also
uniformly converges to
\gamma
. Thus,
\Gamma
is sequentially compact. By (
6.3
), for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have
\gamma[0,1-1/2^{k}]\subset E_{k}
for every
k\geq 0
. Hence,
\Gamma
consists of growing curves in
K
.

Fix a point
z\in K
. If
z\in E_{k}
for some
k\geq 0
, a curve in
\Gamma_{\infty}
joins
E
to
z
. Otherwise, there exists a point
z_{k}\in E_{k}
for every
k
such that
z_{k}\to z
as
k\to\infty
. For each
k
, let
\gamma_{k}
be a curve in
\Gamma_{\infty}
joining
E
to
z_{k}
. By taking a subsequence if necessary, the curve
\gamma_{k}
uniformly converges to a curve
\gamma\in\Gamma
, which joins
E
to
z
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

Statement (1) follows directly from Proposition
6.2
.

(2) If
a
and
b
belong to distinct components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{m}
for some
m\geq 0
, we can choose the required curves
\delta_{\pm}
in
E_{m}
since
E_{m}
is locally connected. Thus, we assume that there exists an end
\{\Omega_{k}\}
of
K
such that
a,b\in\Omega_{k}
for every
k\geq 0
.

Let
U_{a}
be the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus K
containing
a
. Then
U_{a}
is contained in each
\Omega_{k}
. Since
K
is locally connected by Lemma
6.1
, it follows that
\partial U_{a}
is locally connected. Let
\eta:\mbox{$\mathbb{R}$}/\mbox{$\mathbb{Z}$}\to\partial U_{a}
be a parameterization of
\partial U_{a}
.

A curve
\gamma
with endpoints in
E
is said to
split
\{a,b\}
(
rel
E
) if
E
contains a curve
\alpha
with the same endpoints as those of
\gamma
such that
\gamma\cdot\alpha^{-1}
is not contractible in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\{a,b\}
. Note that if
\gamma
splits
\{a,b\}
, then
\gamma\cdot\alpha^{-1}
is not contractible in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\{a,b\}
for
any
curve
\alpha\subset E
with the same endpoints as those of
\gamma
.

According to Proposition
6.2
, for any
t\in\mbox{$\mathbb{R}$}/\mbox{$\mathbb{Z}$}
, there exists a growing curve
\delta_{t}\in\Gamma
with
\delta_{t}(0)\in E
and
\delta_{t}(1)=\eta(t)\in\partial U_{a}
. Then for every
t\in\mbox{$\mathbb{R}$}/\mbox{$\mathbb{Z}$}
, we have two curves (see Figure
10
)

\ell^{-}_{t}:=\delta_{0}\cdot\eta[0,t]\cdot\delta_{t}^{-1}\text{\quad and\quad}\ell^{+}_{t}:=\delta_{t}\cdot\eta[t,1]\cdot\delta_{0}^{-1}.

Since
\ell_{t}^{-}\cdot\ell_{t}^{+}=\delta_{0}\cdot\eta\cdot\delta_{0}^{-1}
, which splits
\{a,b\}
, at least one of
\ell_{t}^{+}
and
\ell_{t}^{-}
splits
\{a,b\}
.

Note that
\ell_{1}^{-}=\delta_{0}\cdot\eta\cdot\delta_{0}^{-1}
, which splits
\{a,b\}
. Let
t_{*}
denote the infimum of
t\in[0,1]
such that
\ell_{t}^{-}
splits
\{a,b\}
. Then there exists a sequence of decreasing numbers
\{t_{n}\}\subset[t_{*},1]
such that
t_{n}\to t_{*}
and
\ell_{t_{n}}^{-}
splits
\{a,b\}
. Let
\{s_{n}\}\subset[0,t_{*}]
be a sequence of increasing numbers converging to
t_{*}
. It follows that each
\ell_{s_{n}}^{+}
splits
\{a,b\}
. Here,
t_{n}
or
s_{n}
are possibly constant for sufficiently large
n
.

Figure 10.
Split
\{a,b\}
by growing curves.

We claim that the curve
\delta_{s_{n}}\cdot\eta[s_{n},t_{n}]\cdot\delta_{t_{n}}^{-1}
splits
\{a,b\}
for each
n\geq 1
; see Figure
10
. Otherwise, since

\ell_{t_{n}}^{-}=\delta_{0}\cdot\eta[0,t_{n}]\cdot\delta_{t_{n}}^{-1}=(\delta_{0}\cdot\eta[0,s_{n}]\cdot\delta_{s_{n}}^{-1})\cdot(\delta_{s_{n}}\cdot\eta[s_{n},t_{n}]\cdot\delta_{t_{n}}^{-1})=\ell_{s_{n}}^{-}\cdot(\delta_{s_{n}}\cdot\eta[s_{n},t_{n}]\cdot\delta_{t_{n}}^{-1})

splits
\{a,b\}
, it follows that
\ell_{s_{n}}^{-}
splits
\{a,b\}
, which contradicts the choice of
t_{*}
.

Since
\{\delta_{s_{n}}\}
and
\{\delta_{t_{n}}\}
are selected from a sequentially compact family
{\Gamma}
of growing curves by Proposition
6.2
, we may assume that
\{\delta_{s_{n}}\}
and
\{\delta_{t_{n}}\}
uniformly converge to growing curves
\delta_{-}
and
\delta_{+}
, respectively. Consequently, both
\delta_{\pm}
join
E
to
\eta(t_{*})
, and the curves
\delta_{s_{n}}\cdot\eta[s_{n},t_{*}]\cdot\delta_{-}^{-1}
and
\delta_{+}\cdot\eta[t_{*},t_{n}]\cdot\delta_{t_{n}}^{-1}
do not split
\{a,b\}
for each sufficiently large integer
n
. Moreover, since

\delta_{s_{n}}\cdot\eta[s_{n},t_{n}]\cdot\delta_{t_{n}}^{-1}=(\delta_{s_{n}}\cdot\eta[s_{n},t_{*}]\cdot\delta_{-}^{-1})\cdot(\delta_{-}\cdot\delta_{+}^{-1})\cdot(\delta_{+}\cdot\eta[t_{*},t_{n}]\cdot\delta_{t_{n}}^{-1})

splits
\{a,b\}
by the claim above, it follows that
\delta_{-}\cdot\delta_{+}^{-1}
splits
\{a,b\}
, and the lemma is proved.
∎

6.3.
Accesses within a growing continuum

In order to construct invariant graphs within extremal chains, we need a sufficient number of preperiodic growing arcs. These arcs will be constructed in this and the next subsections.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map. Suppose that
K
is a growing continuum generated by an
f
-invariant and locally connected continuum
E
. We continue to assume that
E
is a skeleton (rel
P
) of all
E_{k}
, where
E_{k}
denotes the component of
f^{-k}(E)
containing
E
.

Let
P_{0}=P\setminus E
. Then
P_{0}\cap E_{k}=\emptyset
for every
k\geq 0
since
E
is a skeleton of
E_{k}
. Two growing curves
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
in
K
with a common terminal
z
are called
equivalent
if there exist an integer
k\geq 0
and a curve
\delta\subset E_{k}
that joins
\alpha_{1}(0)
to
\alpha_{2}(0)
, such that the closed curve
\gamma:=\alpha_{1}^{-1}\cdot\delta\cdot\alpha_{2}
is contractible in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{0}
, i.e., there exists a continuous map
H:\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that the family of curves
\{H_{s}=H(\cdot,s),s\in[0,1]\}
satisfies

H_{0}=\gamma,\quad H_{1}\equiv\{z\},\quad H_{s}(0)=z,\quad\textup{ and }\quad H_{s}(0,1)\cap P_{0}=\emptyset,\quad\forall s\in(0,1).

This is clearly an equivalence relation. Note that
\gamma
possibly passes through some points in
P\cap E
.

z
z_{1}

Figure 11.
The equivalent growing curves
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
, with marked points colored red.

For each
k\geq 0
, any two growing curves in
E_{k}
with a common terminal are equivalent. A growing curve
\alpha
is called
infinitely growing
if it is not equivalent to any curve (including trivial ones) in
E_{k}
for every
k\geq 0
. By definition, infinitely growing curves cannot be trivial. In Figure
11
, the curve
\alpha_{3}
is infinitely growing to
z_{1}
, while
\alpha_{0}
is not.
An
access
to
z
is an equivalence class of all infinitely growing curves to
z
.

By the
interior
of a curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, we mean the set
\gamma(0,1)
. The sub-curve
\gamma|_{[t_{1},t_{2}]}
of

\gamma
means a curve whose image equals
\gamma[t_{1},t_{2}]
. An (open) arc
\gamma
is called a
crosscut
of a domain
U\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
if
\gamma\subset\overline{U}
with only the two endpoints in
\partial U
.

Recall that two curves
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}:[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
are homotopic rel
P
with endpoints fixed if there exists a continuous map
H:[0,1]\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that
H_{0}=\gamma_{0},H_{1}=\gamma_{1}
, and each curve
H_{s},s\in[0,1],
has the same endpoints as
\gamma_{0}
with its interior disjoint from
P
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\subset K
be two growing curves with a common terminal
z
.

(1)

The curves
\alpha
and
\alpha|_{[t,1]}
are equivalent for any
t\in(0,1)
.

(2)

If
\alpha(t,1)\cap\alpha^{\prime}(t,1)\neq\emptyset
for any
t\in(0,1)
, then
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are equivalent.

(3)

If
\alpha
is infinitely growing, then for every sufficiently large integer
k
, there exists a number
t_{k}\in(0,1)
such that
\alpha(t_{k})\in E_{k}
and
\alpha(t_{k},1)\cap E_{k}=\emptyset
. Moreover, the curve
\alpha|_{[t_{k},1]}
contains an arc
\beta_{k}
that is homotopic to
\alpha|_{[t_{k},1]}
rel
P
with endpoints fixed. In particular,
\beta_{k}
lies in the same access to
z
as
\alpha
.

(4)

Suppose that
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
belong to the same access to
z
, with their interiors disjoint from
P
. Then there exist an integer
m\geq 0
and a continuous family of curves
\{\alpha_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
such that
\alpha_{0}=\alpha
,
\alpha_{1}=\alpha^{\prime}
, and each
\alpha_{s}
joins
E_{m}
to
z
with its interior disjoint from
P
.

Proof.

We fix a disk
W
such that
z\in W
and
(W\setminus\{z\})\cap P=\emptyset
.

(1) The curve
\alpha|_{[0,t]}\subset E_{k}
for some
k
, and
\alpha^{-1}\cdot\alpha|_{[0,t]}\cdot\alpha|_{[t,1]}
is contractible.

(2) There exist some
t,t^{\prime}\in(0,1)
such that
\alpha(t)=\alpha^{\prime}(t^{\prime})
and
\alpha|_{[t,1]},\alpha^{\prime}|_{[t^{\prime},1]}
lie in
W
. It follows that
\alpha|_{[t,1]}
is equivalent to
\alpha^{\prime}|_{[t^{\prime},1]}
, and thus
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are equivalent by statement (1).

(3) To prove the existence of such
t_{k}
’s, suppose, to the contrary, that
\alpha(s_{n})\in E_{k}
for a sequence
\{s_{n}\}\subset(0,1)
that converges to
1
and a certain
k\geq 0
. Then
z\in E_{k}
. Since
E_{k}
is locally arcwise connected by Lemma
A.5
, there exists a curve
\gamma\subset E_{k}\cap W
(possibly trivial) joining a certain
\alpha(s_{n})
to
z
. Thus,
\gamma^{-1}\cdot\alpha|_{[s_{n},1]}
is contractible, which contradicts the assumption that
\alpha
is infinitely growing.

By this statement, we can find
k_{0}>0
such that
\alpha|_{[t_{k},1]}\subset W
and
z\not\in\alpha[t_{k},1)
for each
k>k_{0}
.
It follows that
\alpha|_{[t_{k},1]}
contains an arc
\beta_{k}
with endpoints
\alpha(t_{k})
and
z
. Then
\beta_{k}\subset W
, and its interior avoids
P
.
Hence,
\beta_{k}
is homotopic to
\alpha|_{[t_{k},1]}
rel
P
with endpoints fixed.

(4) If
\alpha^{\prime}
is a sub-curve of
\alpha
, the conclusion is immediate. Thus, it suffices to prove the statement for a pair of sub-curves
\alpha|_{[t,1]}
and
\alpha^{\prime}|_{[t^{\prime},1]}
of
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
, respectively.

If
\alpha(t,1)\cap\alpha^{\prime}(t,1)\not=\emptyset
for any
t\in(0,1)
, then there exist
t,t^{\prime}\in(0,1)
such that
\alpha(t)=\alpha^{\prime}(t^{\prime})
and
\alpha|_{[t,1]},\alpha^{\prime}|_{[t^{\prime},1]}\subset W
. Since the interiors of
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
avoid
P
, it follows that
\alpha|_{[t,1]}
and
\alpha^{\prime}|_{[t^{\prime},1]}
are homotopic rel
P
with endpoints fixed. Hence, statement (4) holds in this case.

Otherwise, by statement (3), replacing
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
with their sub-curves, we can assume that
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are arcs with disjoint interiors such that
\alpha(0),\alpha^{\prime}(0)\in E
and
\alpha(0,1),\alpha^{\prime}(0,1)\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
.

Let
D
and
D^{\prime}
be the components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
containing
\alpha(0,1)
and
\alpha^{\prime}(0,1)
, respectively.
We claim that
D=D^{\prime}
.
If
z\not\in E
, the claim is immediate. Assume
z\in E
. Since
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are infinitely growing, each component of
D\setminus\alpha
and
D^{\prime}\setminus\alpha^{\prime}
contains marked points. This implies
D=D^{\prime}
since
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
belong to the same access. The claim is proved.

Since
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are arcs with disjoint interiors and belong to the same access, there exists a simply connected domain
D_{*}
of
D\setminus(\alpha\cup\alpha^{\prime})
such that
D_{*}\cap P=\emptyset
and
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\subset\partial D_{*}
. Then the desired family of curves
\{\alpha_{s}\}
can be easily chosen within
\overline{D_{*}}
.
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Suppose that
G
is a locally connected skeleton of
E
. Let
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\subset K
be two infinitely growing curves in the same access to
z
, with their initial points on
G
and their interiors disjoint from
P
.
Then there exists a continuous family of curves
\{\alpha_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
joining
G
to
z
, such that the interior of each
\alpha_{s}
is disjoint from
P
.

Proof.

Let
\{\beta_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
be the family of curves derived from Proposition
6.3
(4) such that
\alpha_{0}=\beta_{0}
and
\alpha_{1}=\beta_{1}
. Then the curve
\delta
defined by
\delta(s):=\beta_{s}(0)
lies in a certain
E_{m}
. We will construct a continuous family of curves
\{\eta_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
such that

\eta_{s}(0)=\delta(s),\quad\eta_{s}(1)\in G,\quad\text{ and }\quad\begin{cases}\eta_{s}\equiv\eta_{s}(0)&\text{if }\eta_{s}(0)\in G,\\
\eta_{s}[0,1)\text{ avoids }P&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Then Proposition
6.4
holds by taking
\alpha_{s}:=\eta_{s}^{-1}\cdot\beta_{s},s\in[0,1]
.

Set
X=\{s\in[0,1]:\delta(s)\in G\}
. Since
\delta(0),\delta(1)\in G
, each component of
[0,1]\setminus X
is an open interval. If
s\in X
, define
\eta_{s}\equiv\delta(s)
. Let
(s_{1},s_{2})
be a component of
[0,1]\setminus X
. Then there exists a component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus G
such that
\delta(s_{1}),\delta(s_{2})\in\partial D
and
\delta(s_{1},s_{2})\subset D
.

Since
\delta\subset E_{m}
and
G
is a skeleton of
E_{m}
, it follows that
\delta(s_{1},s_{2})
avoids
P
and does not separate
P
. Consequently, there exists a disk
D^{\prime}
compactly contained in
D
such that
P\cap D\subset D^{\prime}
and
\delta(s_{1},s_{2})
is contained in the annulus
D\setminus\overline{D^{\prime}}
.
Thus, we can choose a continuous family of curves
\{\eta_{s}\}_{s\in[s_{1},s_{2}]}
such that
\eta_{s}(0)=\delta(s)
,
\eta_{s}(1)\subset\partial D\subset G
, and
\eta_{s}(0,1)\subset D\setminus\overline{D^{\prime}}
for any
s\in(s_{1},s_{2})
, and that
\eta_{s_{i}}\equiv\delta(s_{i})
for
i=1,2
. This completes the construction of
\{\eta_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
.
∎

One main result of this subsection is the finiteness of accesses.

Lemma 6.4
.

For any
z\in K
, there exist finitely many accesses to
z
.

Proof.

Let
\Delta
be a finite collection of infinitely growing curves in
K
that lie in pairwise distinct accesses to
z
. It suffices to show that
\#\Delta\leq(\#P)^{2}
.

By Proposition
6.3
(1)–(3), we may assume that all elements in
\Delta
are arcs with pairwise disjoint interiors, such that
\alpha(0,1)\subset D_{\alpha}
and
\alpha(0)\in\partial D_{\alpha}
for every
\alpha\in\Delta
, where
D_{\alpha}
is a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{m}
and
m
is a sufficiently large integer independent of
\alpha
.

Note that every component
D_{\alpha}
must intersect
P
. Thus, there exist at most
\#P
such components. Suppose that a certain
D_{\alpha}
contains the interiors of
k
arcs in
\Delta
. Then these arcs divide
D_{\alpha}
into
k
or
k+1
simply connected domains, each intersecting
P
. It follows that
k\leq\#P
. Therefore, we have
\#\Delta\leq(\#P)^{2}
.
∎

In the following, we will construct numerous preperiodic growing arcs in
K
based on the above lemma. We first prove a lifting property for accesses.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\alpha\subset K
be an infinitely growing curve with terminal
z
. Then

(1)

the curve
f\circ\alpha
is also infinitely growing with terminal
f(z)
;

(2)

if
\beta
and
f\circ\alpha
lie in the same access to
f(z)
, then there exists a curve
\tilde{\beta}
in the same access as
\alpha
such that
f\circ\tilde{\beta}=\beta
.

Proof.

(1) To the contrary, suppose that
f\circ\alpha
is not infinitely growing. Then
z
must be contained in some
E_{k_{0}}
.
By Proposition
6.3
(3), for each sufficiently large integer
k
, there exists a number
t_{k}\in(0,1)
such that
\alpha(t_{k})\in E_{k}
and
\alpha(t_{k},1)\cap E_{k}=\emptyset
. It follows that
f\circ\alpha(t_{k})\in E_{k-1}
and
f\circ\alpha(t_{k},1)\subset D_{k-1}
for a component
D_{k-1}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E_{k-1}
.

Note that the diameter of
f\circ\alpha(t_{k},1)
tends to
0
as
k\to\infty
. Then there exists an arc
\gamma\subset f\circ\alpha([t_{m},1])
that is homotopic to
f\circ\alpha|_{[t_{m},1]}
rel
P
with endpoints fixed for a sufficiently large integer
m
. In particular,
\gamma
is a crosscut of
D_{m-1}
.
By homotopy lifting, we obtain a lift
\tilde{\gamma}
of
\gamma
by
f
that is homotopic to
\alpha|_{[t_{m},1]}
rel
P
with endpoints fixed. Thus,
\tilde{\gamma}
is infinitely growing.

On the other hand, since
f\circ\alpha
is assumed not to be infinitely growing, one of the two components of
D_{m-1}\setminus\gamma
, denoted by
D_{*}
, avoids
P
. Thus, there exists a component
\tilde{D}_{*}
of
f^{-1}(D_{*})
with
\tilde{\gamma}\subset\partial\tilde{D}_{*}
. Since
\tilde{D}_{*}\cap P=\emptyset
and
\partial\tilde{D}_{*}\setminus\tilde{\gamma}\subset E_{m}
,
\tilde{\gamma}
is not infinitely growing, a contradiction.

(2) By statement (1), both
\beta
and
f\circ\alpha
are infinitely growing. Then by Proposition
6.3
(3), we can find numbers
t_{0},t_{1}\in(0,1)
such that
f\circ\alpha(t_{0},1)
and
\beta(t_{1},1)
are disjoint from
P
. By Proposition
6.3
(4), there exists a continuous family of curves
\{\gamma_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
joining some
E_{m}
to
z
such that
\gamma_{0}=f\circ\alpha|_{[t_{0},1]}
,
\gamma_{1}=\beta|_{[t_{1},1]}
, and the interior of each
\gamma_{s}
is disjoint from
P
.

For any
t\in(0,1)
, the curve
\{\gamma_{s}(t):s\in[0,1]\}
has a unique lift based at the point
\alpha|_{[t_{0},1]}(t)
. Thus, by the continuity of
f
, we obtain a continuous family of lifts
\{\tilde{\gamma}_{s}\}
of
\{\gamma_{s}\}
such that each
\tilde{\gamma}_{s}
joins
E_{m+1}
to
z
with its interior avoiding
P
. This implies that
\tilde{\gamma}_{0}=\alpha|_{[t_{0},1]}
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
lie in the same access to
z
.
Since
f\circ\tilde{\gamma}_{1}=\beta|_{[t_{1},1]}
, there exists a growing curve
\tilde{\beta}
such that
f(\tilde{\beta})=\beta
and
\tilde{\beta}|_{[t_{1},1]}=\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
. Then
\alpha
and
\tilde{\beta}
lie in the same access by Proposition
6.3
(1).
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

Suppose that
E\subset J_{f}
and
G
is a locally connected and
f
-invariant continuum serving as a skeleton of
E
rel
P
. Let
\alpha\subset K
be an infinitely growing curve joining
G
to a preperiodic point
z
. Then there exists a growing arc
\beta
in
K
such that

(1)

the arc
\beta
joins
G
to
z
and lies in the same access as
\alpha
;

(2)

for any
t\in(0,1)
, there exists an integer
n_{t}>0
such that
f^{n_{t}}(\beta[0,t])\subset G
;

(3)

there exist two integers
q\geq 0
and
p\geq 1
, such that
f^{q+p}(\beta)\subset f^{q}(\beta)\cup G
and the growing curves
f^{i}(\beta),i=0,\ldots,q+p-1,
lie in pairwise distinct accesses.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.5
(1), the curves
f^{i}(\alpha),i\geq 0,
are all infinitely growing, with initial points in
G
.
According to Lemma
6.4
, there exist minimal integers
q\geq 0
and
p\geq 1
such that
f^{q+p}(\alpha)
and
f^{q}(\alpha)
lie in the same access to
w=f^{q}(z)
. Set
{\alpha}_{0}:=f^{p+q}(\alpha)
and
{\alpha}_{1}:=f^{q}(\alpha)
. Then
f^{p}(\alpha_{1})=\alpha_{0}
. By Lemma
6.5
(2), we may assume the interior of
\alpha_{0}
is disjoint from
P
. Then
\alpha_{1}
joins
G_{p}
to
z
, and its interior is also disjoint from
P
. For simplicity, set
G=G_{p}
and
E=E_{p}
.

By Proposition
6.4
, we have a continuous family of curves
\{{\alpha}_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
joining
G
to
w
such that
\alpha_{s}(0,1)\cap P=\emptyset
for all
s\in[0,1]
. Define a curve
\delta_{0}:[0,1]\to G
by
\delta_{0}(s):={\alpha}_{s}(0)
. As shown in the proof of Lemma
6.5
, there exists a continuous family of curves
\{{\alpha}_{s+1}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
joining
G_{p}
to
w
such that
f^{p}\circ{\alpha}_{s+1}={\alpha}_{s}
. Thus,
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
lie in the same access to
w
, and we obtain a curve
\delta_{1}:[0,1]\to G_{p}
defined by
\delta_{1}(s):={\alpha}_{s+1}(0)
such that
f^{p}\circ\delta_{1}=\delta_{0}
.

Inductively, for every
k\geq 1
, there exist a curve
\delta_{k}\subset G_{pk}
and a growing curve
{\alpha}_{k}
such that

(1)

f^{p}\circ\delta_{k+1}=\delta_{k}
and
\delta_{k}(1)=\delta_{k+1}(0)
;

(2)

{\alpha}_{k}(0)=\delta_{k}(0)
,
{\alpha}_{k}(1)=w
, and
f^{p}\circ{\alpha}_{k+1}={\alpha}_{k}
;

(3)

\alpha_{k}
lies in the same access as
\alpha_{0}
.

For every
m\geq 1
, define a growing curve
\ell_{m}:=\delta_{0}\cdots\delta_{m-1}\cdot\alpha_{m}
. By Proposition
6.3
(1) and point (3) above, the curves
\ell_{m}
and
\alpha_{0}
lie in the same access to
w
for every
m\geq 1
.

By Lemma
A.1
, the diameters of
\delta_{k}
and
{\alpha}_{k}
exponentially decrease to
0
. Then
{\alpha}_{k}\to w
as
k\to\infty
, and
\ell_{m}
uniformly converges to a growing curve
\beta_{q+p}\subset K
with terminal
w
as
m\to\infty
. Clearly,
f^{p}(\beta_{q+p})\subset\beta_{q+p}\cup G
, and the curves
\beta_{q+p}
and
\alpha_{0}
lie in the same access.

By successively applying Lemma
6.5
, for each
i=1,\ldots,q+p
, there exists a curve
\beta_{q+p-i}
joining
G_{i}
to
f^{q+p-i}(z)
such that
f^{i}(\beta_{q+p-i})=\beta_{q+p}
and
\beta_{q+p-i}
and
\alpha_{q+p-i}
lie in the same access to
f^{(q+p-i)}(z)
. By replacing
G
with
G_{q+p}
, the curve
\beta_{0}
satisfies all requirements of the proposition, except that it may not be an arc.

To complete the proof, it suffices to find an arc
\beta\subset\beta_{0}
joining
G
to
z
such that
f^{q+p}(\beta)\subset f^{q}(\beta)\cup G
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
q=0
.

Figure 12.
The curves
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are shown in red, and the arcs
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
in green.

Take two small disks
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
containing
z
such that
\overline{D_{1}}\subset D_{2}
and
g=f^{p}:D_{1}\to D_{2}
is a homeomorphism. Let
Y_{i}
be the closure of the component of
D_{i}\cap\beta_{0}
containing
z
for
i=1,2
. Clearly,
Y_{1}\subset Y_{2}
. Let
\ell\subset D_{2}\setminus Y_{2}
be an open arc joining
z
to a point in
\partial D_{2}\setminus\beta_{0}
. Then for each
i
, the curves
\partial D_{i}
,
Y_{i}
, and
\ell
bound a simply connected domain
\Omega_{i}
with a locally connected boundary such that
\Omega_{1}\subset\Omega_{2}
; see Figure
12
. Let
\eta_{i}=Y_{i}\cap\partial\Omega_{i}
be the curve joining
z
to some point
z_{i}\in\partial D_{i}
. Then
\eta_{1}
is the closure of a component of
\eta_{2}\setminus\{z_{1}\}
.
Since
\beta_{0}
is locally
g
-invariant near
z
, the map
g
sends
Y_{1},\eta_{1}
, and
z_{1}
homeomorphically onto
Y_{2},\eta_{2}
, and
z_{2}
, respectively.

We claim that there exists a unique arc
\lambda_{i}\subset\eta_{i}
joining
z
and
z_{i}
for
i=1,2
. The existence of such an arc follows from the local connectivity of
\eta_{i}
. The curve
(\partial\Omega_{i}\setminus\eta_{i})\cup\lambda_{i}
bounds a disk
W_{i}
containing
\Omega_{i}
. Clearly,
\eta_{i}\subset\overline{W_{i}}
. Suppose
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}
is another such arc. Then
\partial W^{\prime}_{i}\subset\overline{W_{i}}
and
\partial W_{i}\subset\overline{W^{\prime}_{i}}
. Thus,
W_{i}=W_{i}^{\prime}
, which implies
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}^{\prime}
.

Note that
g(\lambda_{1})\subset\eta_{2}
is an arc joining
z
and
z_{2}
. By the uniqueness of
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
, we have that
g(\lambda_{1})=\lambda_{2}
and
\lambda_{1}
is the sub-arc of
\lambda_{2}
from
z
to
z_{1}
.

Choose a sufficiently large integer
N
such that
G_{N}
contains
\lambda_{2}\setminus\lambda_{1}
, and define
\beta:=\lambda_{1}
. Then
\beta\subset\beta_{0}
is an arc satisfying
f^{p}(\beta)\subset\beta\cup G_{N}
. The proof is completed by replacing
G
with
G_{N}
.
∎

6.4.
Links between growing continua

In the previous subsection, we proved that if
z\in K
is a preperiodic point, then there exists a preperiodic growing arc within any access to
z
. In this final part of Section
6
, we aim to find abundant preperiodic points as terminals of growing curves.

Let
K_{\pm}
be growing continua generated by
f
-invariant and locally connected continua
E_{\pm}
, respectively, such that
E_{-}\cap E_{+}=\emptyset
. This implies that
E_{-,k}\cap E_{+,k^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for any
k,k^{\prime}\geq 0
, where
E_{\pm,k}
are the components of
f^{-k}(E_{\pm})
containing
E_{\pm}
, respectively. We continue to assume that
E_{\pm}
serve as skeletons of
E_{\pm,k}
(rel
P
) for every
k\geq 0
.

A
link
between
K_{-}
and
K_{+}
is a curve
\gamma
with
\gamma(0)\in E_{-,k}
and
\gamma(1)\in E_{+,k}
for some
k\geq 0
, such that one of the following two cases occurs:

•

\gamma
is a growing curve in either
K_{-}
or
K_{+}
(
one-sided link
); or

•

\gamma=\alpha_{-}\cdot\alpha_{+}^{-1}
, where
\alpha_{\pm}
are growing curves in
K_{\pm}
, respectively, with a common terminal disjoint from both
P
and any
E_{\pm,m}
for
m\geq 0
(
two-sided link
).

The unique terminal
z
of the growing curves in
\gamma
is called
the
infinity point
of the link
\gamma
. By definition,
\#\gamma^{-1}(z)=1
, and it holds for a two-sided link that
\alpha_{+}\cap\alpha_{-}=\{z\}
. Moreover, a link
\gamma
is one-sided if and only if the infinity point is contained in a certain
E_{\pm,k}
, if and only if the infinity point is an endpoint of
\gamma
.

Self-links of
K
Links between
K_{\pm}
\gamma^{\prime}
\gamma^{\prime}
\gamma
\gamma

Figure 13.
Two types of links

The left image in Figure
13
illustrates two types of links: the curve
\gamma^{\prime}
is a one-sided link, while
\gamma
is a two-sided link.

Set
P_{0}=P\setminus(E_{+}\cup E_{-})
. Then
P_{0}
is disjoint from
E_{\pm,m}
for every
m\geq 0
since
E_{\pm}
serve as skeletons of
E_{\pm,m}
, respectively. Two links
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
between
K_{\pm}
are said to be
equivalent
if there exist two curves
\delta_{\pm}\subset E_{\pm,k}
for some
k
, such that

\delta_{-}
joins
\gamma_{1}(0),\gamma_{2}(0)\in E_{-,k}
,

\delta_{+}
joins
\gamma_{1}(1),\gamma_{2}(1)\in E_{+,k}
, and
the closed curve
\delta_{-}\cdot\gamma_{2}\cdot\delta_{+}^{-1}\cdot\gamma_{1}^{-1}
is contractible in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{0}
.

This is an equivalence relation.
Moreover, link-equivalence is closely related to the concept of access defined in the previous subsection as follows:

(i)

If
\gamma\subset K_{-}
is a one-sided link between
K_{\pm}
, then it must be an infinitely growing curve in
K_{-}
since no
E_{-,k}
contains the infinity point of
\gamma
. Moreover, any growing curve in the same access as
\gamma
is also a link and equivalent to
\gamma
as a link. However, the converse does not hold since two equivalent one-sided links may have distinct terminals.

(ii)

If
\gamma=\alpha_{-}\cdot\alpha_{+}^{-1}
is a two-sided link between
K_{\pm}
, then both
\alpha_{\pm}
are infinitely growing. Moreover, if
\beta_{\pm}\subset K_{\pm}
are growing curves in the same accesses as
\alpha_{\pm}
, respectively, then
\gamma^{\prime}:=\beta_{-}\cdot\beta_{+}^{-1}
is a two-sided link equivalent to
\gamma
.

Corresponding to Proposition
6.3
, we have the following result for links.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
\gamma
be a link between
K_{\pm}
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

Any sub-curve of
\gamma
joining
E_{\pm,k}
for an integer
k
is a link equivalent to
\gamma
.

(2)

For every sufficiently large integer
k
, there exist two numbers
t_{\pm,k}\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma(t_{\pm,k})\in E_{\pm,k}
, respectively, and
\gamma(t_{-,k},t_{+,k})
is disjoint from
E_{-,k}\cup E_{+,k}
. Moreover,
\gamma[t_{-,k},t_{+,k}]
contains an arc
\beta_{k}
homotopic to
\gamma|_{[t_{-,k},t_{+,k}]}
rel
P
with endpoints fixed. In particular,
\beta_{k}
is a link between
K_{\pm}
that is equivalent to
\gamma
and has the same infinity point as
\gamma
.

(3)

Suppose that
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are equivalent links between
K_{\pm}
, with their interiors disjoint from
P
. Then there exist an integer
m\geq 0
and a continuous family of curves
\{\gamma_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
such that
\gamma_{0}=\gamma
,
\gamma_{1}=\gamma^{\prime}
, and each
\gamma_{s}
joins
E_{-,m}
to
E_{+,m}
with its interior disjoint from
P
.

Proof.

According to the relationship between link-equivalence and access stated above this proposition, statements (1) and (2) follow directly from Proposition
6.3
(1)–(3).

To prove statement (3), suppose first that
the infinity points of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
coincide. Then
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are either both one-sided links in one of
K_{\pm}
, or both two-sided links. In this case, statement (3) is an immediate consequence of Proposition
6.3
(4).

If the infinity points of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are distinct, by statements (1) and (2), we may assume that
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are disjoint arcs serving as crosscuts of the unique annular component
A
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus(E_{-}\cup E_{+})
. Since
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are equivalent, there exists a simply connected component
D_{*}
of
A\setminus(\gamma\cup\gamma^{\prime})
such that
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\subset\partial D_{*}
and
D_{*}\cap P=\emptyset
. The required curves
\{\gamma_{s}\}
can be chosen within
\overline{D_{*}}
.
∎

Based on this proposition, we can prove our desired result.

Proposition 6.7
.

Suppose that
K_{\pm}\subset J_{f}
and
\gamma
is a link between
K_{\pm}
. If the infinity point of
\gamma
is wandering, then there exists a curve
\ell=\beta_{-}\cdot\beta_{+}^{-1}
such that

(1)

\beta_{\pm}
are growing curves in
K_{\pm}
, respectively, and their common terminal is preperiodic;

(2)

there exists a sequence of curves
\{\ell_{k}\}
such that each
\ell_{k}
is homotopic to
\gamma
rel
P_{0}
with endpoints fixed and
\ell_{k}\to\ell
as
k\to\infty
.

Note that the curve
\ell
is not necessarily a link between
K_{\pm}
since the common terminal of
\beta_{\pm}
may be a marked point.

Proof.

We first claim that the links between
K_{\pm}
belong to finitely many equivalence classes.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite collection of links between
K_{\pm}
in pairwise distinct equivalence classes. To prove the claim, it suffices to show that
\#\Sigma\leq(\#P)^{6}
. By Proposition
6.6
, we may assume

\bullet
each curve in
\Sigma
is an arc that serves as a crosscut of some component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus(E_{-,m_{0}}\cup E_{+,m_{0}})
;

\bullet
if two arcs in
\Sigma
have distinct infinity points, then they are disjoint.

Let
Z
denote the set of infinity points of links in
\Sigma
. Decompose
\Sigma
as
\Sigma=\bigcup_{z\in Z}\Sigma_{z}
, where
\Sigma_{z}
is the collection of links in
\Sigma
with infinity point
z
. Pick a representative element in each
\Sigma_{z}
and denote their collection by
\Sigma_{1}
. Then
\#\Sigma_{1}=\#Z
and the links in
\Sigma_{1}
are disjoint.
By a similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
6.4
, we have
\#\Sigma_{1}\leq(\#P)^{2}
.

Fix
z\in Z
. By the relationships (i) and (ii) between link-equivalence and access as stated before Proposition
6.6
,
it follows from Lemma
6.4
that
\#\Sigma_{z}\leq(\#P)^{4}
. Therefore,
\#\Sigma\leq(\#P)^{6}
, which proves the claim.

Since the infinity point
z
of
\gamma
is wandering, it cannot be iterated into
P
. Thus, for each
i\geq 0
, the curve
f^{i}(\gamma)
is a link between
K_{\pm}
. By the claim above, there exist integers
q\geq 0
and
p\geq 1
such that
f^{q}(\gamma)
and
f^{q+p}(\gamma)
are equivalent. Set
\gamma_{0}:=f^{q+p}(\gamma)
and
\gamma_{1}:=f^{q}(\gamma)
.

By Proposition
6.6
(1), we may assume, by taking sub-curves if necessary, that the interiors of
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
are disjoint from
P
. Then by Proposition
6.6
(3), there exists a continuous family
\{\gamma_{s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
of curves joining
E_{\pm,k_{0}}
, with their interiors disjoint from
P
. Define two curves
\delta_{\pm,0}
by
\delta_{-,0}(s):=\gamma_{s}(0)
and
\delta_{+,0}(s):=\gamma_{s}(1)
,
s\in[0,1]
. Then
\delta_{\pm,0}\subset E_{\pm,k_{0}}
, respectively.

Since
f^{p}(\gamma_{1})=\gamma_{0}
, for any
t\in(0,1)
, the curve
\{\gamma_{s}(t):s\in[0,1]\}
has a unique lift by
f^{p}
based at
\gamma_{1}(t)
, denoted by
\{\gamma_{s+1}(t):s\in[0,1]\}
. Therefore, we obtain a continuous family of curves
\{\gamma_{s+1}\}_{s\in[0,1]}
such that
f^{p}\circ\gamma_{s+1}=\gamma_{s}
. Consequently,
\gamma_{2}
is a link between
K_{\pm}
and equivalent to
\gamma_{1}
. Define two curves
\delta_{\pm,1}
by
\delta_{-,1}(s):=\gamma_{s+1}(0)
and
\delta_{+,1}(s):=\gamma_{s+1}(1)
,
s\in[0,1]
. Then
\delta_{\pm,1}\subset E_{\pm,k_{0}+p}
and
f^{p}(\delta_{\pm,1})=\delta_{\pm,0}
, respectively.

Inductively applying the argument above, for each
k\geq 1
, we obtain

•

two equivalent links
\gamma_{k}
and
\gamma_{k+1}
between
K_{\pm}
such that
f^{p}(\gamma_{k+1})=\gamma_{k}
;

•

a curve
\delta_{-,k}\subset E_{-,k_{0}+kp}
joining
\gamma_{k}(0)
to
\gamma_{k+1}(0)
such that
f^{p}(\delta_{-,k})=\delta_{-,k-1}
; and

•

a curve
\delta_{+,k}\subset E_{+,k_{0}+kp}
joining
\gamma_{k}(1)
to
\gamma_{k+1}(1)
such that
f^{p}(\delta_{+,k})=\delta_{+,k-1}
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
q=0
. For each
m\geq 1
, let
\beta_{-,m}
and
\beta_{+,m}
denote the concatenations of
\{\delta_{-,k}\}_{k=1}^{m}
and
\{\delta_{+,k}\}_{k=1}^{m}
, respectively.
By Lemma
A.1
, the diameters of
\gamma_{k}
and
\delta_{\pm,k}
exponentially decrease to
0
. It follows that
{\gamma}_{k}
converges to a point
x
with
f^{p}(x)=x
, and that
\beta_{\pm,m}
uniformly converges to growing curves
\beta_{\pm}
in
K_{\pm}
, respectively, such that
\beta_{\pm}
have the common terminal
x
.

For each
m\geq 1
, define
\ell_{m}:=\beta_{-,m}\cdot{\gamma}_{m+1}\cdot\beta_{+,m}^{-1}
. Then
\ell_{m}
is homotopic to
\gamma_{1}
rel
P_{0}
with endpoints fixed. Immediately,
\ell_{m}
converges to
\ell:=\beta_{-}\cdot\beta_{+}^{-1}
as
m\to\infty
.
∎

Finally, let
K
be a growing continuum generated by an
f
-invariant and locally connected continuum
E
. Similar to the notion of links between
K_{\pm}
, we can define
self-links of
K
.

A
self-link
of
K
is a curve
\gamma\subset K
with
\gamma(0),\gamma(1)\in E_{k}
for some
k\geq 0
such that one of the following two cases occurs:

•

\gamma
is an infinitely growing curve in
K
(
one-sided self-link
); or

•

\gamma=\alpha_{-}\cdot\alpha_{+}^{-1}
, where
\alpha_{\pm}
are infinitely growing curves in distinct accesses with a common terminal that avoids both
P
and every
E_{k}
for
k\geq 0
(
two-sided self-link
).

The unique terminal of the growing curves in
\gamma
is called
the
infinity point
of the self-link
\gamma
; see the right image of Figure
13
.

Let
P_{0}=P\setminus E
. Two self-links
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are called
equivalent
if there exist two curves
\delta_{\pm}\subset E_{k}
for some
k
, such that

\delta_{-}
joins
\gamma_{1}(0)
to
\gamma_{2}(0)
,

\delta_{+}
joins
\gamma_{1}(1)
to
\gamma_{2}(1)
, and
the closed curve
\delta_{-}\cdot\gamma_{2}\cdot\delta_{+}^{-1}\cdot\gamma_{1}^{-1}
is contractible in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{0}
.

Let
\gamma
be a self-link of
K
, and let
z
be the infinity point of
\gamma
. It is worth noting that
f\circ\gamma
is also a self-link provided that
f(z)\notin P_{0}
. Indeed, if
\gamma
is a one-sided self-link, this result holds by Lemma
6.5
(1). In the case where
\gamma=\alpha_{-}\cdot\alpha_{+}^{-1}
is a two-sided self-link, if the conclusion were false, then
f\circ\alpha_{\pm}
would lie in the same access. Since
f
is injective near
z
, it follows from Lemma
6.5
(2) that
\alpha_{\pm}
lie in the same access to
z
, a contradiction.

With these definitions and a parallel argument, we can apply a similar argument as in the proof of Proposition
6.7
to derive the following result. Details are omitted.

Proposition 6.8
.

Suppose that
K\subset J_{f}
and
\gamma
is a self-link of
K
. If the infinity point of
\gamma
is wandering, then there exists a curve
\ell=\beta_{-}\cdot\beta_{+}^{-1}
such that

(1)

\beta_{\pm}
are growing curves in
K
, and their common terminal is preperiodic;

(2)

there exists a sequence of curves
\ell_{k}
such that each
\ell_{k}
is homotopic to
\gamma
rel
P_{0}
with endpoints fixed and
\ell_{k}\to\ell
as
k\to\infty
.

7.
Invariant graphs in maximal Fatou chains

In this section, we prove that every periodic level-
n
extremal chain admits an invariant graph on the Julia set if
n\geq 1
. Our proof relies on the inductive construction and the topology of extremal chains established in Sections
3
and
6
, respectively.

7.1.
Invariant graphs associated with level-0 Fatou chains

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map. We will analyze the dynamics of
f
on the union of periodic level-
0
Fatou chains.

Suppose that
E
is a component of the union of all periodic level-
0
Fatou chains with period
p
. Let
K
be the level-
1
extremal chain containing
E
. The main result of this subsection is as follows, which generalizes Theorem
1.2
.

Proposition 7.1
.

There exists a graph
G\subset K\cap J_{f}
such that
f^{p}(G)\subset G
, which is isotopic to a skeleton of
\partial E
rel
P
. Moreover, for each point
z\in G\setminus E
, there exist an integer
n_{0}\geq 1
and a component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
with
\overline{D}\cap P=\emptyset
such that
f^{n_{0}p}(z)\in\overline{D}
.

U_{1}
U_{2}
U_{3}
C_{1}
C_{2}
C_{3}
D_{1}
D_{2}

Figure 14.
The circle-graph
T
of
E=\overline{U_{1}\cup U_{2}\cup U_{3}}
. The circles
C_{i},i=1,2,3,
are intersection circles, and the irregular boundary circles of
T
are
\partial D_{1}
and
\partial D_{2}
.

If
E
contains exactly one Fatou domain, this proposition is a combination of Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
2.3
. Thus, we assume that
E
contains
m\geq 2
Fatou domains. The proof of Proposition
7.1
follows a similar approach as in that of Theorem
1.2
, with the distinction being the presence of intersection points between boundaries of different Fatou domains.

A point
x\in\partial E
is called an
intersection point
if it lies on the boundaries of at least two distinct Fatou domains within
E
. A circle
C\subset\partial E
is called an
intersection circle
if it lies on the boundary of a Fatou domain
U\subset E
and separates
U
from another Fatou domain in
E
. Recall that a circle
C\subset\partial U
is marked if it either intersects or separates
P
. Thus, every intersection circle is marked; see Figure
14
.

By definition, each intersection point of
E
is contained in an intersection circle, and conversely, each intersection circle of
E
contains intersection points. Note that there exist at most
2(m-1)
distinct intersection circles in
E
. Moreover, a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
is not a disk if and only if its boundary contains an intersection circle. On the other hand, for each intersection circle
C
, there exists at most one component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
such that
C\subset\partial D
. Therefore, there exist at most
2(m-1)
components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
that are not disks.

For each Fatou domain
U\subset E
, we denote by
T_{U}\subset\partial U
the finite circle-tree spanned by
\partial U\cap P
and all marked circles in
\partial U
; see Lemma
2.7
for background. Set

T:=\bigcup_{U\subset E}T_{U}.

Since the intersection points of
E
are contained in the intersection circles, which are all marked,
it follows that
T
is connected. By Lemmas
2.10
and
2.7
, we also have
f^{p}(T)\subset T
. Moreover,
T
is a skeleton of
\partial E
(rel
P
) since each
T_{U}
is a skeleton of
\partial U
.

Let
X_{0}
be the union of
P
together with all intersection points of
E
and all cut points of
T_{U}
for all Fatou domains
U\subset E
. Then
X_{0}
is compact, and
f^{p}(X_{0})\subset X_{0}
. Moreover, each component of
T\setminus X_{0}
is an open arc contained in a circle on the boundary of a Fatou domain in
E
.

There exist
m
components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus T
, each containing a Fatou domain in
E
. Let
T_{*}
denote the union of
T
and these
m
components. Since
T_{*}
contains all intersection circles of
E
, by the same reasoning as before, there exist at most
2(m-1)
components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus T_{*}
that are not disks. Therefore,
T
has at most
2(m-1)+m
complementary components that are not disks.

By a
boundary circle
of
T
, we mean the boundary of a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus T_{*}
that is a disk. A boundary circle
C
of
T
is called
regular
if
\#(C\cap X_{0})=2
and
\overline{D}\cap P=\emptyset
, where
D
is the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus T
with
\partial D=C
, and is called
irregular
otherwise.

Lemma 7.1
.

There exist finitely many irregular boundary circles of
T
.

Proof.

Let
D
be a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus T_{*}
that is a disk. Then either
D
is a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U}
for a Fatou domain
U\subset E
, or the boundary
\partial D
is composed of at least two arcs, which are sub-arcs of distinct intersection circles.

In the former case, if
\partial D
is a regular circle of
T_{U}
, then it is a regular boundary circle of
T
. Since
T_{U}
contains finitely many irregular circles, there exist finitely many irregular boundary circles of
T
of this type.

In the latter case, the circle
\partial D
of
T
contains at least two intersection points, say
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
. If
\partial D
is irregular, then either
\overline{D}\cap P\neq\emptyset
; or
\partial D\setminus\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
consists of two open arcs
\alpha_{i}\subset C_{i}
,
i=1,2
, where
C_{i}
is a circle of
T_{U_{i}}
for a Fatou domain
U_{i}\subset E
, such that
\alpha_{1}
or
\alpha_{2}
contains cut points of
T_{U_{1}}
or
T_{U_{2}}
, respectively;
or
\partial D\cap X_{0}
contains at least three intersection points. The number of components of the first type is clearly finite. Note that each
C_{i}
is an intersection circle and contains finitely many cut points of
T_{U_{i}}
. Then the number of components of the second type is also finite.
To complete the proof of the lemma, it suffices to verify the following claim.

Claim
. Let
\Omega_{1},\ldots,\Omega_{n},
with
n\geq 2
, be pairwise disjoint disks such that
B:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\overline{\Omega_{i}}
is connected. Considering the components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus B
that are disks, the boundaries of all but finitely many of these components contain exactly two
intersection points
of
B
, i.e., points belonging to at least two of
\partial{\Omega_{1}},\ldots,\partial{\Omega_{n}}
.

First, suppose that
n=2
. If
\#(\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2})=1
, then
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus B
is connected, and
\partial B
contains only one intersection point. If
\#(\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2})>1
, then the boundary of any component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus B
contains exactly two intersection points. Thus, the claim holds for
n=2
.

By induction, we assume that the claim holds for
n\geq 2
. Let
\Omega_{0}
be a disk disjoint from
\Omega_{1},\ldots,\Omega_{n}
such that both
\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}\overline{\Omega_{i}}
and
B=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\overline{\Omega_{i}}
are connected. Then
\Omega_{0}
is contained in a component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus B
. The intersection points of
B\cup\overline{\Omega_{0}}
are the union of the intersection points of
B
together with
\partial\Omega_{0}\cap\partial D
.

For any component
D^{\prime}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus B
other than
D
, the points in
\partial{\Omega_{0}}\cap\partial D^{\prime}
are the intersection points of
B
in
\partial D^{\prime}
. Thus, it suffices to verify that the boundaries of all but finitely many components of
D\setminus\overline{\Omega_{0}}
contain two intersection points of
\overline{\Omega_{0}}\cup B
.

If
\partial D\cap\partial\Omega_{0}
is a singleton, then
D\setminus\overline{\Omega_{0}}
is connected. If
\#(\partial D\cap\partial\Omega_{0})\geq 2
, except for finitely many ones, every component of
D\setminus\overline{\Omega_{0}}
is a disk whose boundary contains exactly two points of
\partial D\cap\partial\Omega_{0}
and consists of one open arc in
\partial D
and the other in
\partial\Omega_{0}
. Thus, there exist finitely many components of
D\setminus\overline{\Omega_{0}}
whose boundaries contain more than two intersection points of
\overline{\Omega_{0}}\cup B
, since
\partial D
has finitely many intersection points of
B
. The claim is proved.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

We use a similar argument as in the proof of Theorem
1.2
. For a regular boundary circle
C
of
T
, let
C^{\pm}
denote the two components of
C\setminus X_{0}
, and let
B(C^{-})=B(C^{+})
denote the closure of the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus T
whose boundary is
C
. Set

G_{1}=T\setminus\bigcup C^{-},

where the union is taken over all regular boundary circles of
T
. By Lemma
7.1
,
G_{1}
is a graph serving as a skeleton of
\partial E
rel
X_{0}
.

Now, we construct
G_{2}\subset f^{-p}(G_{1})
. For each
n\geq 1
, set
X_{n}:=f^{-np}(X_{0})
. Then
X_{n}\subset X_{n+1}
. Note that if
z\in X_{1}\cap G_{1}
, then
f^{p}(z)\in X_{0}\cap T\subset G_{1}
. Thus, for a component
\alpha_{1}
of
G_{1}\setminus X_{1}
, its image
f^{p}(\alpha_{1})
is a component of
T\setminus X_{0}
.

•

If
f(\alpha_{1})=C^{-}
for a regular boundary circle
C
of
T
, since
C^{+}
and
C^{-}
are isotopic rel
X_{0}
, there exists a unique component
\alpha_{1}^{+}
of
f^{-p}(C^{+})
isotopic to
\alpha_{1}
rel
X_{1}
. Such an arc
\alpha_{1}
is called a
deformation arc of
G_{1}
. Denote by
B(\alpha_{1})
the component of
f^{-p}(B(C^{-}))
containing
\alpha_{1}
. Then
B(\alpha_{1})
is a closed disk such that
B(\alpha_{1})\cap G_{1}=\overline{\alpha_{1}}
and
B(\alpha_{1})\cap X_{1}=\{\alpha_{1}(0),\alpha_{1}(1)\}
.

•

In the other case, we have
f^{p}(\alpha_{1})\subset G_{1}
by the construction of
G_{1}
.

Define the graph
G_{2}
as

G_{2}:=\left(G_{1}\setminus\bigcup\alpha_{1}\right)\cup\bigcup\alpha^{+}_{1},

where the union is taken over all deformation arcs of
G_{1}
. From the previous discussion, we have
f^{p}(G_{2})\subset G_{1}
, and
there exists an isotopy
\Theta^{1}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
rel
P
such that
\Theta^{1}_{t}:=\Theta^{1}(\cdot,t)
satisfies

(1)

\Theta^{1}_{0}=id
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
;

(2)

\Theta^{1}_{t}(z)=z
on a neighborhood of attracting cycles of
f
for
t\in[0,1]
;

(3)

if
z\in G_{1}
is not in any deformation arc, then
\Theta^{1}_{t}(z)=z
for
t\in[0,1]
; and

(4)

if
\alpha_{1}
is a deformation arc of
G_{1}
, then
\Theta^{1}_{1}(\alpha_{1})=\alpha_{1}^{+}
and
\Theta^{1}(\overline{\alpha_{1}}\times[0,1])=B(\alpha_{1})
.

Consequently,
\theta_{1}(G_{1})=G_{2}
with
\theta_{1}:=\Theta^{1}_{1}
.

By inductively applying Lemma
A.2
, we obtain an isotopy
\Theta^{n}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
rel
P
and a graph
G_{n+1}
for each
n\geq 1
, such that
\Theta^{n}_{0}=id
