and
\Theta^{n}_{t}\circ f^{p}(z)=f^{p}\circ\Theta^{n+1}_{t}(z)
for all
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
,
t\in[0,1]
, and
G_{n+1}=\theta_{n}(G_{n})
with
\theta_{n}:=\Theta^{n}_{1}
.
Thus,
f^{p}(G_{n+1})\subset G_{n}
. Besides, there exist some components of
G_{n}\setminus X_{n}
, called the
deformation arcs of
G_{n}

(
under
\Theta^{n})
, such that

•

if
z\in G_{n}
is not in any deformation arc of
G_{n}
, then
\Theta^{n}_{t}(z)=z
for
t\in[0,1]
;

•

if
\alpha_{n}
is a deformation arc of
G_{n}
, then
the deformation of
\overline{\alpha_{n}}
under
\Theta^{n}
, denoted by
B(\alpha_{n})
, is a closed disk such that
B(\alpha_{n})\cap G_{n}=\overline{\alpha_{n}}
and
B(\alpha_{n})\cap X_{n}=\{\alpha_{n}(0),\alpha_{n}(1)\}
.

Denote
\phi_{n}=\theta_{n-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{0}
for
n\geq 1
with
\theta_{0}:=id
. Then
G_{n}=\phi_{n}(G_{1})
. By Lemma
A.3
,
\{\phi_{n}\}
uniformly converges to a quotient map
\varphi
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. It follows that
f^{p}(G)\subset G
with
G:=\varphi(G_{1})
.

Fix a deformation arc
\alpha_{n}
of
G_{n}
,
n\geq 1
, and set
\alpha_{n-k}:=f^{kp}(\alpha_{n})
for
0\leq k\leq n
. From the lifting construction of
\Theta^{n}
, it follows that
\alpha_{n-k}
is a deformation arc of
G_{n-k}
and
f^{kp}(B(\alpha_{n}))=B(\alpha_{n-k})
for
0\leq k\leq n-1
, and that
\alpha_{0}=C^{-}
for a regular boundary circle
C
of
T
and

f^{np}:B(\alpha_{n})\to B(\alpha_{0})
is a homeomorphism.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
\alpha_{m}
and
\beta_{n}
be two distinct deformation arcs of
G_{m}
and
G_{n}
, respectively, with
m\geq n\geq 1
. Then either
B(\alpha_{m})\subset B(\beta_{n})
, or
\#\,(B(\alpha_{m})\cap B(\beta_{n}))\leq 2
.

Proof.

Set
\beta_{0}:=f^{np}(\beta_{n})
and
\alpha_{m-n}:=f^{np}(\alpha_{m})
.
We claim that either
B(\alpha_{m-n})\subset B(\beta_{0})
, or
\#\,(B(\alpha_{m-n})\cap B(\beta_{0}))\leq 2
.
Note that
\beta_{0}=C^{-}
for a regular boundary circle
C
of
T
. The two open arcs
C^{\pm}
are contained in the boundaries of Fatou domains
U_{1},U_{2}\subset E
, respectively.

If
U_{1}=U_{2}
, the interior of
B(\beta_{0})
is a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U_{1}}
, and
B(\alpha_{m-n})\subset\overline{D}
for a component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U_{1}}
. Thus, either
B(\beta_{0})=\overline{D}
or
\#(B(\beta_{0})\cap\overline{D})\leq 1
by Lemma
2.2
. Then the claim holds.

If
U_{1}\not=U_{2}
, there exists a component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U_{1}}
such that
U_{2}\subset D
and the interior of
B(\beta_{0})
is a component of
D\setminus\overline{U_{2}}
. Moreover, there exists a component
W
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus(\overline{U_{1}}\cup\overline{U_{2}})
with
B(\alpha_{m-n})\subset\overline{W}
. If
W
is a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U_{1}}
or
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\overline{U_{2}}
, then
\#(\overline{W}\cap B(\alpha_{0}))\leq 1
by Lemma
2.2
. Otherwise,
W
is a component of
D\setminus\overline{U_{2}}
. In this case, either
\overline{W}=B(\beta_{0})
, or
\overline{W}\cap B(\beta_{0})
consists of at most two intersection points in
X_{0}\cap C
. Then the claim also holds.

The proposition follows directly from the above claim and a pullback argument.
∎

The remaining parts of the proof of Proposition
7.1
are the same as the corresponding parts of the proofs of Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
2.3
. We omit the details.
∎

Corollary 7.1
.

Suppose that
K\not=K^{\prime}
are periodic level-
1
extremal chains. Let
G\subset K
and
G^{\prime}\subset K^{\prime}
be invariant graphs derived from Proposition
7.1
. Then
G\cap G^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that both
K
and
K^{\prime}
are
f
-invariant. Let
E
and
E^{\prime}
denote the union of all periodic level-
0
Fatou chains contained in
K
and
K^{\prime}
, respectively. Then
K=\overline{\bigcup_{k}E_{k}}
and
K^{\prime}=\overline{\bigcup E_{k}^{\prime}}
. Moreover,
E_{k}\cap E^{\prime}_{m}=\emptyset
for any
k,m\geq 0
.

Suppose, to the contrary, that
G\cap G^{\prime}
contains a point
z
. We can assume that
f^{n}(z)\not\in E
for all
n\geq 0
since
E\cap E^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
Since
E\cap E_{k}^{\prime}=\emptyset
for every
k\geq 1
, all
E_{k}^{\prime}
lie in the same component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
. On the other hand,
by Proposition
7.1
, there exist an integer
n_{0}\geq 1
and a component
D
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
such that
\overline{D}\cap P=\emptyset
and
f^{n_{0}}(z)\in\overline{D}
. Since
f^{n_{0}}(z)\not\in E
, we obtain
f^{n_{0}}(z)\in D
. Then
K^{\prime}
intersects
D
. It follows that
E_{k}^{\prime}
intersects
D
for a sufficiently large integer
k
, and hence
E^{\prime}\subset D
. However, this contradicts
D\cap P=\emptyset
.
∎

Corollary 7.2
.

Suppose that
K
is an
f
-invariant level-
1
extremal chain, and
E
is the union of boundaries of periodic Fatou domains in
K
. Let
G\subset K
be the invariant graph obtained in Proposition
7.1
. Set
S:=E\cup G
. Then,
S_{n}\subset K
for
n\geq 1
, and
G_{N}
is a skeleton of
S_{n}
for some
N
and all
n\geq N
, where
S_{n}
and
G_{n}
are the components of
f^{-n}(S)
and
f^{-n}(G)
containing
S
and
G
, respectively.

Proof.

By the construction of
G
, there exist a graph
\Gamma_{0}
serving as a skeleton of
E
rel
P
and an isotopy
\Psi^{0}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
rel
X_{0}
such that
\Psi^{0}_{0}=id
,
\Psi^{0}_{1}(\Gamma_{0})=G
, and
\Psi^{0}_{s_{k}}(\Gamma_{0})\subset E_{k}
for a sequence
\{s_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\subset(0,1)
with
s_{k}\to 1
as
k\to\infty
.

Fix any
n\geq 1
. By Lemma
4.4
, there exists a unique component
\Gamma_{n}
of
f^{-n}(\Gamma_{0})
serving as a skeleton of
E_{n}
. Let
\Psi^{n}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
rel
X_{0}
be the lift of the isotopy
\Psi^{0}
by
f^{n}
such that
\Psi^{n}_{0}=id
. Then
\Psi^{n}_{s_{k}}(\Gamma_{n})
is contained in
E_{k+n}
and converges to
\Psi^{n}_{1}(\Gamma_{n})
as
k\to\infty
, which is a component of
f^{-n}(G)
. Thus,
\Psi^{n}_{1}(\Gamma_{n})\subset K
. If
X_{0}\cap E=\emptyset
, then
\partial K=E
is a Jordan curve, and this corollary clearly holds. Otherwise, we have
X_{0}\cap E\subset\Psi^{n}_{1}(\Gamma_{n})\cap G_{n}
. Therefore,
G_{n}=\Psi^{n}_{1}(\Gamma_{n})\subset K
.

Note that both
E
and
G
serve as skeletons of
S
. By Lemma
4.4
,
E_{n}
and
G_{n}
are the unique components of
f^{-n}(E)
and
f^{-n}(G)
contained in
S_{n}
, respectively. Thus,
S_{n}=E_{n}\cup G_{n}\subset K
. Finally, by Corollary
4.1
and Lemma
4.4
, there exists an
N>0
such that
\Gamma_{N}
is a skeleton of
E_{n}
for every
n\geq N
. Since
\Gamma_{n}\sim G_{n}
rel
P
, the graph
G_{N}
is a skeleton of
S_{n}
for every
n\geq N
.
∎

7.2.
Invariant graphs on extremal chains

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map with
J_{f}\not=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. The sketch for the construction of invariant graphs on extremal chains is as follows.

Suppose that
E
is the intersection of
J_{f}
with a component of the union of all periodic level-
0
Fatou chains. Let
K
be the intersection of
J_{f}
with the level-
1
extremal chain containing
E
. By Proposition
7.1
, there exists an invariant graph
G\subset K
isotopic to a skeleton of
E
rel
P
. To construct an invariant graph that serves as a skeleton of

K
, a natural approach is to add a finite number of arcs to
G
such that

(1)

the combined set of
G
and the added arcs form a skeleton of
K
; and

(2)

each added arc
\gamma
is preperiodic with respect to
G
, i.e., there exist
q\geq 0
and
p\geq 1
such that
f^{q+p}(\gamma)\subset f^{q}(\gamma)\cup G
.

Indeed, the first condition can be derived from Lemma
6.3
, while the second one follows from Propositions
6.5
,
6.7
, and
6.8
.

By employing a similar inductive argument, we can construct an invariant graph on any periodic level-
n
extremal chain for every
n\geq 1
.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map, and let
K_{1},\ldots,K_{m}
be pairwise distinct continua such that each
K_{i}
is the intersection of
J_{f}
and a periodic level-
n
extremal chain with
n\geq 1
. Suppose that
\mathbf{K}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}K_{i}
is connected and
f(\mathbf{K})=\mathbf{K}
. Then there exists a graph
G
serving as a skeleton of
\mathbf{K}
rel
P
such that
f(G)\subset G
.

This proposition immediately implies Theorem
1.4
. It is worth mentioning that the proposition is false if the level
n=0
, as shown in Theorem
2.3
.

Proof.

The proof goes by induction on the level
n
. First, assume that
n=1
.

For each
1\leq i\leq m
, let
E_{i}
denote the union of boundaries of all periodic Fatou domains within
K_{i}
. By Lemma
3.3
, each
K_{i}
is the growing continuum generated by
E_{i}
.
As indicated at the beginning of Section
6
, we may assume that
E_{i}
is a skeleton of
E_{i,k}
(rel
P
) for every
k\geq 1
, where
E_{i,k}
denotes the component of
f^{-p_{i}k}(E_{i})
containing
E_{i}
and
p_{i}
is the period of
E_{i}
.

Claim.
There exist infinitely growing curves
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}
in
K
with preperiodic terminals such that, by replacing each
E_{i}
with
E_{i,N}
for a sufficiently large integer
N
, the set
(\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}E_{i})\cup(\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}\gamma_{j})
is a skeleton of
\mathbf{K}
.

Proof of the Claim.

Let
z
be a marked point in
\mathbf{K}
. Then
z\in K_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq m
. If
z\not\in E_{i}
, by Lemma
6.3
(1), there exists a growing curve
\alpha_{z}\subset K_{i}
joining
E_{i}
to
z
. Since
E_{i}
is a skeleton of every
E_{i,k}
, it holds that
z\not\in\bigcup_{k>0}E_{i,k}
. Thus,
\alpha_{z}
is infinitely growing.

Suppose
x,y\in P
are separated by
\mathbf{K}
. Then there exists a smallest integer
s\geq 1
such that, by re-enumerating
K_{i}
if necessary, the points
x
and
y
are separated by the union of
K_{1},\ldots,K_{s}
.

In the case of
s=1
, if
x
and
y
are separated by
E_{1,k}
for some
k\geq 1
, then they are separated by
E_{1}
since
E_{1}
is a skeleton. Otherwise, by Lemma
6.3
(2), there exists a curve
\eta=\beta_{-}\cdot\beta_{+}^{-1}\subset K_{1}
such that
E_{1}\cup\eta
separates
x
from
y
, where
\beta_{\pm}
are growing curves in
K_{1}
.

If the common terminal
z
of
\beta_{\pm}
is disjoint from
E_{1,k}
for all
k
, then the curve
\eta
serves as a two-sided self-link of
K_{1}
provided that
z\notin P
. If
z
is contained in some
E_{1,k_{0}}
, then one of
\beta_{\pm}
, say
\beta_{-}
, is infinitely growing, and
\beta_{-}\cup E_{1,k_{0}}
separates
x
from
y
. In this case,
\beta_{-}
serves as a one-sided self-link of
K_{1}
, and we reset
\eta=\beta_{-}
.

In both cases, we can apply Proposition
6.8
to the self-link
\eta
, and thus obtain a curve

\eta_{z}=\beta_{z}^{\prime}\cdot\beta_{z}^{-1}\subset K_{1}
such that the common terminal
z
of the growing curves
\beta_{z}^{\prime}
and
\beta_{z}
is preperiodic, and that
\eta_{z}\cup E_{1}
separates
x
from
y
.
By replacing
E_{1}
with some
E_{1,k}
, we may further assume that each of
\beta^{\prime}_{z}
and
\beta_{z}
is either trivial or infinitely growing.

In the case of
s=2
, let
D
be the component of
\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus(K_{1}\cup K_{2})
containing
x
. Since
\partial D
is locally connected by Theorem
6.1
, a Jordan curve
\alpha\subset\partial D
separates
x
from
y
. By the minimum of
s
, there exists a unique arc
\alpha_{1}
among components of
\alpha\setminus K_{2}
such that
\alpha_{1}\cup K_{2}
separates
x
from
y
. Let
\alpha_{2}
be an arc in
K_{2}
with the same endpoints as
\alpha_{1}
. Then
\alpha_{1}\cup\alpha_{2}
forms a Jordan curve that separates
x
from
y
.
For
s\geq 3
, with similar arguments, there exist arcs
\alpha_{i}\subset K_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,s
, such that their union is a Jordan curve separating
x
from
y
. Let
Z
be the set of endpoints of the arcs
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}
.

Fix a point
z\in Z
. There exist exactly two distinct integers
i=i(z)
and
i^{\prime}=i^{\prime}(z)
among
\{1,\ldots,s\}
such that
z\in\alpha_{i}\cap\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\subset K_{i}\cap K_{i^{\prime}}
. By Lemma
6.3
(1), there exist growing curves
\tilde{\beta}_{z}
and
\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}_{z}
in
K_{i}
and
K_{i^{\prime}}
, respectively, with the common terminal
z
. We can further require that
\tilde{\beta}_{z}
(resp.,
\tilde{\beta}_{z}^{\prime}
) is a trivial curve if
z\in E_{i,k_{0}}
(resp.,
E_{i^{\prime},k_{0}}
) for some
k_{0}
.

If
z
is preperiodic, we set
\beta_{z}=\tilde{\beta}_{z}
and
\beta_{z}^{\prime}=\tilde{\beta}_{z}^{\prime}
. Otherwise,

\tilde{\eta}_{z}=\tilde{\beta}_{z}^{\prime}\cdot\tilde{\beta}_{z}^{-1}
is a link between
K_{i}
and
K_{i^{\prime}}
. In particular, it is a two-sided link if and only if
z
is disjoint from
E_{i,k}
and
E_{i^{\prime},k}
for all
k\geq 0
.
In this case, we can apply Proposition
6.7
to the link
\tilde{\eta}_{z}
and obtain a curve
{\eta}_{z}={\beta}_{z}^{\prime}\cdot{\beta}_{z}^{-1}
such that
\tilde{\eta}_{z}
and
\eta_{z}
are homotopic rel
\{x,y\}
with endpoints fixed, and the common terminal of the growing curves
{\beta}_{z}\subset K_{i}
and
{\beta}_{z}^{\prime}\subset K_{i^{\prime}}
are preperiodic.

By the minimality of
s
, for a sufficiently large integer
k_{0}
, the union of
\eta_{z}
,
z\in Z,
and all
E_{j,k_{0}}
,
1\leq j\leq s
, is connected and separates
x
from
y
. By replacing each
E_{j}
with some
E_{j,k}
, we may assume

•

for each
z\in Z
, either
z\in E_{i}
for some
E_{i}
, or
z
avoids
E_{i,k}
for all
1\leq i\leq s
and
k\geq 0
;

•

each
{\beta}_{z}
(resp.,
\beta_{z}^{\prime}
) is either trivial or infinitely growing.

Finally, the required growing curves
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}
consist of all
\alpha_{z}
and the non-trivial curves
{\beta}_{z}
and
{\beta}_{z}^{\prime}
described above. Thus, the claim is proved.
∎

Let
Q\subset\mathbf{K}
denote the set of all points in the orbits of
\gamma_{1}(1),\ldots,\gamma_{r}(1)
. Then
f(Q)\subset Q
. According to Proposition
7.1
and Corollary
7.1
, each
K_{i}
contains a graph
G_{i}
such that

•

G_{i}
is a skeleton of
S_{i}:=G_{i}\cup E_{i}
rel
P
and contains
Q\cap E_{i}
;

•

f(\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}G_{i})\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}G_{i}
and
S_{i}\cap S_{j}=\emptyset
if
i\not=j
.

By Corollary
7.2
, each
K_{i}
is also the growing continuum generated by
S_{i}
. For every
k\geq 1
, denote by
S_{i,k}
and
G_{i,k}
the components of the
k
-th pre-image by
f^{p_{i}}
of
S_{i}
and
G_{i}
, respectively, such that
S_{i}\subset S_{i,k}
and
G_{i}\subset G_{i,k}
.

Let
\Delta
be a maximal collection of infinitely growing curves in
K_{1},\ldots,K_{m}
, which have initial points in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}G_{i}
and terminals in
Q
and belong to pairwise distinct accesses. According to Lemma
6.4
,
\Delta
contains finitely many elements. The claim above implies that the union of
G_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,m,
together with all curves in
\Delta
, is a skeleton of
\mathbf{K}
rel
P
.

For any
\delta\in\Delta
with terminal
z:=\delta(1)
, its image
f(\delta)
is an infinitely growing curve to
f(z)\in Q
by Lemma
6.5
(1). By the maximality of
\Delta
, we obtain a self-map
f_{h}:\Delta\to\Delta
such that
f_{h}(\delta)
is defined to be the unique element of
\Delta
in the same access as
f(\delta)
.

Mark a curve
\delta_{*}
in each cycle under
f_{h}
. Suppose that
\delta_{*}\subset K_{i}
with period
p
under
f_{h}
. By Proposition
6.5
, we may assume that

•

for any
t\in(0,1)
, there exists an integer
k>1
such that
\delta_{*}[0,t]\subset G_{i,k}
; and

•

\delta_{*}
is an
f^{p}
-invariant arc in the sense that
f^{p}(\delta_{*})\subset\delta_{*}\cup G_{i}
.

Since
\Delta
has finitely many elements, any curve
\delta\in\Delta
is eventually iterated by
f_{h}
to a marked one
\delta_{*}
. Let
q\geq 0
be the smallest number such that
f_{h}^{q}(\delta)=\delta_{*}
. Assume
\delta(0)\in G_{j}
. By Lemma
6.5
(2), there exists a lift
\delta^{\prime}
of
\delta_{*}
by
f^{q}
that lies in the same access as
\delta
and has the initial point in
G_{j,q}
.

Let
N
be a sufficiently large integer such that the initial point of each
\delta^{\prime}
with
\delta\in\Delta
lies in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}G_{i,N}
. Define
G:=(\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}G_{i,N})\cup(\bigcup_{\delta\in\Delta}\delta^{\prime})
. The previous discussion shows that
f(G)\subset G
and
G
is a skeleton of
\mathbf{K}
rel
P
.

Since the curves in
\Delta
are infinitely growing and lie in pairwise distinct accesses, by Proposition
6.3
(2), there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
\delta^{\prime}[1-\epsilon,1)
with
\delta\in\Delta
are pairwise disjoint, each disjoint from
G_{i,N},i=1,\ldots,m
. On the other hand, the arcs
\delta^{\prime}[0,1-\epsilon],\delta\in\Delta
are contained in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}G_{i,N_{1}}
for some
N_{1}>N
. Thus, the locally branched points of
G
are contained in those of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}G_{i,N_{1}}
together with
Q
, which are finite. Thus,
G
is a graph. Now, we have proved this proposition in the case of
n=1
.

Suppose that the proposition holds for level-
n
extremal chains with
n\geq 1
. Let
K_{1},\ldots,K_{m}
be pairwise distinct continua such that each
K_{i}
is the intersection of
J_{f}
and a periodic level-
(n+1)
extremal chain. For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
, denote by
E_{i}
the intersections of
J_{f}
and the union of periodic level-
n
extremal chains within
K_{i}
.
Then
K_{i}
is the growing continuum generated by
E_{i}
. By induction, there exists a graph
G_{i}
serving as a skeleton of
E_{i}
such that
f(\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}G_{i})\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}G_{i}
.

Note that in this case, we have
G_{i}\subset E_{i}
and set
S_{i}:=E_{i}
. In contrast, in the case of
n=1
, the graph
G_{i}
is not necessarily contained in
E_{i}
, and thus we performed a transformation from
E_{i}
to
S_{i}=E_{i}\cup G_{i}
by Corollary
7.2
therein. By a similar argument as in the case of
n=1
, we obtain the desired invariant graph
G\subset\mathbf{K}
.
∎

8.
Invariant graphs of rational maps

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map with
J_{f}\neq\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. As stated in the introduction, it suffices to prove Proposition
1.1
in order to construct the invariant graph required by Theorem
1.1
.

According to Corollary
4.2
and Theorem
1.6
, by possibly enlarging
P
, there exists a stable set
{\mathcal{K}}\subset J_{f}
that induces a cluster-Sierpiński decomposition of
(f,P)
, such that the decomposition

\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}=\mathcal{K}\sqcup\mathcal{V}\sqcup\mathcal{A}\sqcup\mathcal{S}

satisfies the following properties:

(P1)

Each component of
{\mathcal{K}}
contains points of
P
;

(P2)

Every component of
\mathcal{V}
is complex-type and disjoint from any attracting cycle of
f
;

(P3)

Every component of
\mathcal{S}
is a simply connected domain of simple type;

(P4)

Every component
A
of
\mathcal{A}
is an annulus of annular type. Moreover, if
A\cap f^{-1}(\mathcal{K})\neq\emptyset
, then
A
contains an annular-type component of
f^{-1}(\mathcal{K})
.

Therefore, it suffices to prove Proposition
1.1
under the properties (P1)–(P4).

The proof of this proposition will be divided into three parts. First, we identify a graph in each component of

\mathcal{E}={\mathcal{K}}\sqcup{\mathcal{V}}
such that their union is
f
-invariant. Next, we construct invariant arcs in
\mathcal{A}
to connect these graphs together. Finally, we join every marked point in
\mathcal{S}\cap J_{f}
to the previous graph.

Proof of Proposition
1.1
.

At the beginning, we select several specific marked points.

In each cycle of
{\mathcal{V}}
under
f_{\#}
, we designate a
preferred
component
V
. Denote its period by
p
. For each
n\geq 0
, let
V_{n}
denote the unique complex-type component of
f^{-np}(V)
contained in
V
.
By Theorem
1.6
and property (P2), there exists a marked Sierpiński rational map
(g,Q_{g})
as the blow-up by
\pi
of the exact sub-system
f^{p}:V_{1}\to V
, i.e.,

•

\pi(J_{g})=\bigcap\overline{V_{n}}
and
\pi\circ g=f^{p}\circ\pi
on
J_{g}
;

•

\pi
sends the closure of each Fatou domain onto a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus V_{n}
for some
n\geq 0
.

Due to property (P1), the marked set
Q_{g}
coincides with the union of
\pi^{-1}(P\cap V)
and the centers of Fatou domains outside
\pi^{-1}({V})
.

By the conditions of the proposition, let
G_{g}\supset Q_{g}
be a
g
-invariant regulated graph. Then for each Fatou domain
D
of
g
, the set
Y_{D}:=G_{g}\cap\partial D
satisfies:

•

g(Y_{D})\subset Y_{g(D)}
, and
Y_{D}\not=\emptyset
if
D\cap Q_{g}\not=\emptyset
;

•

Y_{D}
is a finite set, and there exist only finitely many Fatou domains
D
such that
\#Y_{D}\geq 3
.

Since
V
avoids the periodic Fatou domains by property (P2), the choice of
Q_{g}
implies that
Y_{V}:=\bigcup_{D}\pi(Y_{D})
lies in
\partial V
and each component of
\partial V
intersects
Y_{V}
, where
D
ranges over all marked Fatou domains of
(g,Q_{g})
. Moreover, we have
f^{p}(Y_{V})\subset Y_{V}
.
If
V^{\prime}
is another component of
{\mathcal{V}}
such that
f^{q}_{\#}(V^{\prime})=V
, set
Y_{V^{\prime}}:=f^{-q}(Y_{V})\cap\partial V^{\prime}
.
Thus,

Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}:=\bigcup Y_{V}

is an
f
-invariant and finite set in
\partial{\mathcal{V}}\subset{\mathcal{K}}
, where the union is taken over all components of
{\mathcal{V}}
.

For a finitely connected domain
W
, an
oriented boundary component
of
W
means a component of
\partial W
equipped with an orientation pointing into
W
.

Let
\Lambda
be the collection of oriented boundary components of all annuli in
{\rm Comp}({\mathcal{A}})
. Then any two elements of
\Lambda
are distinct even if they overlap.

For any
\lambda\in\Lambda
, since
\lambda\subset{\mathcal{K}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}
is a stable set, there exists either an annular-type component
A_{1}
of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{A}})
or an annular-type component
V_{1}
of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{V}})
such that
\lambda
is an oriented boundary component of
A_{1}
or
V_{1}
. Thus, its image
f(\lambda)
is either also an element of
\Lambda
, or an oriented boundary component of a certain
V\in{\rm Comp}({\mathcal{V}})
. Set

(8.1)

\Lambda_{*}=\{\lambda\in\Lambda:f^{n}(\lambda)\in\Lambda\text{ for all $n\geq 0$}\}.

Since
f(\partial{\mathcal{V}})\subset\partial{\mathcal{V}}
, the orbit of any
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{*}
will stay in
\partial{\mathcal{V}}
after leaving
\Lambda
.

By Theorem
1.5
, we can assign a point
z_{\lambda}
to each element
\lambda\in\Lambda_{*}
such that
f(z_{\lambda})=z_{f(\lambda)}
. Then the finite set
\{z_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\Lambda_{*}\}
is
f
-invariant and contained in
{\mathcal{K}}
.
On the other hand, there exists an integer
M>0
such that
f^{M}(\lambda)\subset\partial{\mathcal{V}}
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{*}
. Since
f(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})\subset Y_{{\mathcal{V}}}\subset{\mathcal{K}}
, we obtain an
f
-invariant and finite set

Q:=(f^{-M}(Y_{{\mathcal{V}}})\cap{\mathcal{K}})\,\bigcup\,\{z_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\Lambda_{*}\}\subset{\mathcal{K}}.

Part I. Construct invariant graphs in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}={\mathcal{K}}\sqcup{\mathcal{V}}}
.

By Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
and Lemma
4.4
, each component
K
of
{\mathcal{K}}
contains a graph
G_{K}
serving as a skeleton of
K
rel
P\cup Q
such that the union
\bigcup_{K}G_{K}
is
f
-invariant.

Let
V
be a preferred
f_{\#}
-periodic component of
{\mathcal{V}}
with period
p
. Denote by
\mathscr{B}
the collection of the complementary components of
V_{n}
for all
n>0
.

By Theorem
1.6
, for each
B\in\mathscr{B}
,
\pi^{-1}(B)=\overline{D}
and
\pi^{-1}(\partial B)=\partial D
, where
D
is a Fatou domain of
g
, and
\pi^{-1}(z)
is a singleton if
z
does not belong to any element of
\mathscr{B}
.

We set
\Gamma:=\pi(G_{g})
and
Y_{B}:=\pi(Y_{D})
with
B=\pi(\overline{D})
. According to the properties of
Y_{D}
presented at the third paragraph of the proof, we have that

•

Y_{B}\subset\partial B
and
f^{p}(Y_{B})\subset Y_{B^{\prime}}
if
\partial B^{\prime}=f^{p}(\partial B)
;

•

Y_{B}
is a finite set and there exist only finitely many
B\in\mathscr{B}
with
\#Y_{B}\geq 3
;

•

Y_{V}=\bigcup_{B}Y_{B}
and
Y_{B}\neq\emptyset
, where
B
is taken over all components of
\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus V
;

•

if
z\in\Gamma\setminus\bigcup_{B\in\mathscr{B}}B
, then
z\in J_{f}
and
f^{p}(z)\in\Gamma
.

To obtain an
f^{p}
-invariant graph associated with
V
, we need to revise
\Gamma\cap B
to an appropriate graph
G_{B}
for each
B\in\mathscr{B}
that intersects
\Gamma
.

If
B
is a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus V
, then
\partial B\subset K
for a component
K
of
{\mathcal{K}}
. Define
G_{B}=G_{K}
. Note that
G_{K}
contains
Y_{B}
by the choices of
Q
and
G_{K}
.

If
B
is not a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus V
, then
B\cap P=\emptyset
, and there exist a smallest positive integer
k
and a component
B^{\prime}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus V
such that
\partial B
is a component of
f^{-kp}(\partial B^{\prime})
. Let
K
and
K^{\prime}
be the components of
f^{-kp}({\mathcal{K}})
containing
\partial B
and
\partial B^{\prime}
, respectively. Then
f^{kp}(K)=K^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
4.4
, the set
\tilde{G}_{B}=f^{-kp}(G_{B^{\prime}})\cap K
is a component of
f^{-kp}(G_{B^{\prime}})
contained in
B
. Thus,
\tilde{G}_{B}
is a graph. Since
f^{kp}(Y_{B})\subset Y_{B^{\prime}}
, it follows that
Y_{B}\subset\tilde{G}_{B}
. Define
G_{B}
as follows:

(1)

If
\#Y_{B}\geq 3
, set
G_{B}=\tilde{G}_{B}
; if
\#Y_{B}=1
, set
G_{B}=Y_{B}
;

(2)

If
\#Y_{B}=2
, let
G_{B}
be an arc in
\tilde{G}_{B}
joining the two points of
Y_{B}
such that
f^{kp}(G_{B})\subset G_{B^{\prime}}
and
f^{p}(G_{B})\subset G_{f^{p}(B)}
.

Thus, we obtain an
f^{p}
-invariant continuum

G_{V}:=\bigg(\Gamma\setminus\bigcup_{B\in\mathscr{B}}B\bigg)\bigcup\bigg(\bigcup_{B\in\mathscr{B}}G_{B}\bigg),

which lies in
J_{f}
and contains
P\cap V
. Since the diameters of
B\in\mathscr{B}
exponentially converge to zero by Lemma
A.1
, the continuum
G_{V}
is a graph.

If
V^{\prime}
is a component of
{\mathcal{V}}
such that
f^{q}_{\#}(V^{\prime})=V
for a smallest
q\geq 1
,
then define
G_{V^{\prime}}=f^{-q}(G_{V})\cap V^{\prime}.

Note that the accumulation set of
G_{V^{\prime}}
on
\partial V^{\prime}
is contained in
Y_{V^{\prime}}\subset Q
.

Define the set

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}:=\bigg(\bigcup_{K\in{\rm Comp}({\mathcal{K}})}G_{K}\bigg)\ \ \bigcup\bigg(\bigcup_{V\in{\rm Comp}({\mathcal{V}})}G_{V}\bigg),

which is
f
-invariant and contains
Q
. Moreover, it satisfies the following two properties:

(a)

For each component
E
of
\mathcal{E}
, the set
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{E}}\cap E
is a graph serving as a skeleton of
E\cap J_{f}
rel
P
;

(b)

For each component
V
of
{\mathcal{V}}
and any component
V^{\prime}
of
f^{-1}(V)
, any pair of distinct boundary components
\lambda_{\pm}
of
{V}^{\prime}
can be joined
by an arc in
f^{-1}({\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{E}})
, which lies in the annulus
A(\lambda_{+},\lambda_{-})
bounded by
\lambda_{\pm}
and has the endpoints in
f^{-1}(Y_{V})
.

For property (a), it suffices to show
the connectivity of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{E}}\cap E
. Let
V\subset E
be any component of
{\mathcal{V}}
. By construction, for each boundary component
\lambda
of
V
, the accumulation points of
G_{V}
on
\lambda
are non-empty and lie in the graph
G_{K}
, where
K
is a component of
{\mathcal{K}}
contained in
E
such that
\lambda\subset K
. This implies that
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}\cap E
is connected.

To prove property (b), we choose a sequence of domains
V_{\epsilon}
compactly contained in
V
that converges to
V
as
\epsilon\to 0
, such that
V\setminus\overline{V_{\epsilon}}
consists of annuli disjoint from
P
, and that
G_{\epsilon}=(V_{\epsilon}\cap G_{V})\cup\partial V_{\epsilon}
is connected. Then each
G_{\epsilon}
is a skeleton of
\overline{V_{\epsilon}}
rel
P
, and
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}G_{\epsilon}=(V\cap G_{V})\cup\partial V
.

Set
V_{\epsilon}^{\prime}=f^{-1}(V_{\epsilon})\cap V^{\prime}
. Then
V_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
is a domain, and each of its boundary components is parallel to a component of
\partial V^{\prime}
, and vice versa. Moreover,
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\overline{V_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}=\overline{V^{\prime}}
. By Lemma
4.4
,
G^{\prime}_{\epsilon}:=f^{-1}(G_{\epsilon})\cap\overline{V_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}
is connected. Thus, it contains all components of
\partial V_{\epsilon}
. Consequently, the Hausdorff limit
G^{\prime}
of
{G}^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
is connected and contains
\partial V^{\prime}
. Moreover,
{G}^{\prime}\cap{V}^{\prime}=f^{-1}(G_{V})\cap{V}^{\prime}
.

From the previous discussion, there exist pairwise disjoint open arcs
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}
in
G^{\prime}\cap V^{\prime}
and components
\lambda_{-}=\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m+1}=\lambda_{+}
of
\partial V^{\prime}
such that each
\alpha_{i}
joins
\lambda_{i}
to
\lambda_{i+1}
and its endpoints belong to
f^{-1}(Y_{V})
. Note that for every
i\in\{2,\ldots,m-1\}
,
\lambda_{i}
is contained in a component
K_{i}\subset A(\lambda_{-},\lambda_{+})
of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}})
. Thus, we can find an arc
\beta_{i}\subset K_{i}
joining
\alpha_{i-1}(1)
to
\alpha_{i}(0)
such that
f(\beta_{i})\subset G_{f(K_{i})}
. Finally, the arc
(\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i})\cup(\bigcup_{j=2}^{m-1}\beta_{i})
satisfies property (b).

Part II. Connect the graphs in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}
.

By properties (P2)–(P4), any two components of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{E}}
are separated by a component of
{\mathcal{A}}
, and vice versa. Thus, to obtain a global invariant graph, we need to construct appropriate arcs serving as bridges that cross
{\mathcal{A}}
and join components of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{E}}
together.

Step 0. Assign a preperiodic point
x_{\lambda}\in Q
to every
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

Recall that
\Lambda
is the collection of oriented boundary components of all annuli
A\in{\rm Comp}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\Lambda_{*}\subset\Lambda
consists of all elements whose orbits under
f
stay in
\Lambda
; see (
8.1
). We have assigned one point
x_{\lambda}\in\lambda
for each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{*}
such that
f(x_{\lambda})=x_{f(\lambda)}
and
x_{\lambda}\in Q
. Thus, it remains to assign a point to each element of
\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{*}
.

Fix any
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{*}
. It is an oriented boundary component of a unique component
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
.

If
f(\lambda)\subset\partial V
for a component
V
of
{\mathcal{V}}
, then there exists an annular-type component
V_{1}
of
f^{-1}(V)
contained in
A
such that
\lambda
is an oriented boundary component of
V_{1}
. The boundary
\partial V_{1}
has the other annular-type component
\lambda^{\prime}
. By property (b) of
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}
, there exists an open arc
\beta\subset A(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
joining
\lambda
to
\lambda^{\prime}
, such that
f(\beta)\subset{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}
and the endpoints of
\beta
lie in
f^{-1}(Y_{V})
. Define
x_{\lambda}
to be the endpoint of
\beta
in
\lambda
. It follows that
x_{\lambda}
belongs to
f^{-1}(Y_{V})\cap{\mathcal{K}}\subset Q
.

If
f(\lambda)\in\Lambda
and
x_{f(\lambda)}\in f(\lambda)
has been chosen, we assign a point
x_{\lambda}\in\lambda
such that
f(x_{\lambda})=x_{f(\lambda)}
.
Then
x_{\lambda}
belongs to
Q
by the definition of
Q
.

Step 1.
Construct the initial graph
G_{0}
.

For each component
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
, we denote its two oriented boundary components by
\lambda_{\pm,A}
. Let
z_{\pm,A}\subset\lambda_{\pm,A}
be the points assigned to
\lambda_{\pm,A}
, respectively.

If
A
intersects
f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}})
, we call it
intersection-type
; otherwise,
f(A)
is still a component of
{\mathcal{A}}
. In the latter case, there exists a smallest integer
n_{A}\geq 1
such that
f^{n_{A}}(A)
is an intersection-type component of
{\mathcal{A}}
since
f
has no Herman rings.

We claim that there exists an open arc
\gamma_{A}
joining
z_{\pm,A}
in each component
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
such that
f(\gamma_{A})=\gamma_{f(A)}
when
A
is not intersection-type.

First, we choose an open arc
\alpha_{A}
with endpoints
z_{\pm,A}
in each component
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Fix an intersection-type component
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
. For any component
A^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{A}}
with
f^{n(A^{\prime})}(A^{\prime})=A
, the curve
\alpha=f^{n(A^{\prime})}(\alpha_{A^{\prime}})
lies in
A
and joins
z_{\pm,A}
. Consequently,
\alpha
is homotopic to
\alpha_{A}
with endpoints fixed, up to an
N(A^{\prime})
-time twist around
A
. Let
N
be the smallest common multiple of all such numbers
N(A^{\prime})
and set
\gamma_{A}=T^{N}(\alpha_{A})
, where
T(\cdot)
denotes the twist map around
A
. Then
A^{\prime}
contains a unique component
\gamma_{A^{\prime}}
of
f^{-n(A^{\prime})}(\gamma_{A})
with endpoints
z_{\pm,A^{\prime}}
. The claim is proved.

Since the endpoints of each
\gamma_{A}
belong to
Q\subset{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{E}}
, the arc
\gamma_{A}
joins the two components of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{E}}
adjacent to
A
together. Thus, we obtain the initial graph

G_{0}={\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{E}}\cup\bigcup\gamma_{A},

where
A
ranges over all components of
{\mathcal{A}}
. The vertices of
G_{0}
are composed of the points in
Q\cup(P\cap{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathcal{E}}})
and the locally branched points of
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}
. Then each
\gamma_{A}
is an edge of
G_{0}
.

Step 2.
Construct a graph
G_{1}\subset f^{-1}(G_{0})
isotopic to
G_{0}
.

We first construct a curve
\gamma_{A}^{1}
for each component
A
of
{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\gamma_{A}^{1}(0,1)\subset A
,
f(\gamma_{A}^{1})\subset G_{0}
, and
\gamma_{A}^{1}
is homotopic to
\gamma_{A}
(rel
P
) with endpoints fixed.

If
A
is not intersection-type, define
\gamma_{A}^{1}=\gamma_{A}
by the claim in Step 1.

If
A
is intersection-type, let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{s}
, with
s\geq 2,
be the annular-type components of
A\setminus f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}})
arranged from left to right by property (P4). Let
\lambda_{\pm,i}
be the annular-type boundary components of
A_{i}
. Then
\lambda_{+,i}\cup\lambda_{-,i+1}
is contained in an annular-type component
K_{i}
of
f^{-1}({\mathcal{K}})
for each
1\leq i\leq s-1
. By Lemma
4.4
,
\Gamma_{i}:=f^{-1}(G_{f(K_{i})})\cap K_{i}
is a graph serving as a skeleton of
K_{i}
.

If
f(A_{1})
is a component of
{\mathcal{A}}
, let
\alpha_{1}
be the lift of
\gamma_{f(A_{1})}
based at
z_{-,A}
. Otherwise,
f(A_{1})
is a component of
{\mathcal{V}}
. By property (b) of
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}
given in Part I and the choice of
z_{-,A}
in Step
0
, there exists an open arc
\alpha_{1}\subset A_{1}
that joins
z_{-,A}
to
\lambda_{+,1}
and satisfies
f(\alpha_{1})\subset{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}
. Similarly, we can find an open arc
\alpha_{i}\subset A_{i}\cap f^{-1}(G_{0})
for every
i\in\{2,\ldots,s\}
such that
\alpha_{i}
joins
\lambda_{\pm,i}
and one endpoint of
\alpha_{s}
is
z_{+,A}
. Therefore,
the points
z_{\pm,A}
can be connected by an open arc
\beta_{A}
in

\bigcup_{i=1}^{s}\alpha_{i}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{s-1}\Gamma_{i},

and it holds that
\beta_{A}\subset A\cap f^{-1}(G_{0})
.

Note that
\beta_{A}
is homotopic to
\gamma_{A}
with endpoints fixed, up to an
m_{A}
-time twist around
A
. Since
\Gamma_{1}
is a skeleton of
K_{1}
, the graph
\Gamma_{1}
separates
\partial A
. Thus, we can find a curve
\beta\subset\Gamma_{1}
such that

\gamma_{A}^{1}=(\beta_{A}\setminus K_{1})\cup\beta

is a curve homotopic to
\gamma_{A}
rel
P
with endpoints fixed.

Define a graph

G_{1}:={\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{E}}\cup\bigcup\gamma_{A}^{1}\subset f^{-1}(G_{0}),

where
A
ranges over all components of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Although a certain
\gamma_{A}^{1}
may have self-intersections, we also consider it an edge of
G_{1}
.
Thus, each edge of
G_{0}
is homotopic rel
P
to an edge of
G_{1}
with endpoints fixed, and the homotopy is the identity when the edge is in
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}
.

For
n\geq 0
, let
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
be the union of all annular-type components of
f^{-n}(\mathcal{A})
. Consequently, the components of
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
are annuli, and
{\mathcal{A}}_{n+1}\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
. By inductively lifting the homotopy of the edges of
G_{0}
and
G_{1}
, we obtain a graph

G_{n}={\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{E}}\bigcup\,(\cup\gamma_{A}^{n})

for every
n\geq 0
, where
A
runs over all components of
{\mathcal{A}}
, such that
f(G_{n+1})\subset G_{n}
, and the curves
\gamma_{A}^{n+1}
and
\gamma_{A}^{n}
are homotopic rel
P
with endpoints fixed, which differ only within
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
.

Since the degree of
f^{n}
on each component of
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
tends to
\infty
as
n\to\infty
, there exists an integer
N\geq 0
such that the
n
-th lift of each
\gamma^{1}_{A}
is an arc for every
n\geq N
. Therefore, there exists a homeomorphism
h_{0}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
that is isotopic to
id
rel
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}_{N}
such that
h_{0}(G_{N})=G_{N+1}
. For the sake of simplicity, we assume that
N=0
.

Step 3.
Construct an invariant graph
G^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
A.2
, we get a sequence of homeomorphisms
\{h_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
such that
h_{n}
is isotopic to
id
rel
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus f^{-n}({\mathcal{A}})
and
h_{n}\circ f=f\circ h_{n+1}
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Recursively define the graph
G_{n+1}=h_{n}(G_{n})
. It then follows that

(8.2)

h_{n}(x)=x\text{ if $x\in G_{n}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\quad\textup{and}\quad h_{n}(x)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{n}$ if $x\in G_{n}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{n}$.}

Let
\phi_{n}:=h_{n}\circ\cdots\circ h_{0}
for
n\geq 0
. By Lemma
A.3
,
\phi_{n}
uniformly converges to a quotient map
\phi:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Thus,
G_{n+1}=\phi_{n}(G_{0})
converges to a continuum
G^{\prime}:=\phi(G_{0})
in the sense of the Hausdorff metric. Consequently,
f(G^{\prime})\subset G^{\prime}\subset J_{f}
.

In order to prove that
G^{\prime}
is a graph, it suffices to show that
\phi^{-1}(z)\cap G_{0}
is connected for any
z\in G^{\prime}
. In other words, we will verify that, for any two distinct points
x,y\in G_{0}
with
\phi(x)=\phi(y)
, there exists an arc
l_{x,y}\subset G_{0}
joining
x
and
y
such that
\phi(l_{x,y})
is a singleton.

Fix a pair of distinct points
x
and
y
. Denote
x_{n}=\phi_{n-1}(x)
and
y_{n}=\phi_{n-1}(y)
, which lie in
G_{n}
. Since
\phi(x)=\phi(y)
, at least one of
x
and
y
, say
x
, satisfies that
x_{n}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
for all
n
by (
8.2
).

If
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
lie in the closure of the same component of
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
for each
n
, then
\phi([x,y])
is a singleton, where
[x,y]
denotes the arc in
G_{0}\cap\overline{\mathcal{A}}
joining
x
and
y
. Indeed, let
A_{n}
be the component of
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
such that
x_{n},y_{n}\in\overline{A_{n}}
. Then
(x_{n},y_{n})=\phi_{n-1}(x,y)
is the open arc in
G_{n}\cap A_{n}
joining
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
. Since
f^{n}[x_{n},y_{n}]
is an arc contained in
G_{0}\cap\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}
, by Lemma
A.1
, the diameter of
[x_{n},y_{n}]
converges to zero as
n\to\infty
. Thus,
\phi[x,y]
is a singleton.

On the other hand, since
\phi(x)=\phi(y)
, it follows from (
8.2
) that
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
cannot be separated by components of
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
for each
n
. Hence, we are reduced to the case where there exists some
m\geq 0
such that
x_{m}
and
y_{m}
are neither contained in the closure of a component of
{\mathcal{A}}_{m}
nor separated by components of
{\mathcal{A}}_{m}
. Then there exist two possibilities:

Case 1.
x_{m}\in A
and
y_{m}\in E\setminus\lambda
, where
A
is a component of
{\mathcal{A}}_{m}
,
E
is a component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}_{m}
, and
\lambda=E\cap\partial A
is a boundary component of
A
.

In this case, let
z_{\lambda}\in\lambda
be the assigned point to
\lambda
given in Step 0. Then
z_{\lambda}\not=y_{m}
and
y_{m}=\phi(y)
. Since
\phi(x)=\phi(y)
, the point
x_{m+k}
must belong to the unique component of
{\mathcal{A}}_{m+k}
whose boundary contains
\lambda
, for each
k\geq 0
. However, by the previous discussion, we have
\phi(x)=z_{\lambda}
, which contradicts the assumption that
\phi(x)=\phi(y)
.

Case 2.
x_{m}\in A_{1}
and
y_{m}\in A_{2}
, where
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are distinct components of
{\mathcal{A}}_{m}
, such that each
A_{i}
has a boundary component
\lambda_{i}
contained in a component
E
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{m}
.

In this case, let
z_{1}\in\lambda_{1}
and
z_{2}\in\lambda_{2}
be the assigned points to
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
, respectively. Similarly as above, the points
x_{m+k}
and
z_{1}
(resp.,
y_{m+k}
and
z_{2}
) belong to the closure of the same component of
{\mathcal{A}}_{m+k}
for each
k\geq 0
. Therefore,
[x_{m+k},z_{1}]
and
[z_{2},y_{m+k}]
converge to
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, respectively. Since
\phi(x)=\phi(y)
, it follows that
z_{1}=z_{2}
. Thus,
\phi(l_{x,y})
is a singleton with
l_{x,y}=\phi_{m-1}^{-1}([x_{m},z_{1}]\cup[z_{1},y_{m}])
.

Therefore,
G^{\prime}
is an
f
-invariant graph, and by property (P3), its complementary components are all simply connected domains of simple type.

Part III. Completion of the proof of Proposition
1.1
.

To complete the proof, it remains to join the marked points in
{\mathcal{S}}\cap J_{f}
to the graph
G^{\prime}
.

Since each complementary component of
G^{\prime}
contains at most one marked point, it follows that
f^{-n}(G^{\prime})
is connected for all
n>0
. By replacing
G^{\prime}
with
f^{-n}(G^{\prime})
if necessary, we may assume that each point of
P
is either contained in
G^{\prime}
or never iterated into
G^{\prime}
.

Let
K
be the growing continuum generated by
G^{\prime}
. It is clear that
K=J_{f}
. Let
z\in J_{f}
be a point in
P\setminus G^{\prime}
with period
p
. According to Lemma
6.3
(1), there exists an infinitely growing curve
\gamma
in
K
that joins
G^{\prime}
to
z
. Since each complementary component of
G^{\prime}
contains at most one point of
P
, the growing curve
f^{p}(\gamma)
belongs to the same access to
z
as
\gamma
. Therefore, by
Proposition
6.5
, we can assume that
\gamma
is a growing arc in
K
such that
f^{p}(\gamma)\subset\gamma\cup G^{\prime}
. Consequently, the union of
G^{\prime}
and
\bigcup_{i=0}^{p-1}f^{i}(\gamma)
is an
f
-invariant graph and contains the orbit of
z
.

We repeat the process for each cycle in
(P\setminus G^{\prime})\cap J_{f}
and then take an
m
-th iterated pre-image for a sufficiently large integer
m
. The resulting graph
G
is an
f
-invariant skeleton of
J_{f}
rel
P
. This completes the proof of Proposition
1.1
.
∎

Appendix A

A.1.
Orbifold metric and homotopic length

Let
f
be a PCF rational map. Denote by
P_{f}^{\prime}
the post-critical points of
f
in the Fatou set. Then there exists a complete metric
\omega
, called the
orbifold metric
, on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{f}^{\prime}
; see
[
35
, Apendix A.10]
or
[
37
, Section 19]
, as well as
[
3
, Apendix A.10]
.

This metric is induced by a conformal metric
\omega(z)|dz|
with
\omega(z)
smooth in
the complement of
P_{f}
, and has a singularity of the type

\omega=\frac{A(z_{0})|dz|}{|z-z_{0}|^{1-1/n(z_{0})}},\quad n(z_{0})>1,

near each post-critical point
z_{0}\in J_{f}
. Moreover, we have
\|f^{\prime}(z)\|_{\omega}>1
when
z,f(z)\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{f}^{\prime}
; see
[
37
, Theorem 19.6]
for details.

Fix a compact set
\mathcal{O}\supset J_{f}
such that
f^{-1}(\mathcal{O})\subset\mathcal{O}
and
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\mathcal{O}
is a small neighborhood of
P_{f}^{\prime}
. Let
\sigma(z)|dz|
be the standard spherical metric. There exist constants
C>0
and
\rho>1
such that

(A.1)

\|f^{\prime}(z)\|_{\omega}\geq\rho\quad\text{for }z\in f^{-1}(\mathcal{O}),

and

(A.2)

\sigma(z)\leq C\cdot\omega(z)\quad\text{for }z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{f}.

Let
P\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a finite set in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Two curves
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}:[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
are called
homotopic rel
P
with endpoints fixed
if there exists a continuous map
H:[0,1]\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that

•

H(\cdot,0)=\gamma_{0}
and
H(\cdot,1)=\gamma_{1}
;

•

each curve
\gamma_{s}:=H(\cdot,s),s\in[0,1]
has the same endpoints as
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{s}(0,1)\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P
.

Let
\gamma:[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a curve with
\gamma(0,1)\cap P_{f}=\emptyset
. The
homotopic length
of
\gamma
, denoted by
L_{\omega}[\gamma]
, is defined as the infimum of the lengths of curves under the orbifold metric, among all smooth curves that are homotopic to
\gamma
rel
P_{f}
with endpoints fixed.

By (
A.2
), we have

(A.3)

{\rm dist}(\gamma(0),\gamma(1)):={\rm dist}_{\sigma}(\gamma(0),\gamma(1))\leq C\cdot L_{\omega}[\gamma].

For a path-connected set
E\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, its
homotopic diameter

\text{H-diam}_{\omega}(E)
is defined as the supremum of homotopic lengths of all curves in
E
. It follows from (
A.3
) that

(A.4)

{\rm diam}(E):={\rm diam}_{\sigma}(E)\leq C\cdot\text{H-diam}_{\omega}(E).

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\gamma_{n},\gamma\subset\mathcal{O}
be curves such that
\gamma(0,1)\cap P_{f}=\emptyset
and
f^{n}:\gamma_{n}\to\gamma
is a homeomorphism. Then
L_{\omega}[\gamma_{n}]\leq L_{\omega}[\gamma]/\rho^{n}.
Moreover,
suppose that
E
and
E_{n}
are two path-connected sets in
\mathcal{O}
such that
f^{n}:E_{n}\to E
is a homeomorphism and
\text{{\rm H}-{\rm diam}}_{\omega}(E)<\infty
. Then

{\rm diam}(E_{n})\leq C\cdot\text{{\rm H}-{\rm diam}}_{\omega}(E_{n})\leq C\cdot\text{{\rm H}-{\rm diam}}_{\omega}(E)/\rho^{n}.

Proof.

The first conclusion follows from inequality (
A.1
).
Choose any curve
\alpha_{n}\subset E_{n}
. Then
f^{n}:\alpha_{n}\to\alpha:=f^{n}(\alpha_{n})\,(\subset E)
is a homeomorphism. Thus,
L_{\omega}[\alpha_{n}]\leq L_{\omega}[\alpha]/\rho^{n}\leq\text{H-diam}_{\omega}(E)/\rho^{n}
. Since
\alpha_{n}
is arbitrarily chosen, it holds that
\text{H-diam}_{\omega}(E_{n})\leq\text{H-diam}_{\omega}(E)/\rho^{n}
.
∎

A.2.
Lifts of isotopies

Applying the usual homotopy lifting theorem for covering maps (see
[
22
, Proposition 1.30]
), it is not difficult to prove the following result about lifts of isotopies by rational maps. The details of the proof can be found in
[
3
, Proposition 11.3]
.

Lemma A.2
.

Suppose that
f,g:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
are PCF rational maps, and
h_{0},\widetilde{h}_{0}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
are homeomorphisms such that
h_{0}=\widetilde{h}_{0}
on
P_{f}
and
h_{0}\circ f=g\circ\widetilde{h}_{0}
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
. Let
H:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be an isotopy rel
P_{f}
with
H_{0}=h_{0}
. Then
H
can be uniquely lifted to an isotopy
\widetilde{H}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
rel
f^{-1}(P_{f})
such that
\widetilde{H}_{0}=\widetilde{h}_{0}
and
H_{t}\circ f=g\circ\widetilde{H}_{t}
on
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.

Let
(f,P)
be a marked rational map, and let
\mathcal{O}
be the compact set given in Appendix
A.1
. Then
{\mathcal{D}}:=\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus\mathcal{O}
is a small neighborhood of
P_{f}^{\prime}
.

Let
\theta_{0}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
be a homeomorphism isotopic to
id
rel
P\cup{\mathcal{D}}
. By Lemma
A.2
, there exists a homeomorphism
\theta_{1}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
isotopic to
id
rel
P
such that
\theta_{0}\circ f=f\circ\theta_{1}
. Inductively, we have a sequence of homeomorphisms
\{\theta_{n},n\geq 1\}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
isotopic to
id
rel
P
such that
\theta_{n}\circ f=f\circ\theta_{n+1}
. Denote
\phi_{n}=\theta_{n-1}\circ\cdots\circ\theta_{0}
.

A continuous onto map
\pi:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is a
quotient map
if
\pi^{-1}(z)
is either a singleton or a full continuum for any point
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
.

Lemma A.3
.

The sequence
\{\phi_{n}\}
uniformly converges to a quotient map of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

Let
\Theta^{0}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
rel
P
be an isotopy such that
\Theta^{0}_{0}=id
,
\Theta^{0}_{1}=\theta_{0}
, and
\Theta^{0}_{t}(z)=z
for all
z\in P\cup{\mathcal{D}}
and
t\in[0,1]
.
By inductively applying Lemma
A.2
, for each
n\geq 1
, we obtain an isotopy
\Theta^{n}:\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
such that

•

\Theta^{n}_{0}=id
and
\Theta^{n}_{1}=\theta_{n}
;

•

\Theta^{n}_{t}(z)=z
for all
z\in f^{-n}(P\cup{\mathcal{D}})
and
t\in[0,1]
; and

•

\Theta^{n}_{t}\circ f=f\circ\Theta^{n+1}_{t}
for all
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
and
t\in[0,1]
.

For each point
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
, define a curve
\gamma_{z}:[0,1]\to\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
by
\gamma_{z}(t):=\Theta^{0}_{t}(z)
. From the compactness, there exists a constant
L_{0}
such that
L_{\omega}[\gamma_{z}]\leq L_{0}
for all
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus{\mathcal{D}}
. To prove the lemma, it suffices to show that there exist constants
M>0
and
\rho>1
such that for all
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
and
n\geq 1
,

{\rm dist}(\phi_{n}(z),\phi_{n+1}(z))\leq M\rho^{-n}.

Fix any
z\in\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
and
n\geq 1
. Set
w=f^{n}(\phi_{n}(z))
. Let
\beta
be the lift of
\gamma_{w}
based at
\phi_{n}(z)
. The other endpoint of
\beta
is
\phi_{n+1}(z)
. If
w\in P\cup{\mathcal{D}}
, then
\gamma_{w}
is a singleton, and hence
\phi_{n}(z)=\phi_{n+1}(z)
. Otherwise, it follows from Lemma
A.1
and equality (
A.3
) that

{\rm dist}(\phi_{n}(z),\phi_{n+1}(z))\leq CL_{\omega}[\beta]\leq CL_{0}\rho^{-n}.

Thus,
\{\phi_{n}\}
uniformly converges to a continuous map
\phi_{\infty}
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
as
n\to\infty
. Since
\phi_{\infty}
is a uniform limit of homeomorphisms, it is a quotient map; see e.g.
[
8
, Lemma 3.1]
.
∎

A.3.
Local connectivity

It is known that a continuum
E\subset\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}
is locally connected if and only if the boundary of each component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
is locally connected and the spherical diameters of components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
converge to zero; see e.g.
[
37
, Lemma 19.5]
. We will show that

Lemma A.4
.

Let
f
be a PCF rational map, and let
E
be a continuum with
\partial E\subset J_{f}
. Then
E
is locally connected if and only if the boundary of each component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
is locally connected and the homotopic diameters of components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
disjoint from
P_{f}
converge to zero.

Proof.

First, suppose that
E
is locally connected. Since the homotopic lengths of curves in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus P_{f}
vary continuously, each component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
disjoint from
P_{f}
has a finite homotopic diameter. To the contrary, assume that
\{D_{n}\}
is a sequence of components of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
disjoint from
P_{f}
, such that
\text{H-diam}_{\omega}(D_{n})\geq\epsilon_{0}>0
. Since
{\rm diam}(D_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, by taking a subsequence, we may assume that
\{\overline{D_{n}}\}
converges to a point
a\in E
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, let
\Delta(\epsilon)
be the round disk with center
a
and orbifold radius
\epsilon
. Then
\Delta(\epsilon)
contains at most one point of
P_{f}
when
\epsilon
is sufficiently small. On the other hand, for sufficiently large
n
,
D_{n}\subset\Delta(\epsilon_{0}/3)
. This implies that
\text{H-diam}_{\omega}(D_{n})\leq 2\epsilon_{0}/3
, a contradiction.

The converse part of the lemma follows directly from (
A.4
).
∎

The following result is well known; see e.g.
[
37
, Lemmas 17.17 and 17.18]
.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
X
be a connected and compact metric space. If
X
is locally connected, then it is arcwise connected and locally arcwise connected.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
E\subset\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}
be a locally connected continuum. Then there exists a family of curves in
E
that are equicontinuous such that any two points of
E
are joined by a curve in this family.

Proof.

For any component
U
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
, we fix a Riemann mapping
\phi_{U}:U\to\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
. Since
\partial U
is locally connected,
\phi_{U}^{-1}
has a continuous extension from
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}}
to
\overline{U}
. For any
crosscut

\alpha
of
U
, let
D(\alpha)
denote the component of
U\setminus\alpha
with a smaller diameter. Here, a crosscut of
U
means an arc with its interior in
U
and its endpoints on
\partial U
. By the local connectivity of
E
, for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\rho_{\epsilon}>0
such that for each component
U
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
,

(1)

if the distance between
a,b\in\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
is bounded above by
\rho_{\epsilon}
, then
|\phi_{U}^{-1}(a)-\phi_{U}^{-1}(b)|<\epsilon
;

(2)

if the diameter of a crosscut
\alpha
of
U
is bounded above by
\rho_{\epsilon}
, then
\textup{diam}(D(\alpha))<\epsilon
.

Let
\Gamma
be the collection of all line segments with endpoints in
E
. We will revise each
\gamma\in\Gamma
to an arc
\tilde{\gamma}\subset E
such that
\{\tilde{\gamma}:\gamma\in\Gamma\}
is equicontinuous.

Fix
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Denote
X_{\gamma}:=\{t\in[0,1]:\gamma(t)\in E\}
. Then for any component
I
of
[0,1]\setminus X_{\gamma}
, the open segment
\alpha=\gamma(I)
is a crosscut for some component
U
of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
. Let
\tilde{\alpha}=\partial\phi_{U}(D(\alpha))\cap\partial\mbox{$\mathbb{D}$}
. Then there exists a linear map
h_{I}:\overline{\alpha}\to\tilde{\alpha}
.

Now, define a map
\tilde{\gamma}:[0,1]\to E
as

\tilde{\gamma}(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\gamma(t)&\hbox{if $t\in X_{\gamma}$,}\\[5.0pt]
\phi_{U}^{-1}\circ h_{I}\circ\gamma(t)&\hbox{if $t\in I$ and $\gamma(I)\subset U$,}\end{array}\right.

where
I
is the component of
[0,1]\setminus X_{\gamma}
containing
t
.

We claim that
\tilde{\gamma}
is a curve. To see this, let
\{I_{n}\}
be a sequence of components of
[0,1]\setminus X_{\gamma}
converging to a point
t_{*}
. Let
U_{n}
be the component of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathbb{C}$}}\setminus E
such that
\alpha_{n}:=\gamma(I_{n})
is a crosscut of
U_{n}
. Then
\textup{diam}(\alpha_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
by the continuity of
\gamma
.

It follows from point (2) above that
{\rm diam}(D(\alpha_{n}))\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Since
\tilde{\gamma}(I_{n})=\partial D(\alpha_{n})\cap\partial U
, it follows that
\tilde{\gamma}(I_{n})\to\tilde{\gamma}(t_{*})
as
n\to\infty
. Thus,
\tilde{\gamma}
is continuous, and the claim is proved.

We will prove that the family of curves
\{\tilde{\gamma},\gamma\in\Gamma\}
is equicontinuous. Given any
\epsilon>0
, since the family
\Gamma
is equicontinuous, there exists a number
\delta>0
such that
|\gamma(t_{1})-\gamma(t_{2})|<\textup{min}\{{\rho^{2}_{\epsilon}}/({2\pi}),\epsilon\}
whenever
|t_{1}-t_{2}|<\delta
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Fix any
\gamma\in\Gamma
. If
t_{1},t_{2}\in X_{\gamma}
, then
|\tilde{\gamma}(t_{1})-\tilde{\gamma}(t_{2})|=|\gamma(t_{1})-\gamma(t_{2})|<\epsilon
whenever
|t_{1}-t_{2}|<\delta
.

We now assume that
t_{1},t_{2}\in\overline{I}
for a component
I
of
[0,1]\setminus X_{\gamma}
. Let
\alpha=\gamma(I)
. If
\textup{diam}(\alpha)<\rho_{\epsilon}
, point (2) above implies
|\tilde{\gamma}(t_{1})-\tilde{\gamma}(t_{2})|\leq\textup{diam}(D(\alpha))<\epsilon
. Otherwise, we have
|h_{I}^{\prime}|<2\pi/\rho_{\epsilon}
. In this case, if
|t_{1}-t_{2}|<\delta
, it holds that

|h_{I}\circ\gamma(t_{1})-h_{I}\circ\gamma(t_{2})|=|\gamma(t_{1})-\gamma(t_{2})|\cdot|h_{I}^{\prime}|<\rho_{\epsilon}.

It then follows from point (1) above that
|\tilde{\gamma}(t_{1})-\tilde{\gamma}(t_{2})|<\epsilon
.

Finally, assume that
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
lie in the closures of distinct components
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
of
[0,1]\setminus X_{\gamma}
, respectively. If
|t_{1}-t_{2}|<\delta
, the two endpoints
t^{\prime}_{1}
and
t_{2}^{\prime}
of
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
between
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
satisfy that
|t_{1}-t^{\prime}_{1}|<\delta
and
|t_{2}-t^{\prime}_{2}|<\delta
. Then according to the previous two cases,

|\tilde{\gamma}(t_{1})-\tilde{\gamma}(t_{2})|\leq|\tilde{\gamma}(t_{1})-\tilde{\gamma}(t^{\prime}_{1})|+|\tilde{\gamma}(t^{\prime}_{1})-\tilde{\gamma}(t^{\prime}_{2})|+|\tilde{\gamma}(t^{\prime}_{2})-\tilde{\gamma}(t_{2})|<3\epsilon.

Therefore, the family
\{\tilde{\gamma},\gamma\in\Gamma\}
is equicontinuous.
∎
